**Summary.** This work tackles the difficult problem of characterizing non-equilibrium steady states in quantum matter by deriving an emergent Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory (MFT). Using a boundary-driven XXZ spin chain model, the authors show that the system's large-scale dynamics are governed by nonlinear diffusion equations featuring density-dependent transport coefficients. This establishes MFT as a powerful, universal framework for interacting quantum diffusive systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, microscopic derivation of nonlinear, interacting hydrodynamics, extending the applicability of MFT beyond simple constant-diffusivity models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is deriving a universal hydrodynamic description for non-equilibrium steady states (NESS) in interacting quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** The authors derive an emergent classical Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory (MFT) for the system, showing that the hydrodynamics features density-dependent diffusivity and mobility.

**Method.** The derivation proceeds by taking a continuum limit of the microscopic Lindblad dynamics, leading to nonlinear diffusion equations for the density field.

**Model / system.** The system is a boundary-driven XXZ spin chain with bulk dephasing, whose dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation. The effective low-energy description resembles an Interacting Symmetric Simple Exclusion Process (ISEP).

**Key observables.** Stationary density profile, long-range correlations, and the full counting statistics of the current.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Density-dependent diffusivity $D(ar{n})$ and mobility $\sigma(ar{n})$, and the scaling limit $	au = s/N^2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on a Renormalization Group (RG) argument suggesting a diffusive strong-coupling fixed point, and the slow-mode manifold approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare tensor-network results for the microscopic model and the ISEP description, showing local diffusivity vs. magnetization and correlation functions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the microscopic dynamics via Lindblad equations, then uses RG arguments to derive the effective MFT equations, and finally applies this framework to compute observables like correlation functions and full counting statistics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A universal characterization of non-equilibrium steady states in interacting quantum many-body systems remains one of the central challenges of statistical physics. Here, we address this problem for a paradigmatic model of diffusive interacting quantum matter---the boundary-driven XXZ spin chain with bulk dephasing---and derive, directly from its microscopic Lindblad dynamics, an emergent classical Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory (MFT) governing its large-scale fluctuations. Crucially, the resulting hydrodynamics carries a density-dependent diffusivity and mobility as the fingerprint of interactions. This effective description enables the exact computation of the stationary density profile, long-range correlations, and the full counting statistics of the current, in excellent agreement with tensor-network simulations. Our work demonstrates that noisy quantum many-body systems can realize the universality class of genuinely interacting diffusive matter, beyond the constant-diffusivity class of the symmetric simple exclusion process, and establishes MFT as a powerful universal framework for interacting diffusive quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes that prethermalization is an observable- and state-selective phenomenon, meaning not all physical quantities will exhibit a long-lived plateau even if the Hamiltonian suggests it. Crucially, the authors derive a general lower bound on this prethermal lifetime using the Loschmidt echo, providing a powerful diagnostic tool for characterizing non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, quantitative tools (like the Loschmidt echo bound) to predict the duration of non-equilibrium dynamics, which is crucial for designing experiments that probe intermediate quantum phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the conditions under which prethermalization occurs in quantum many-body systems, specifically addressing whether separated energy scales guarantee a prethermal plateau for all observables.

**Main result.** Prethermalization is highly selective; its existence depends on the specific observable and the initial state, not just the Hamiltonian structure. Furthermore, the Loschmidt echo provides a general lower bound on the prethermal lifetime for any bounded observable.

**Method.** The authors use theoretical analysis involving Hamiltonian perturbation theory, analyzing matrix elements in the energy eigenbasis, and deriving bounds using the trace-norm inequality applied to the Loschmidt echo and Uhlmann fidelity.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to long-range interacting spin models, exemplified by a 1D transverse field Ising model with power-law interactions ($\hat{H}(\alpha)$). The dynamics are studied in the presence of a small perturbation ($\hat{H} = \hat{H}_0 + \epsilon \hat{V}$).

**Key observables.** Prethermal plateau visibility, entanglement entropy ($S_{	ext{Ent}}$), total magnetization ($M_z$), first moment of excitation density ($C_z$), Loschmidt echo ($L(t)$), and Uhlmann fidelity ($F(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction-range exponent ($\alpha$), perturbation strength ($\epsilon$), and time scales relative to $\epsilon^{-1}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the perturbation strength $\epsilon$ being small and the time $t$ being short relative to the inverse perturbation strength ($t \ll \epsilon^{-1}$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of time evolution for different observables ($S_{	ext{Ent}}$ vs. $M_z$) showing selectivity, and demonstrate the mathematical bound relating the observable deviation to the Loschmidt echo departure.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework of prethermalization in weakly perturbed systems. It then analyzes the role of symmetry (permutation invariance) in suppressing plateaus for certain observables. Finally, it derives and applies the rigorous bound using the Loschmidt echo and fidelity to quantify the minimum prethermal lifetime.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Prethermalization describes long-lived intermediate regimes that precede equilibrium and can dominate experimentally accessible dynamics. Here, we show that a separation of spectral energy scales, despite giving rise to a hierarchy of dynamical timescales, does not by itself guarantee the appearance of a prethermal plateau. Under the same Hamiltonian, some observables may exhibit prethermal behavior, while others relax directly toward equilibrium. We uncover the mechanism governing this selectivity, showing that the emergence of a prethermal plateau depends not only on the Hamiltonian, but also on the observable and the initial state. Our results apply broadly to systems close to a fully permutation-symmetric limit and are illustrated with a long-range interacting spin model. We further prove that the Loschmidt echo provides a lower bound on the prethermal lifetime of any bounded observable whenever the prethermal and exact dynamics are unitary.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive dressed-state framework to model light scattering from ensembles of atoms. It calculates fluorescence, absorption, and correlation functions by treating the full vectorial coupling to the electromagnetic field. The results highlight universal collective behaviors and predict novel quantum optical phenomena arising from atomic interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general, and computationally implemented tool for analyzing collective quantum optical responses in structured arrays of atoms, bridging quantum electrodynamics with many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a general dressed-state framework for computing light scattering spectra and photon correlations from ensembles of two-level atoms driven by intense coherent fields.

**Main result.** The framework reveals universal master curves controlled by collective saturation parameters, and identifies new collective effects like incoherent Raman channels and slow decay timescales when ground-state degeneracy is present.

**Method.** The authors use a full vectorial, dressed-state approach solving the Lindbladian to obtain optical observables as explicit sums of Lorentzian lines.

**Model / system.** The system consists of an ensemble of N_at two-level atoms with arbitrary J_g -> J_e transitions, interacting with the quantized electromagnetic field in free space under intense coherent driving fields.

**Key observables.** Fluorescence spectra, probe absorption spectra, and photon-photon correlations (g^(2)(τ)).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Intense coherent drive strength, interatomic distance, and the collective saturation parameter.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the full vectorial treatment, noting that the scalar approximation fails qualitatively in certain geometries.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 sketches the setup showing N_at atoms in free space driven by a coherent laser field, with scattered light measured at a detector.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the general dressed-state framework using a multilevel coupled-dipole master equation, applies it to calculate various spectra using the Quantum Regression Theorem, and extends the analysis to include ground-state degeneracy effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a general dressed-state framework for computing fluorescence spectra, probe absorption spectra, and photon-photon correlations of light scattered by ensembles of $N_\mathrm{at}$ two-level atoms with arbitrary $J_g \to J_e$ transitions driven by intense coherent fields. The approach employs a full vectorial treatment of the electromagnetic field, handles any atomic geometries, illumination directions, and polarizations, and yields optical observables as explicit sums of Lorentzian lines whose positions, widths, and weights are directly tied to the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the Lindbladian. The framework is implemented in an open-source Python package and benchmarked against exact single- and two-atom calculations. We identify geometries in which the full vectorial description is essential, and the scalar approximation fails qualitatively. Applying the method to pairs of atoms with a $J_g=0\to J_e=1$ transition, we show that elastic and inelastic scattered intensities collapse onto universal master curves controlled by a single collective saturation parameter built from the dominant superradiant mode, across several orders of magnitude in drive strength and interatomic distance. We identify collective phenomena that require a description beyond this single-mode picture. Extending the analysis to atoms with ground-state degeneracy, we find that most collective features carry over, while two qualitatively new effects emerge: an incoherent spontaneous Raman channel that modifies the scaling of inelastic emission, and a slow timescale in the time-delayed correlations $g^{(2)}(τ)$ governed by the competition between Raman scattering and subradiant decay, controlled by a single dimensionless parameter. These results provide both physical insight and practical computational tools for engineering collective optical responses in few-atom systems such as optical tweezer arrays.

</details>

**Authors:** Jaewon Kim, Hyun Gyu Song, Daegwang Choi, Yong-Hoon Cho

**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `analog quantum simulation` **4/5** · 🔥 `driven-dissipative phase transition` **4/5** · `quantum optics experiment` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `QC/QI experiment` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `interference shaping light` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates the active, all-optical switching of topological charges in polariton condensates confined within a photonic crystal. By tuning the excitation power, the system forces the condensate to switch between discrete, quantized topological states. This work establishes a novel platform for controlling topological properties using nonlinear optical interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, controllable platform for manipulating topological invariants using nonlinear optical effects (polariton nonlinearity), which is highly relevant for quantum information processing and open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge addressed is achieving active, all-optical reconfiguration (switching) of topological charges in photonic systems that are inherently topologically protected.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated topological charge switching in trapped polariton condensates by tuning the excitation power, causing the condensate to switch between distinct, quantized topological charge states.

**Method.** The work combines experimental characterization using angle-resolved PL and reflectance spectroscopy with theoretical modeling using a 1D non-Hermitian Dirac Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes trapped exciton-polariton condensates within a photonic crystal well structure. The physics is modeled by a 1D non-Hermitian Dirac Hamiltonian, which describes the coupled dynamics of the polaritons.

**Key observables.** Topological charges ($q = +1$ to $+4$), quantized energy levels ($n=1, 2, 3, 4$), spectral blueshift, and single-mode occupancy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Excitation power ($P$) relative to the condensation threshold ($P_{th}$), well lattice constant, and barrier bandgap energy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The switching mechanism is attributed to the polariton blueshift exceeding the barrier bandgap energy, and the system is modeled by the non-Hermitian Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the switching process, experimental confirmation of quantized states via PL spectra vs. well width, and the power-dependent transition showing the shift from one topological state to the next.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform (photonic crystal wells), confirms the existence of quantized trapped states, and then demonstrates the switching mechanism by showing how increasing excitation power drives the condensate into a higher-energy, different topological state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological charges in photonic systems provide a robust degree of freedom with direct applications in optical information processing. Bound states in the continuum (BICs) in photonic crystal slabs inherently carry such quantized topological charges, yet they are topologically protected, making active reconfiguration fundamentally challenging. Here, we demonstrate topological charge switching in trapped BIC polariton condensates in a photonic crystal, where spatial modulation of the photonic crystals supports multiple confined states, each carrying a distinct charge. Trapped condensates within the Dirac bandgap are expelled into the leaky band via polariton blueshift, enabling single-mode selection by solely tuning the excitation power. This work offers a platform for optically controlling topological charges by harnessing polariton nonlinearity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a unified theory connecting dynamical phase transitions in quantum systems (unitary evolution) and classical systems (dissipative relaxation). By analyzing the complex-time behavior of a dynamical generating function, the authors show that the semiclassical limit bridges the gap, allowing single-particle quantum systems to exhibit transitions analogous to those found in classical statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a powerful, unified mathematical framework to study non-equilibrium dynamics and phase transitions in both quantum and classical regimes, extending concepts from many-body physics to single-particle systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper unifies the description of dynamical phase transitions (DPTs) occurring in both the unitary evolution of an isolated quantum particle and the dissipative relaxation of a classical Brownian particle.

**Main result.** It establishes a direct, unified connection between quantum and classical finite-time DPTs, showing that the semiclassical limit acts analogously to the thermodynamic limit.

**Method.** The analysis employs a dynamical generating function, utilizing saddle-point approximations of path integrals and analyzing the condensation of Fisher zeros in the complex-time plane.

**Model / system.** The models include a quantum particle undergoing quench dynamics and a classical Brownian particle relaxing in a potential, both analyzed via their respective propagators or Fokker-Planck equations.

**Key observables.** Survival Amplitude (SA)/Survival Rate Function (SRF), and a unifying, trajectory-based order parameter realized as a classical correlation function and a sequential quantum weak value.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Semiclassical limit ($\hbar 	o 0$) and weak-noise limit ($\eta 	o 0$); the competition between different saddle trajectories.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on the saddle-point approximation for the path integral and the identification of Fisher zeros in the complex time plane.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate saddle trajectories determining the propagator, and show the SRF for both quantum and classical problems, revealing kinks at critical times.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the unifying framework by first analyzing the quantum and classical dynamics separately using the generating function, then demonstrating the correspondence through the complex-time structure of the Fisher zeros, and finally defining the shared order parameter.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a unifying description of dynamical phase transitions in the unitary evolution of an isolated quantum particle and the dissipative relaxation of a classical Brownian particle, based on a dynamical generat- ing function. In the semiclassical and weak-noise limits, Fisher zeros of this function condense in the complex- time plane and reach the corresponding physical axes, producing dynamical phase transitions through a competition between return trajectories. This establishes a direct connection between quantum and classical (finite-time) dynamical phase transitions, where the semiclassical limit plays the role of the thermodynamic limit. In particular, the established link naturally provides a classical version of the Loschmidt amplitude and shows how dynamical quantum phase transitions, typically associated with isolated many-body systems, can arise in a single-particle quantum system. The dynamical phases are distinguished by a unifying, trajectory- based order parameter, realized as a classical correlation function and a sequential quantum weak value.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes defect production in imbalanced-pairing Kitaev models driven across quantum critical and exceptional points. By comparing two mathematical frameworks, the authors show how defect scaling laws are modified when time-reversal symmetry is broken, leading to phenomena like defect freezing. This provides critical insights into non-equilibrium physics in non-Hermitian quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends critical dynamics concepts (KZM) into the non-Hermitian domain, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems, driven dynamics, and topological phases relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the non-equilibrium dynamics and defect production in imbalanced-pairing Kitaev models as the system is driven across critical and exceptional points.

**Main result.** The defect density exhibits conventional or extended Kibble-Zurek scaling in certain regimes, but traversing the time-reversal-symmetry-broken region leads to defect freezing, while excess defects still follow KZM scaling.

**Method.** The analysis employs two distinct theoretical frameworks—the wave-function normalization approach and the biorthogonal framework—to study the time evolution governed by a non-Hermitian Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is the one- and two-dimensional imbalanced-pairing Kitaev model, described by a time-dependent, non-Hermitian Hamiltonian $H(t)$ controlled by a parameter $h(t)$.

**Key observables.** Defect density ($n^d$), quasiparticle spectrum (real vs. complex eigenvalues), and the scaling exponents ($eta$) of the defect production.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The pairing imbalance parameter (positive vs. negative), the nature of the quench (ending at a critical point vs. an exceptional point), and dimensionality (1D vs. 2D).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes initial preparation in the instantaneous right ground state, and defect freezing itself indicates a breakdown of adiabaticity.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured by analyzing the dynamics using two frameworks (wave-function normalization and biorthogonal), detailing the model and phase diagram, deriving the quench dynamics, and finally investigating the specific defect production mechanisms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate driven dynamics across critical and exceptional points in the one- and two-dimensional imbalanced-pairing Kitaev models using both the wave-function normalization approach and the biorthogonal framework. For a positive pairing imbalance parameter, the quasiparticle spectrum remains real, and a pairing imbalance neither shifts the equilibrium phase boundaries nor generates imaginary eigenenergies. In this regime, the defect density follows the conventional Kibble--Zurek scaling in one dimension and the extended Kibble--Zurek scaling, arising from a gapless manifold, in two dimensions within both frameworks. The corresponding scaling exponents are therefore governed by those of the Hermitian transition. For a negative pairing imbalance parameter, time-reversal symmetry is broken, the quasiparticle spectrum develops complex eigenvalues, and the gap closes at exceptional points. For ramps ending at an exceptional point, the defect density follows the modified Kibble--Zurek scaling in the wave-function normalization approach, whereas it obeys the conventional Kibble--Zurek scaling in the biorthogonal framework. When the ramp traverses the time-reversal-symmetry-broken region, a finite density of defects remains even in the adiabatic limit, leading to defect freezing in both frameworks. Although this frozen background indicates a breakdown of adiabaticity, the excess defects generated on top of this background continue to obey the conventional Kibble--Zurek scaling in one dimension and the extended Kibble--Zurek scaling in two dimensions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework showing that continuous quantum measurement and feedback can boost quantum sensing and clock accuracy in open systems. It derives a modified thermodynamic uncertainty relation proving that the achievable precision is limited not just by thermal dissipation, but also by the information cost associated with the feedback control itself.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative link between measurement back-action, non-equilibrium thermodynamics, and fundamental limits on precision, extending the standard fluctuation theorems to include active control mechanisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how continuous quantum measurement and feedback control can enhance quantum metrology and clock precision in open quantum systems, particularly concerning the limits imposed by thermodynamics.

**Main result.** Feedback enhances parameter estimation and clock precision beyond bounds set by reservoir entropy production alone, necessitating the inclusion of an information entropy cost from the feedback apparatus in the modified thermodynamic uncertainty relation.

**Method.** The analysis employs full counting statistics derived from the tilted generator of the feedback-modified master equation, relating precision to the Fisher information and thermodynamic bounds to effective entropy production.

**Model / system.** The framework is applied to open quantum systems, exemplified by a charge-monitored double quantum dot (DQD) coupled to thermal reservoirs, and generalized to a driven three-level system.

**Key observables.** Fisher information ($F_{fb}$), clock precision ($P_{clk}$), reservoir entropy production ($\dot{\Sigma}_{th}$), and information entropy production ($\dot{\Sigma}_{info}^{fb}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Feedback unitary angle ($\phi$), temperature ($T$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and detuning ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes unital jump feedback maps and relies on deriving the effective thermodynamic uncertainty relation by incorporating the information cost of the feedback process.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the enhancement of Fisher information and clock precision using feedback compared to no-feedback cases, and critically show that the reservoir-only TUR ratio falls below unity while the feedback-modified ratio remains above unity.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using full counting statistics and the tilted generator for feedback-modified dynamics. It then applies this to parameter estimation (DQD) and clock stabilization, culminating in the derivation and validation of the feedback-modified Thermodynamic Uncertainty Relation using both electronic transport and general driven systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Feedback can convert continuously monitored quantum jumps into a thermodynamic resource. We formulate full counting statistics for open quantum systems under unital jump feedback by incorporating the feedback maps into the tilted generator. The resulting trajectory ensemble determines both current fluctuations and the Fisher information of the measurement record. We show that feedback can enhance reservoir-parameter estimation and clock precision without necessarily changing average thermodynamic currents. This enhanced precision is not bounded by reservoir entropy production alone. By embedding the reduced dynamics in an enlarged measurement-feedback process, we derive a feedback-modified thermodynamic uncertainty relation in which the information entropy production of the feedback apparatus supplies the missing cost. A charge-monitored double quantum dot illustrates the framework: jump-conditioned feedback improves thermometry and chemical-potential sensing, and stabilizes a quantum clock defined by output-current ticks.

</details>

### [Nonlocal wavefront shaping through complex media](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01017v1)

**Authors:** Yanis Trouyet, Neelan Gounden, Pedro Ornelas, Patrick Cameron, Andrew Forbes, Hugo Defienne  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01017v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates nonlocal wavefront shaping by utilizing spatially entangled photon pairs to correct for scattering distortions in complex optical media. By applying phase corrections to one photon that avoids the scatterer, the system restores the spatial correlations of its entangled partner. This technique offers a pathway for compact imaging through otherwise inaccessible, highly scattering environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, as it uses quantum entanglement to engineer wave propagation through highly lossy and complex media, effectively bypassing the need for in-situ active elements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of mitigating scattering in complex media for advanced imaging by overcoming the limitation of existing local wavefront correction techniques.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated nonlocal wavefront shaping using spatially entangled photon pairs, restoring spatial correlations in all tested scattering regimes.

**Method.** Nonlocal wavefront shaping is achieved by applying a phase correction to one photon (idler) that bypasses the scattering medium to compensate for distortions experienced by its entangled partner (signal) passing through the medium.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses spatially entangled photon pairs generated via SPDC and tests the technique across three regimes: low-order aberrations (PDMS), single-scattering (Parafilm), and multiple-scattering (simulations).

**Key observables.** Spatial Intensity Correlation Matrix G^(2)(r_i, r_s) and the central correlation value C_0, used to quantify the restoration of spatial correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Scattering mean free path (l_s), number of active SLM macropixels (N), and the measured enhancement ratio (e.g., $\eta \approx 2.4$ or $\eta = 49.5$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the entanglement between photon pairs and the ability to physically decouple the wavefront correction from the scattering medium.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, showing restored $G^{(2)}$ projections under optimized vs. unoptimized conditions for aberrations and scattering media.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the problem of local correction, then detailing the nonlocal approach using entangled pairs, and finally presenting experimental results across low-order aberration, single-scattering, and multiple-scattering regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Wavefront shaping is a key technique for mitigating scattering in complex media, enabling advanced imaging and optical communication. Yet existing approaches are inherently local, requiring active correction elements - such as spatial light modulators or deformable mirrors - to lie directly in the optical path of the scattered light, which limits their integration into compact imaging systems. Here, we experimentally demonstrate nonlocal wavefront shaping using spatially entangled photon pairs. By applying a phase correction to a photon that never interacts with the scattering medium, we compensate for the distortions experienced by its entangled partner and restore their initial spatial correlations. Our approach physically decouples the wavefront correction from the scattering medium, paving the way for imaging through complex media in compact and otherwise inaccessible systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a method to simulate compact U(1) gauge theories on bosonic quantum hardware by employing a GKP stabilizer to enforce necessary compactness. The authors derive error-mitigation protocols and successfully apply the framework to calculate the energy splitting related to monopole physics in QED3. It represents a significant step in realizing lattice gauge theory simulations on continuous-variable quantum computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the hardware-software interface for simulating fundamental condensed matter models, providing concrete, error-mitigated protocols for mapping topological physics onto continuous-variable quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental challenge of encoding compact lattice gauge theories, which use angular variables, onto continuous, unbounded bosonic quantum hardware.

**Main result.** They successfully construct an encoding using GKP stabilizers that maps the compact theory to bosonic modes, and they demonstrate error mitigation techniques allowing recovery of key physical observables.

**Method.** The methodology involves developing a one-to-one encoding, applying GKP stabilizers to enforce compactness, and using quantum simulation techniques like Trotterization and spectral analysis to extract physical information.

**Model / system.** The system is compact U(1) gauge fields, specifically applied to compact QED3 on a lattice. The dynamics are governed by a Kogut–Susskind Hamiltonian, analyzed after solving Gauss's law.

**Key observables.** Twist Energy ($\Delta_{tw}$), energy splitting between charge sectors, and various correlators used for spectral function reconstruction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameter (finite vs. infinite limit), lattice size ($N$), and the error parameter ($\Gamma$) used for extrapolation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that imperfections manifest as computable shifts rather than uncontrolled leakage, and that extrapolation techniques can reliably remove finite-squeezing biases.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lattice structure, the path for Gauss's law closure, and the general setup for the quantum simulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the encoding (mapping compact variables to bosonic modes via GKP stabilizers) to analyzing the resulting Hamiltonian dynamics in different computational frames (Loop vs. Link basis), and finally detailing advanced measurement protocols for error-corrected spectral measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Compact lattice gauge theories are formulated in terms of angular variables and integer electric fluxes, while bosonic quantum hardware provides oscillator modes with continuous, unbounded quadratures. We bridge this gap with a one-to-one encoding. After Gauss's law is solved, each remaining gauge degree of freedom is carried by a single oscillator mode, with its interactions built from trigonometric gates, and a Gottesman--Kitaev--Preskill (GKP)-type stabilizer provides the compactness that the hardware does not. The encoding becomes exact in the limit of infinite squeezing, and at finite squeezing, the leading imperfections act as small, computable shifts of physical observables rather than uncontrolled leakage. We apply the construction to compact QED$_3$ and derive the error budget at finite squeezing, characterizing the leading errors in closed form, and showing that they can be corrected, subtracted, or extrapolated away. We construct syndrome-extraction protocols that detect and remove the displacement component of photon loss, delimit the noise it does not reach, compare two choices of dynamical variables, and collect the scaling of mode count, gate count, and measurement cost. A one-plaquette example reproduces the exact compact-rotor dynamics, and real-time spectroscopy with controlled extrapolations recovers the exponentially small energy splitting between charge sectors, the seed of the monopole physics of the theory, at the percent level against its exact value.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, hardware-agnostic framework to solve Stochastic Unit Commitment by translating it into a Maximum-Weight Independent Set (MWIS) problem. The authors validate this workflow end-to-end on a neutral-atom quantum processor, showing that the quantum-enhanced approach yields results matching or exceeding classical benchmarks. It highlights the current physical limitations of scaling on these quantum hardware platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, industrial-scale demonstration of mapping a complex, real-world optimization problem onto a specific quantum hardware modality (neutral atoms), directly addressing the gap between theoretical quantum algorithms and practical application.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of integrating structured combinatorial optimization problems, like Stochastic Unit Commitment (UC), onto near-term quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The proposed end-to-end workflow successfully demonstrates that hardware-generated solutions match or exceed exact classical solutions on a 15-day campaign, establishing a viable computational pathway.

**Method.** It compiles the discrete decision layer of UC into a move-based Maximum-Weight Independent Set (MWIS) problem, which is then mapped onto a neutral-atom quantum processor using the Rydberg blockade mechanism.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is the QuEra Aquila neutral-atom quantum processor. The problem is modeled as finding an independent set in a weighted conflict graph derived from scheduling moves in a green hydrogen electrolyser park.

**Key observables.** Hardware-generated dispatch margins, AHS-post ratio (quality of quantum result vs. exact MWIS), and the valid-shot rate for scaling analysis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The primary bottleneck for scaling is identified as the probability that the full atom array survives, rather than the graph embedding itself.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the MWIS representation is backend-agnostic, and that classical post-processing (LP repair, SA) can compensate for hardware imperfections.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the six defined move types and summarize the trend showing the collapse of the valid-shot rate as the problem size increases, while other metrics remain stable.

**Paper structure.** The paper details a hybrid pipeline: classical move generation $\rightarrow$ graph encoding/folding $\rightarrow$ quantum solving (AHS) $\rightarrow$ classical post-processing (SA/LP repair) $\rightarrow$ final validation against the original stochastic LP.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum hardware is beginning to address structured combinatorial optimisation, but two steps still block practical use: mapping real operational models onto hardware-compatible instances, and converting noisy hardware output back into feasible decisions. Here we introduce a backend-agnostic computational interface that compiles the discrete decision layer of stochastic unit commitment into a move-based maximum-weight independent set (MWIS) problem, while retaining continuous dispatch and feasibility recovery in the classical computational layer. We validate the approach in a green hydrogen scheduling setting and deploy it on the QuEra Aquila neutral-atom quantum processor. This is the first end-to-end industrial scheduling workflow that connects real operational decisions to programmable neutral-atom hardware through a solver-agnostic MWIS representation. Across a 15-day hardware campaign on 50-node instances, hardware-generated solutions after classical refinement match or exceed the dispatch margins obtained from exact MWIS on every day. When scaling to 144 nodes, encoding quality remains stable, while the probability that the full atom array survives, rather than graph embedding, emerges as the dominant bottleneck to further scaling. Together, these results establish a hardware-compatible computational pathway toward larger problem scales, and lay the groundwork for exploring regimes in which exact classical optimisation may no longer scale efficiently.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a variational framework to learn quantum noise models directly from syndrome and logical observable data collected during quantum error correction experiments. By minimizing the binary cross-entropy loss, the method achieves an information-theoretically optimal noise estimate. This approach is highly valuable because it allows for continuous, real-time tracking of device noise drifts without requiring dedicated offline characterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, data-driven methodology to characterize complex noise processes in quantum hardware directly from operational measurements, bypassing the need for idealized, separate characterization protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is accurately characterizing quantum noise models for high-performance quantum error correction (QEC) without relying on dedicated, separate characterization experiments.

**Main result.** The proposed variational framework successfully recovers noise models whose logical error rates match independently characterized results, and it can track device drifts in real time.

**Method.** The noise model is learned by formulating the problem as a variational optimization, minimizing the binary cross-entropy between the decoder's predictions and measured logical outcomes.

**Model / system.** The work applies to quantum error-correcting codes, using circuit-level data from hardware like Google's Sycamore processor, and is mathematically formalized using tensor networks for decoding.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate (LER), syndrome data, and logical observable outcomes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Variational noise parameters ($	heta$), device drift amplitude ($\sigma$), and the code distance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The objective function is proven to be principled, minimizing the cross-entropy achieves the information-theoretic minimum LER, and the method is generally applicable to any stabilizer code.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 shows the convergence of LER and MSE during training on a distance-3 surface code. Figure 4 demonstrates the online tracking of noise drift over time steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the variational framework, proves its information-theoretic optimality, instantiates it using tensor networks for exact gradient calculation, validates it on hardware data, and demonstrates real-time tracking of device drift.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Accurate noise models are essential for high-performance quantum error correction, yet characterizing the noise of a quantum device typically requires dedicated experiments. We present a variational framework that learns the noise model directly from quantum-error-correction syndrome and logical-observable data collected during error-corrected memory experiments. The fault-event probabilities are treated as variational parameters and optimized via gradient descent to minimize the binary cross-entropy between the decoder's predictions and experimental logical-observable outcomes. We prove that this objective is principled rather than ad hoc: a sufficiently expressive noise ansatz attains the information-theoretic minimum logical error rate. We instantiate this framework using a tensor-network decoder, which provides exact maximum-likelihood decoding and analytically differentiable gradients with respect to all noise parameters. Using circuit-level data from Google's Sycamore processor, and starting from an uninformed prior, the optimization recovers noise models whose logical error rates agree to within $2\%$ with those of Google's independently characterized detector error model. The mean squared error between the learned and reference noise parameters shows a clear overall decrease throughout training, confirming that the method recovers physically meaningful noise structure, not merely parameters that happen to decode well. We further demonstrate that the optimization can track device drifts in real time via warm-started updates, maintaining near-optimal decoding performance under synthetically evolving noise without the need for re-characterization. The approach is decoder-agnostic in its formulation and naturally extends to correlated noise models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a breakthrough in superconducting qubit control by demonstrating microwave-free state preparation and measurement. By coupling a computational qubit to a flux qubit, the authors achieve high-fidelity operations in timescales an order of magnitude faster than previous methods. This advancement significantly improves the scalability and performance metrics critical for building practical quantum computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering bottlenecks in superconducting quantum computing by proposing a fundamentally faster, microwave-free readout mechanism, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum control theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing fast, high-fidelity, and scalable state preparation and measurement (SPAM) protocols for superconducting qubits, as current methods are limited by slow microwave signals and long measurement times.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated microwave-free SPAM for superconducting qubits with fidelities exceeding 99% in extremely fast timescales (e.g., 10 ns for readout), an order of magnitude improvement over state-of-the-art techniques.

**Method.** The work employs adiabatic quantum evolution, utilizing a coupled Computational Qubit (CQ) and a Flux Qubit (FQ) to perform state transfer and readout without external microwave driving.

**Model / system.** The system involves superconducting qubits, specifically a CQ coupled to an FQ. The readout mechanism relies on an adiabatic swap of the quantum state into the FQ, followed by an anneal that converts the quantum state into a measurable classical circulating current.

**Key observables.** Fidelity (>99%), readout time ($\sim 10$ ns), quantum-to-digital conversion time (15 ns), and coherence time ($T_2^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The process is governed by balancing competing timescales: the Landau-Zener transition rate ($	au_{LZ}$) versus the intrinsic decay rate ($	au_D$), with optimal operation occurring around 10 ns.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes compatibility with scalable superconducting digital control systems (SFQ electronics) and relies on the adiabatic nature of the state transfer process.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the adiabatic process of state conversion from the CQ to the FQ, the implementation of Rapid Adiabatic Passage (RAP) for preparation, and the use of Quantum Flux Parametrons (QFPs) for signal amplification.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the limitations of current SPAM techniques, then detailing the novel CQ/FQ coupled system and the RAP preparation protocol, followed by the readout mechanism involving adiabatic swapping and current projection, and finally discussing error sources like TLS loss.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fast, high-fidelity, scalable state preparation and measurement is critical to the realization of a quantum computing system. The state-of-the-art methods for preparation and readout of superconducting qubits require finely tuned microwave signals and ~100 ns of measurement time, which are major obstacles to the scalability and performance of superconducting quantum computers. Here, we have demonstrated novel, microwave-free methods for both preparation and readout of superconducting qubits with >99% fidelity in only 10 ns for either operation while maintaining qubit coherence. This technology is compatible with scalable superconducting digital control systems, and using quantum flux parametrons for amplification, we demonstrated full quantum-to-digital conversion in only 15 ns, which is an order of magnitude faster than state-of-the-art microwave-based techniques.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a quantum optical protocol for generating high-fidelity multicomponent Schrödinger cat states (e.g., 4-state, 8-state) by measuring photon numbers in multimode Gaussian states. The scheme uses squeezed vacuum states and beam splitters, demonstrating that fidelity can approach theoretical limits, although the probability of generating higher-order states decreases rapidly.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally relevant protocol for creating complex quantum states (cat states) that are highly sought after for quantum computation and quantum metrology, directly linking measurement theory to state preparation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes a probabilistic scheme to generate high-fidelity multicomponent Schrödinger cat states, specifically focusing on 4-component and 8-component superpositions.

**Main result.** The scheme successfully generates states approximating the target cat states with fidelities approaching the theoretical maximum, although the generation probability decreases rapidly for higher components.

**Method.** The generation relies on performing photon-number-resolving measurements on multimode Gaussian states, which are prepared using squeezed vacuum states and beam splitters.

**Model / system.** The system involves multimode Gaussian states, which are manipulated using linear optical elements (beam splitters) and measured using Photon-Number-Resolving Detectors (PNRDs). The target states are superpositions of Fock states, known as Schrödinger cat states.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($\mathcal{F}$), generation probability ($\mathcal{P}$), and the expansion coefficients of the generated state across Fock number basis states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The state is characterized by parameters $A$ and $B$ for 4-states, and the process involves optimizing over several physical parameters $\{r_1, r_2, t_1, t_2, \phi\}$ and the coherent state amplitude $eta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to perform PNR measurements and relies on deriving the optimal parameter configurations to maximize fidelity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the generation schemes (e.g., using two pairs of squeezed states for 4-states) and compare the achieved fidelity ($\mathcal{F}$) against the theoretical maximum ($\mathcal{F}_{	ext{max}}$) as a function of the number of superposition terms ($p$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the general scheme for 4-states using PNR measurements on Gaussian states. It then details a specialized, optimized scheme for 4-states, followed by the extension to 8-states by mixing two 4-states. Finally, it discusses the recursive generalization to higher-order states and analyzes the trade-off between fidelity and generation probability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we propose a scheme for generating superpositions of Fock states whose numbers differ by four or eight. The proposed protocol is based on photon-number-resolving measurements on multimode Gaussian states. We compare of the generated states with multicomponent Schrödinger cat states. We evaluate the fidelity and determine how it scales with the number of detected particles. Furthermore, we derive the optimal configuration for the generation such states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the design and experimental validation of superconducting flux memory devices intended to provide persistent, on-chip flux biasing for superconducting qubits. By developing three distinct memory types, the authors demonstrate a scalable method to reduce external control wiring complexity. The successful demonstration of stable flux biasing for over 20 hours paves the way for larger, more integrated quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of robust, on-chip, and persistent control elements like flux memory is crucial for scaling up quantum processors, directly impacting the feasibility of large-scale quantum computation architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for on-chip, persistent flux biasing sources for superconducting quantum circuits, such as qubits, to reduce the complexity of external control wiring.

**Main result.** The authors successfully developed and demonstrated three types of flux memory (SJJ, Flux Lock, Htron) that can store a stable DC flux bias for extended periods (over 20 hours), enabling scalable qubit control.

**Method.** The research involves developing superconducting circuits, simulating their behavior using SPICE, and experimentally characterizing the programmable flux storage and output using in-situ flux detectors.

**Model / system.** The core system is superconducting flux memory, implemented in three forms: Single Josephson Junction (SJJ), Flux Lock (requiring two JJs), and a Josephson Junction-free Cryotron (htron). These are designed to store persistent currents in superconducting loops.

**Key observables.** Stored flux (measured in units of flux quanta, $\Phi_0$), qubit state tunability frequency, and the achievable step size of the flux bias.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ability to scale control lines from $N^2$ to $2N$ in a 2D array is a key parameter demonstrating scalability. The stability time of 20 hours is a critical performance metric.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitations noted are the potential energy barrier depth limiting storage, and that the current simulation does not include kinetic inductance effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematics of the three memory types, the proposed 2D addressing scheme, and experimental results showing the flux biasing of a transmon qubit with stable frequency shifts over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of control line scaling, details the three memory architectures (SJJ, Flux Lock, Htron), presents the methods for programming and characterizing each type, and culminates in experimental demonstrations of using the memory to bias and tune a transmon qubit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the development of flux memory for use with superconducting circuits. This technology stores persistent currents in superconducting loops on-chip to be used to provide flux biasing for superconducting circuits, like qubits. We developed three types of flux memory and draw comparisons among them for circuit design. We demonstrate the utility of flux memory by using an in-situ flux detector and characterize each approach and further demonstrate that once flux is set in a memory cell, benchtop DC control sources can be powered off, leaving the on-chip flux bias in place. We propose that flux memory can be arranged in a two-dimensional configuration to multiplex control signals and reduce how line counts scale (N^2 devices -> 2N control lines), and our experimental results pave the path to the proposed scalability. We demonstrate the use of flux memory to flux bias a transmon qubit and show the tunability of the qubit state to a target frequency which remained stable on-chip for 20 hours.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses time-resolved spectroscopy on polariton condensates to probe the mechanisms driving bosonic stimulation. By comparing one-photon and two-photon pumping, the authors find that the dynamics are governed by different scattering processes. This suggests a novel dark state-mediated stimulation channel that could be crucial for highly-efficient THz lasing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium many-body dynamics in a controllable quantum optical system, providing quantitative evidence for multiple, distinct bosonic stimulation channels in a solid-state condensate.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the temporal signature of bosonic stimulation induced by dark excitons within a polariton condensate, comparing the dynamics under one-photon versus two-photon pumping.

**Main result.** A distinct difference in the detuning dependence of the buildup and relaxation rates is observed between the two pumping schemes, suggesting a new dark state-originated stimulation channel in the two-photon case.

**Method.** The research combines time-resolved photoluminescence spectroscopy with fitting the measured buildup and relaxation rates to a quadratic function of the condensate exciton fraction.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a strongly-coupled light-matter exciton-polariton condensate in a semiconductor microcavity. The dynamics are modeled using a semi-classical Boltzmann rate equation approach.

**Key observables.** Buildup and relaxation rates of the condensate-induced blueshifts, measured as a function of the LP ground state exciton fraction (|X_0|^2).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning ($\Delta$), temperature ($T \sim 6$ K), and the ratio of one-photon vs. two-photon pumping excitation energies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed rate dependencies can be captured by fitting the rates to a quadratic function of the exciton fraction, and that the model captures the dominant scattering mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, angle-resolved PL measurements across the condensation threshold for both pumping types, and plots showing the extracted buildup and relaxation rates as a function of |X_0|^2 for comparison.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform and the goal of comparing one- and two-photon pumping. It presents time-resolved measurements of the blueshift dynamics, analyzes the detuning dependence of the rates, and concludes by interpreting the differences in rate dependence as evidence for distinct stimulation channels.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strongly-coupled light-matter exciton-polaritons constitute an on-chip solid-state platform where the macroscopic quantum phenomenon of condensation is not only achievable at elevated temperatures, but can also be optically controlled, including through their interaction with inaccessible "dark" states. A recent study has shown that polariton condensation can be established under nonlinear two-photon pumping, paving the way towards dark state-condensate coherent control and highly-efficient terahertz (THz) lasing. In this letter, we show for the first time a temporal signature of dark exciton induced bosonic stimulation by investigating one- and two-photon pumped condensation with time-resolved photoluminescence spectroscopy. Our results show a clear difference in the detuning dependence of the buildup and relaxation rates of the condensate-induced blueshifts under one- and two-photon pumping. This difference is associated with the stronger exciton-fraction dependence expected for one-photon pumped condensation, where polariton-polariton stimulation dominates, compared with two-photon pumped condensation, where a 2p-exciton-to-lower-polariton THz transition can provide another stimulation channel, together with various spin-flip, electron-hole exchange and phonon-based mechanisms. These observations indicate the presence of a new dark state originated stimulation channel that could facilitate highly-efficient THz lasing in semiconductor microcavities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using a driven electromagnetic cavity as a novel platform to generate a steady-state population of chiral phonons. By modeling the cavity-phonon interaction, the authors demonstrate that this setup can induce a measurable, persistent effective magnetic field, offering a powerful tool to study phonon-mediated magnetism without relying on transient light pulses.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and open quantum systems because it models the coherent, driven coupling between two distinct quantum subsystems (photons/cavity modes and phonons) to engineer a macroscopic quantum response (magnetization) in a steady state.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to generate a steady-state population of chiral phonons, which carry angular momentum, to study the phono-magnetic effect, bypassing the limitations of transient excitation methods.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that using a driven electromagnetic cavity can generate a steady-state, non-zero effective phonon-induced magnetic field ($	ext{B}_{	ext{eff}}$) with realistic drive power.

**Method.** The methodology involves deriving the general interaction Hamiltonian between the cavity electromagnetic field and the chiral phonon modes, followed by solving the driven-dissipative dynamics using the Heisenberg-Langevin equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a material hosting chiral phonons coupled to a driven electromagnetic cavity. The model utilizes the cavity-phonon interaction Hamiltonian, $\hat{H}_{ac}$, and considers both chiral and linear cavity configurations.

**Key observables.** Effective magnetic field ($	ext{B}_{	ext{eff}}$), steady-state phonon population imbalance ($n_L - n_R$), and the phonon-cavity coupling rate ($g_k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Optimal coupling strength ($g_{opt}$), cavity decay rates ($\kappa_a, \kappa_c$), and external drive power ($P$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the small sample-cavity volume ratio ($V_s \ll V_c$) and employs the Rotating Wave Approximation (RWA) for the dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the interaction between chiral phonons and the cavity field, showing the dependence of phonon population imbalance on coupling strength and detuning, and visualizing the resulting polariton frequencies.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for steady-state generation to deriving the general interaction Hamiltonian, applying this to chiral and linear cavity setups, solving the resulting driven-dissipative dynamics, and finally calculating the resulting effective magnetic field.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Lattice vibrations carrying angular momentum give rise to fundamental phenomena such as the phonon Hall effect and the Einstein-de Haas effect, offering new opportunities for manipulating angular momentum in condensed-matter systems. Generating such circularly polarized phonons requires either the use of an external magnetic field, or of polarized light pulses. The latter approach does not require the use of any magnetic response of the material, but it has limitations, in particular regarding the duration of the pulse. Consequently, any effect stemming from phonons generated by light pulses is transient. In this paper, we propose the use of a driven electromagnetic cavity as a route to generate a steady-state population of chiral phonons. We derive a general description of cavity-chiral phonon interaction, with a particular focus on modes of chiral cavities. We show that, under optimized conditions, a significant effective phonon-induced magnetic field can be generated by means of an external drive with realistic power. Our approach is specially tailored for Gamma point phonons with THz frequencies, and opens a new route for investigating chiral phonons with electromagnetic cavities.

</details>

**Summary.** This review provides a comprehensive overview of Exceptional Points (EPs) in open photonic systems, establishing a unified theoretical framework for understanding these non-Hermitian degeneracies. It details various types of EPs and demonstrates their utility in controlling light properties for applications ranging from sensing to nonlinear optics. The work concludes by critically assessing the practical challenges for realizing EP-based photonic technologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant for a theoretical physicist working on AMO or open quantum systems as it provides a rigorous mathematical framework (Jordan blocks, non-Hermiticity) to analyze phenomena in open quantum optics and photonics, extending concepts from quantum mechanics to classical wave systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a unified framework for understanding and utilizing Exceptional Points (EPs) in open photonic systems, which are governed by non-Hermitian physics.

**Main result.** EPs enable advanced light control, including enhanced sensing, directional scattering, and polarization manipulation, by exploiting the non-analytic spectral topology inherent to these degeneracies.

**Method.** The review systematically analyzes EPs by classifying them according to the underlying operator (e.g., scattering matrix, Liouvillian) and employing spectral theory, Jordan normal form analysis, and perturbation theory.

**Model / system.** The primary system is open photonic structures, modeled using non-Hermitian Hamiltonians or Liouvillian superoperators. The analysis covers various types of EPs, including Hamiltonian, absorbing, Bloch, and Floquet EPs.

**Key observables.** Eigenvalue coalescence, spectral topology (monodromy), eigenvalue splitting ($\propto \sqrt{\epsilon}$ at $EP_2$), and measurable photonic responses like directional scattering and polarization control.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters involve the degree of non-Hermiticity, the coupling strengths, and the system's response to external perturbations ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The review critically assesses limitations regarding robustness, noise, and scalability, acknowledging that enhanced responsivity does not guarantee superior measurement precision.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a historical timeline of EP development (Figure 1) and the generalization of the Jordan form, illustrating the mathematical structure of the degeneracies.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a comprehensive review, first defining EPs and their mathematical basis, then systematically categorizing different types of EPs in various physical contexts (Hamiltonian, scattering, Liouvillian), and finally reviewing their diverse applications and current technological limitations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Open photonic systems provide a versatile platform for non-Hermitian physics, enabling control over complex spectra, transport, and light-matter interactions. Exceptional points (EPs), at which eigenvalues and eigenvectors coalesce and the governing operator becomes defective, play a central role because they combine branch-point spectral topology, nonanalytic perturbative response, and controllable eigenstate conversion. This Review provides a unified framework for EP photonics by systematically distinguishing exceptional degeneracies according to the underlying operator, spectral variable, boundary conditions, and experimentally accessible observables. We discuss Hamiltonian EPs, absorbing EPs associated with scattering zeros, real-frequency scattering-matrix and Jones-matrix EPs, Bloch and Floquet EPs, and Liouvillian EPs in open quantum systems. We review their spectral topology, static and dynamical encircling, higher-order exceptional structures, and coexistence with bound states in the continuum, together with applications in sensing, lasing, coherent absorption, directional scattering, polarization and wavefront control, nonlinear optics, optical storage, nonreciprocal photonics, and quantum photonics. We also critically assess the current limitations, practical challenges, and future perspectives of EP-based photonic technologies, with particular attention to robustness, noise, scalability, and experimentally measurable performance.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates quantum phase transitions in the cavity Heisenberg spin-chain model, a system relevant to quantum optics. By combining quantum information measures with semiclassical analysis, the authors map out a phase diagram revealing a unique 'deformed phase.' They establish a deep connection between excited-state transitions and the system's ergodic properties using spectral analysis.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a general theoretical framework for characterizing complex phase structures and spectral features in light-matter quantum many-body systems, which is highly relevant to cavity QED and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates ground-state and excited-state quantum phase transitions (QPTs/ESQPTs) and the ergodic-nonergodic transition (ENET) in the cavity Heisenberg spin-chain model.

**Main result.** The authors identified a novel 'deformed phase' interpolating between normal and superradiant regimes, characterized by logarithmic nonanalyticity in the density of states. They also established a spectral-structure mechanism linking ESQPTs to the ENET.

**Method.** The study combines quantum information measures with semiclassical fixed-point analysis, analyzing the system in the semiclassical limit ($\hbar 	o 0$).

**Model / system.** The system is the cavity Heisenberg spin-chain (CHS) model, which couples a Heisenberg spin chain to a single-mode cavity radiation field, relating it to the Dicke model.

**Key observables.** Order parameter $\langle J_z angle$, Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR), Density of States (DoS), and multifractal dimensions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on various coupling strengths ($\omega_c, g_1, \omega_a, g_2$) and the system size $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the semiclassical approximation ($\hbar 	o 0$) and fixed-point analysis of the classical Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 uses observables like $\langle J_z angle/(N/2)$ and IPR to distinguish between the Normal Phase (NP), Superradiant Phase (SP), and the intermediate Deformed Phase. Figure 2 maps the phase diagram across these three regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the Hamiltonian and the physical problem (QPTs/ESQPTs). It then uses semiclassical analysis to map out the phase diagram (NP, SP, Deformed). Finally, it analyzes the spectral properties and diagnostics to confirm the existence and nature of the transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay between quantum criticality and ergodicity breaking constitutes a central challenge in complex quantum many-body systems. Here, we investigate ground-state quantum phase transitions (QPTs) and excited-state quantum phase transitions (ESQPTs), as well as ergodic-nonergodic transition in the cavity Heisenberg spin-chain (CHS) model. By combining quantum information measures with semiclassical fixed-point analysis, we identify a deformed phase that interpolates between the normal and superradiant phases, featuring a logarithmic nonanalyticity in the density of states (DoS) and two additional jump discontinuities. We further elucidate the spectral-structure mechanism underlying the connection between ESQPTs and the ergodic--nonergodic transition (ENET) via level statistics, participation ratios, and multifractal analysis. Our results provide a general framework for characterizing phase structures and spectral features in light--matter quantum many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes an information-geometric framework to analyze non-equilibrium quantum dynamics by treating the state space as an extended manifold parameterized by time and a control parameter. It derives key geometric metrics, such as the mixed metric component $g_{ht}$, which quantify the coupling between parameter changes and time evolution. The results show that these geometric measures correlate with, but offer complementary information to, standard diagnostics like entanglement entropy and the Loschmidt echo.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit focus on the mixed metric component $g_{ht}$ and the resulting geometric coupling $ho_{ht}$ offers a novel, coordinate-independent diagnostic tool for quantifying how parameter tuning influences time evolution in non-equilibrium quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops an information-geometric framework to characterize the dynamics of quantum states evolving out of equilibrium.

**Main result.** The geometric structure, quantified by the mixed metric component $g_{ht}$ and the Metric-Volume Density (MVD), provides a systematic way to measure the interplay between parameter variation and real-time evolution.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) metric tensor defined on an extended information manifold parameterized by a control parameter $h$ and time $t$.

**Model / system.** The transverse-field Ising chain model is used as a primary laboratory system, but the framework is shown to be general and applicable to models like the $Z_3$ Potts chain and the lattice Schwinger model.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information metric components ($g_{hh}, g_{tt}, g_{ht}$), Metric-Volume Density (DMV), normalized alignment coefficient ($ho_{ht}$), entanglement entropy ($S_A(t)$), and Loschmidt echo ($L(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The post-quench Hamiltonian parameter ($h$ or $\alpha$) and real time ($t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the state can be mapped onto an extended information manifold, and correlations with conventional measures are analyzed but not assumed to be perfectly predictive.

**Figures summary.** Figures show heatmaps of the QFI metric components on the $(h, t)$ manifold, the evolution of DMV and $ho_{ht}$ over time, and correlation matrices comparing geometric measures with entanglement entropy and the Loschmidt echo.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the geometric framework by defining the QFI metric on the $(h, t)$ manifold, derives key geometric observables like $g_{ht}$ and $ho_{ht}$, and then applies this framework to various models (Ising, Potts, Schwinger) to correlate these geometric measures with established non-equilibrium diagnostics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We discuss an information-geometric framework for characterizing quantum states in non-equilibrium dynamics. Using the transverse-field Ising chain model as a laboratory, we investigate the quantum Fisher information metric with particular emphasis on the mixed metric component $g_{ht}$ and related geometric observables, where $h$ is a controllable post-quench Hamiltonian parameter and $t$ is real time. The framework treats $h$ and $t$ as coordinates of an extended information manifold $(h,t)$. The geometric observables characterize the speed of evolution on the manifold and the alignment between the temporal and parameter-deformation directions. Correlations between these geometric quantities and two widely used measures of non-equilibrium dynamics, the entanglement entropy and the Loschmidt echo, are analyzed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a size-dependent kinetic construction to model nanocrystal growth, moving beyond symmetry-preserving assumptions. By showing that facet growth rates depend on the facet's size, the authors demonstrate that even tiny initial asymmetries can be amplified into large, macroscopic shape anisotropies. This mechanism is crucial for understanding how crystal morphology is determined by local, size-sensitive kinetic processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of size-dependent, non-linear kinetics leading to symmetry breaking is highly relevant to non-equilibrium dynamics, pattern formation, and the study of open quantum systems where boundary effects or finite size corrections become crucial.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a predictive theory explaining how infinitesimal initial asymmetries in nanocrystals can be amplified into strongly asymmetric morphologies, overcoming the symmetry constraints of traditional growth models.

**Main result.** Size-dependent kinetic growth rates, arising from finite facet effects, cause deterministic shape evolution to amplify small perturbations, leading to macroscopic shape symmetry breaking.

**Method.** The authors developed a size-dependent kinetic construction generalizing the Wulff construction, using probabilistic lattice gas models to derive facet growth velocities dependent on facet size.

**Model / system.** The study models the growth of nanocrystals (e.g., on square, triangular, or FCC lattices) where facet growth rates are governed by local nucleation barriers and ligand coverages that vary with the instantaneous facet size.

**Key observables.** Facet growth velocities ($V_\alpha(D)$), shape asymmetry measures ($A_{2d}, A_{3d}$), and the evolution of crystal morphology (e.g., cuboctahedra to rods).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Facet size ($D$), ligand coverage ($ho_l$), and the crossover areas ($A_{c,\alpha}$) where growth kinetics change significantly.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes ligands equilibrate quickly, allowing the facet state to switch between ligand-poor and ligand-rich regimes, and the growth is deterministic based on derived rate laws.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the microscopic origin of size-dependent velocities, show the instability of symmetric growth on 2D lattices, and demonstrate the amplification of small seed perturbations into highly anisotropic 3D rod shapes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the limitations of the standard kinetic Wulff construction to developing the size-dependent kinetic construction using a lattice gas model. It then applies this theory to 2D and 3D systems, showing how size-dependent rates lead to shape instability and macroscopic asymmetry amplification.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The kinetic Wulff construction predicts symmetric nonequilibrium shapes when crystallographically equivalent facets share a fixed growth rate. However, nanocrystals grow through finite facets whose nucleation barriers and ligand coverages depend on facet size. Here we develop a size-dependent kinetic construction and show that as a consequence infinitesimal seed asymmetries can be amplified into strongly asymmetric nanocrystal morphologies even when all symmetry-related facets obey the same microscopic growth law. Spatially heterogeneous nucleation rates and boundary limited growth generate facet growth velocities that depend on size, and deterministic shape evolution translates these local rates into global shape symmetry breaking. We illustrate the mechanism of persistent anisotropic growth on square and triangular lattices in two dimensions and on FCC seeds in three dimensions, where cuboctahedra can evolve toward rods or tetrahedra depending on which facet-area perturbations are amplified.

</details>
