**Summary.** This work proposes a general theoretical framework for quantum mechanics based on 'spacetime-local beables' that resolves the conflict posed by Bell's theorem. It distinguishes between the underlying ontic state and the observer's epistemic state, providing a mechanism for wavefunction collapse. By using Gibbs field models, the theory reproduces quantum predictions in a specific limit while respecting a generalized notion of causality.

**Why it may be interesting.** It offers a foundational, non-standard approach to quantum foundations that attempts to derive quantum mechanics from principles of spacetime locality and causality, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the conflict between Bell's theorem, which rules out locally causal theories for quantum entanglement, and the need to formulate a general theory of quantum mechanics.

**Main result.** It proposes a general framework using 'spacetime-local beables' governed by Gibbs fields, distinguishing between an ontic state (actual reality) and an epistemic state (knowledge), which naturally explains wavefunction collapse.

**Method.** The authors develop a mathematical framework based on Spacetime-Local Gibbs-Field (SLGF) models that generalize Bell's local causality to 'lenient causality.'

**Model / system.** The theory models physical setups involving sources, detectors, and internal variables, using a mathematical structure analogous to statistical physics (Gibbs random fields).

**Key observables.** The empirically verifiable predictions are expected to show parallels to the Born rule, and the epistemic state is analogous to the quantum state $\Psi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The limit $\gamma 	o 0$ is crucial for recovering standard Quantum Mechanics predictions in the toy models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes 'lenient causality' (allowing future inputs for internal variables) while maintaining signal causality for outcomes, and it treats the arrow of time as a separate assumption.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 sketches the spacetime setup, contrasting ruled-out locally causal descriptions with the proposed time-reversal symmetric Gibbs field structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the SLGF framework, defines the ontic and epistemic states, develops the mathematical structure using local weights, and tests the framework by comparing predictions to the Born rule using a toy model (Schulman model).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bell's theorem rules out developing a locally causal theory to describe quantum phenomena. Many take this to imply that any model of quantum entanglement must employ variables (called beables by Bell) which follow nonlocal rules, even though signaling is local. The alternative is to adopt an all-at-once (block universe) approach, with beables which may depend on both past and future inputs, even though signaling is causal. Within this lenient-causality approach (a.k.a. retrocausal), simple cases of entanglement have been successfully described by locally mediated stochastic toy models, i.e.,~toy models which are local in a sense which generalizes Bell's local causality. Developing a widely applicable reformulation of quantum mechanics along these lines is a grand challenge. This work presents a general framework for such models and theories, and identifies the corresponding ontic and epistemic states. The epistemic state is closely analogous to the quantum state, yielding an explanation for the collapse of the wavefunction. In the case of the models of the framework, it is clear what the information is about. The expression for the empirically verifiable predictions of the models in terms of the ontic and epistemic states displays remarkable parallels to the Born rule. A toy-model example is discussed.

</details>

### [Completing or Refusing Low-Dimensional Records of Structured Quantum Circuits: Measurement Loss, Compression Loss, and Hardware Drift](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01303v1)

**Authors:** Gunhee Cho, Juhee Lee  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01303v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `QC/QI experiment` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.01303_figures/2609.01303_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Information descent and decision routes. Panel (a) locates loss introduced by preprocessing, measurement, and record compression. Panel (b) separates the default finite-data audit from the optional algebraic audit for claims over all analytic control curves. The latter requires analytic response germs and a declared library of physically executable augmentations; it is not inferred from finite counts alone.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.01303_figures/2609.01303_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 2: The three-qubit running circuit. It prepares one excitation, redistributes it by G01(a) and G02(b), and records the computational bitstring. Its ideal law assigns zero probability outside S = {100, 010, 001}.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive framework to audit low-dimensional records summarizing quantum circuits, quantifying information loss due to measurement or compression. It merges quantum information theory with algebraic geometry to create an executable 'approve/refuse/defer' decision protocol. This is vital for building reliable quantum hardware by certifying that simplified measurements retain sufficient fidelity to the true quantum process.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a mathematically rigorous, general framework for quantifying and certifying the information loss when abstracting complex quantum processes into simplified, hardware-friendly metrics, which is crucial for scaling quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental challenge of determining if a low-dimensional statistical record of a structured quantum circuit accurately preserves the full quantum information, accounting for measurement loss, compression loss, and hardware drift.

**Main result.** The authors establish a rigorous, executable 'completion-or-refusal' procedure based on algebraic criteria, allowing for finite-sample decisions (Approve, Refuse, or Defer) regarding the fidelity of the low-dimensional record.

**Method.** The methodology combines advanced quantum information metrics (Quantum Fisher Information, Hellinger residual) with tools from algebraic geometry (integral closures, Rees valuations) to create a multi-stage audit framework.

**Model / system.** The system involves structured quantum circuits implemented on quantum hardware, whose outcomes are summarized by low-dimensional records. The analysis models the process through stages: initial state, circuit evolution, measurement, and final record compression.

**Key observables.** Hellinger residual, simultaneous confidence bounds, quantum Fisher information metrics ($g_{SLD}, g_{Q,e}, g_{T,e,a}$), and the decision outcomes: Approve, Refuse, Defer.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit parameters ($	heta$), noise models (avoided assumption), finite-sample size, and the declared loss budget.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method aims to avoid assuming a specific parametric hardware-noise model or a low-dimensional statistical family, though the final experimental validation is limited to specific backends and circuits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 summarizes two analysis routes: a default finite-data audit and an optional algebraic audit using response ideals, explicitly separating the two decision paths.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework connecting all-arc criteria (algebraic/global) to executable, finite-sample decision procedures (local/experimental), culminating in hardware tests on specific circuit families.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Structured quantum circuits are often summarized by low-dimensional records. Records lose control-dependent information when they merge outcomes whose probabilities respond differently to circuit parameters. We assess this loss without a parametric hardware-noise model or low-dimensional statistical family. Quantum Fisher information bounds premeasurement sensitivity; Fisher metrics of measurements and records describe device-attainable sensitivity. A Hellinger residual measures the response removed by a record, while its local quadratic term is the conditional covariance of the full-outcome score. Simultaneous confidence bounds support approval, refusal, or deferral.   We prove a finite-library completion theorem: executable augmentations terminate with either a record preserving every declared response or proof that no library augmentation removes the loss. For an analytic control germ, integral closures characterize preservation along every analytic control arc, and finitely many Rees valuations detect failure. This state--measurement--record chain connects an all-arc criterion to executable completion or refusal, an attainable-Fisher local kernel criterion, and finite-sample decisions.   A three-qubit calculation separates measurement loss from record loss. IBM experiments on Kingston and Marrakesh test decisions in fixed-particle-number and GHZ families, including negative controls. An end-to-end Kingston experiment reduces calibration shots by 33.3% while meeting prespecified noninferiority criteria on two held-out objectives. In two-epoch IQM Garnet data, median record-level drift is 0.0797 times full-distribution drift, but a significant residual remains in 35 of 36 settings. These experiments validate failure detection on the tested circuits; they do not establish universal compression performance or device quantum Fisher information.

</details>

Note at N = 1, the POP is just p0.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper derives a strict, physics-independent lower bound on the entropy production for any system undergoing periodic driving. By analyzing the Mismatch Cost (MMC) over multiple cycles, the authors establish a generalized second law applicable to diverse systems like spin models and digital automata. This result significantly strengthens current understanding of non-equilibrium thermodynamics in time-periodic settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** The derivation of a universal, physics-independent bound on entropy production for periodic systems provides a powerful new tool for analyzing non-equilibrium dynamics in complex systems, extending beyond standard Markovian assumptions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Deriving a physics-independent lower bound on the entropy production for any periodic process, strengthening the standard second law of thermodynamics.

**Main result.** A lower bound on entropy production is established using the Mismatch Cost (MMC) that depends only on the initial distribution and the driving map, regardless of the underlying physical details.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Mismatch Cost (MMC) decomposition of a general thermodynamic cost function, optimizing over all possible prior distributions to find a minimal dissipation bound.

**Model / system.** The framework applies to general stochastic processes evolving under periodic driving, modeled by repeatedly applying a stochastic map G. Specific applications include the Curie-Weiss model (Ising spins) and Deterministic Finite Automata (DFA).

**Key observables.** Entropy Production ($\Sigma_t$), Mismatch Cost (MMC), and the periodic-optimal prior ($ar{q}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The periodicity of the driving process, the initial distribution $p_0$, and the map $G$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The process is modeled by the repeated application of a single map G, and the analysis relies on the general structure of the MMC decomposition.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the general MMC decomposition, then specializing this to periodic processes, and finally applying the derived bounds to specific physical models like the Curie-Weiss model and DFAs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many physical systems evolve under periodic driving: the same control protocol is applied again and again, even though the state of the system itself need not return to where it started after each cycle. We derive a physics-independent lower bound on the entropy production of \emph{any} periodic process modeled by the evolution of an initial distribution $p_0$ by a repeated application of the same map $G$. This is a strict strengthening of the second law of thermodynamics for periodic processes. It does not require that the single-period dynamics arise from a CTMC, satisfy (local) detailed balance, or be subject to other typical restrictions. We discuss its application to spins in the Curie-Weiss model and Deterministic Finite Automata, with possible extensions to other uniform computers.

</details>

**Authors:** Alex Rice, Chris Heunen, Tobias Grosser  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01037v1>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the limitations of static quantum circuit models by proposing a Dynamic Intermediate Representation (IR). This new IR elevates quantum gates to first-class, runtime values, allowing classical computation to dynamically control quantum behavior. It enables the compact representation and optimization of complex hybrid quantum-classical algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial compilation framework for simulating and optimizing complex quantum algorithms that inherently mix classical decision-making with quantum evolution, which is vital for near-term quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The standard quantum circuit model is too static to effectively represent hybrid quantum-classical applications where gate choices depend on runtime data or measurement outcomes.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a new Intermediate Representation (IR) that treats quantum gates as first-class, dynamic values, enabling classical control flow to steer quantum computation.

**Method.** The core method is the design of a dynamic IR, realized using MLIR dialects, which incorporates dynamic gate operations (e.g., qssa.dyn_gate) alongside static quantum operations.

**Model / system.** The model concerns hybrid quantum-classical programs, where quantum operations are combined with classical data and control flow. The IR supports representing quantum states and operations using value semantics (SSA form).

**Key observables.** Gate values (e.g., I, X, Y, Z, S), phase flips, and the ability to model conditional quantum operations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the quantum gates and the classical control flow determining gate execution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The traditional circuit model is inherently static, and the XZ gadget is used to simplify the representation of conditional Pauli gates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dynamic gate model's ability to represent complex sequences, such as two concatenated probabilistic phase-flip channels or double phase flips, using dynamic control flow compared to static methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for dynamic gates, details the XZ gadget for conditional Pauli gates, describes the IR structure using MLIR dialects (qssa.dyn_gate), and shows how this framework supports optimizations and analysis for hybrid programs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum compilers typically follow the circuit model, representing programs as fixed sequences of gates. This static view breaks down in hybrid quantum-classical applications, where gate choices depend on runtime data or measurement results. We introduce a new Intermediate Representation (IR) that elevates gates to first-class values, enabling their dynamic creation, composition, and control. This unified representation allows classical computation to steer quantum behaviour, capturing phenomena including stochastic gate selection, adaptive error correction, and measurement-driven computation within a single framework. Case studies in noise modelling, randomised compilation, error correction, and measurement-based quantum computing show that our IR expresses these programs compactly and supports optimisations that were not possible in the circuit model. Evaluation on a benchmark suite of hybrid quantum-classical programs indicates that our IR represents programs compactly and facilitates compiler analysis and transformation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops efficient quantum expanders implementable with shallow circuits and applies them to construct 1D quantum Hamiltonians. The key achievement is demonstrating a construction that realizes the conjectured optimal relationship between the ground state entanglement entropy and the spectral gap ($S \propto \Delta^{-1/2}$). This advances the understanding of entanglement scaling in gapped quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit construction of Hamiltonians achieving the conjectured optimal entanglement-gap scaling ($\alpha=1/2$) is highly relevant for understanding the fundamental limits of entanglement in gapped quantum phases, which is central to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses constructing quantum expanders with efficient circuit implementations and establishing optimal relationships between spectral gap and entanglement entropy in 1D quantum systems.

**Main result.** They constructed a family of frustration-free 1D Hamiltonians achieving the conjectured optimal entanglement-gap scaling $S = \Theta(\Delta^{-1/2})$, and developed depth-1 quantum expanders on the unitary group.

**Method.** The work combines the construction of quantum expanders (using Pauli/CNOT gates) with theoretical analysis of 1D Hamiltonians, relating spectral gaps to entanglement entropy via specific constructions.

**Model / system.** The models involve quantum expanders on the unitary group $U(d)$ and $SU(d)$, and 1D quantum many-body systems described by Hamiltonians $\{H_n\}$ that are engineered using these expanders.

**Key observables.** Spectral Gap ($\Delta$), Bipartite Entanglement Entropy ($S$), and the scaling exponent $\alpha$ relating $S$ and $\Delta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $n$ of the system, the constant gap $\Delta$, and the required circuit depth (achieved to be depth-1).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the conjectured optimal scaling $S = \Theta(\Delta^{-1/2})$ for frustration-free 1D Hamiltonians, which the authors successfully achieve a construction for.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the context implies figures illustrating the expander construction, the Hamiltonian family, and the scaling relationship between $S$ and $\Delta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining and constructing depth-1 quantum expanders. It then applies these expanders to build a family of frustration-free 1D Hamiltonians, proving the optimal entanglement-gap scaling. Finally, it extends the expander concept to the unitary group for applications like state testing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a constant-degree and constant-gap quantum expander on $n$ qubits where each unitary can be implemented by a depth-$1$ and 1D circuit of Pauli or CNOT gates. We provide two applications of this expander. First, we use it to construct a family of frustration-free 1D Hamiltonians whose ground states obey the entanglement-gap relation $S = Θ(Δ^{-1/2})$; this is believed to be optimal, but achieving it had been open. Second, we use it to provide a streaming protocol that tests for closeness to a class of 1D volume-law entangled states.   Moreover, we extend our quantum expander to a constant-degree and constant-gap expander on the unitary group where each unitary is a single $T$ gate, a single $T^{\dagger}$ gate, or a depth-$1$ Clifford circuit. This implies that a random sequence of unitaries from the expander yields a gapped walk on a dense subgroup of the unitary group. This improves upon previous work by Bourgain and Gamburd which did not control the dependence of the gap on the dimension.

</details>

**Authors:** Mi-Jung So, James Moran, Youngrong Lim, Mahn-Soo Choi, Hyukjoon Kwon  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01373v1>

Each party mixes the received signal mode (1 or 2) with an ancillary mode (3 or 4) on a local beamsplitter of angle θ, performs homodyne detection on the ancillary output to obtain M = {x3, x4}, and—conditioned on the classically communicated outcomes—applies a single-mode Gaussian op- eration ˆUGi(M) to the surviving signal mode.</sub>

Each party mixes the received mode with an auxiliary vacuum mode |0⟩using a local beamsplitter with parameter θ. Both parties then perform homodyne detection on one of the output modes, producing outcomes x3 and x4, while the remaining mode forms the post-measurement state used for the fidelity readout. We collect these outcomes into the classical set M = {x3, x4}. This corresponds to choosing l = 0 and ˆUGi(M) = I, the identity operator, in the general circuit.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2609.01373_figures/2609.01373_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2609.01373_figures/2609.01373_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 4. Measurement-based displacement scheme. Con- ditioned on the set of measurement outcomes M = {x3, x4} obtained on modes 3 and 4, the displacement gates ˆD (αi(M)) (i = 1, 2) shift the post-measurement state to the phase-space origin, rendering the output fidelity indepen- dent of M and optimal. This corresponds to choosing l = 0 and ˆUGi(M) = ˆD(αi(M)) in the general circuit.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2609.01373_figures/2609.01373_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 5. Fidelity as a function of θ for the simplest scheme (Fig. 2) and the measurement-based displacement circuit (Fig. 4), shown for several values of λ. Each value of λ is repre- sented by a distinct color, with the dashed and solid curves of the same color corresponding to the simplest scheme and the measurement-based displacement circuit, respectively. The latter consistently achieves higher fidelity, demonstrating the advantage of this approach.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to quantify the trade-off between successfully identifying a quantum state and disturbing it during the measurement process. It focuses on two-mode squeezed vacuum states using local Gaussian measurements. The key finding is that incorporating pre-shared entanglement can enhance discrimination beyond the standard theoretical limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial infinite-dimensional generalization of the information-disturbance tradeoff, extending established concepts from finite-dimensional quantum systems to continuous-variable quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work analyzes the non-destructive discrimination of two-mode squeezed vacuum (TMSV) states using local Gaussian measurements.

**Main result.** The authors establish an explicit tradeoff relation between success probability and measurement-induced disturbance, and show that pre-shared entanglement allows exceeding the standard local Gaussian bound.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving the success probability and fidelity using Gaussian state theory, optimizing a score function ($P \cdot F$), and comparing different measurement protocols.

**Model / system.** The system involves two-mode squeezed vacuum states, which are continuous-variable (CV) Gaussian states. The process is modeled using local Gaussian measurements and potentially pre-shared entanglement.

**Key observables.** Success Probability ($P$), Fidelity ($F$), and the combined score function ($P \cdot F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing Parameter ($\lambda$), beamsplitter angle ($	heta$), and the strength of pre-shared entanglement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the GLOCC1 class (at most one adaptive stage) for the primary bound derivation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the general local Gaussian circuit for discrimination, comparing performance across different measurement schemes (simplest vs. displacement vs. squeezing) as a function of the beam splitter angle $	heta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general framework for local Gaussian discrimination, then optimizing the score function $P \cdot F$ for various measurement strategies, and finally deriving the optimal trade-off relation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Typical measurement setups in quantum systems are destructive, meaning that states are irretrievably altered after measurement. In this work, we analyse non-destructive discrimination of two two-mode squeezed vacuum states using local Gaussian measurements. We investigate a tradeoff relation between the success probability of discrimination and the fidelity of the resulting state with the initial state, and construct a protocol given by local Gaussian measurements, which is optimal within our numerically explored class. We also extend to the case where we allow for additional pre-shared entanglement, and show that this regime allows us to exceed the standard local Gaussian bound for the fidelity-success probability tradeoff. Our work provides a natural extension of the tradeoff between information gain and disturbance in entangled-state discrimination, previously established for finite-dimensional quantum systems, to infinite-dimensional continuous-variable systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously benchmarks various classical optimization algorithms for VQE applied to frustrated quantum spin models. By characterizing the geometry of the energy landscape, the authors demonstrate that optimization performance is highly dependent on the model structure and that the total error budget must account separately for optimization failure and the inherent variational gap. This guides the development of more robust quantum simulation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep, quantitative analysis of the limitations of classical optimization techniques when applied to complex, frustrated quantum many-body systems, which is crucial for designing practical quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper benchmarks classical optimization algorithms for Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) calculations to understand how the underlying Hamiltonian-ansatz landscape geometry dictates performance.

**Main result.** Optimizer performance varies significantly across different frustrated spin model hierarchies, and the study successfully separates optimization error from the inherent variational gap error.

**Method.** The authors benchmark eight classical optimizers (local, stochastic, evolutionary, etc.) using exact statevector simulation on controlled spin models, characterizing the energy landscape geometry.

**Model / system.** The study uses frustrated quantum spin models, including diagonal Ising glasses, transverse-field Ising, and anisotropic Heisenberg models. The Hamiltonian is parameterized to cover these different physical regimes.

**Key observables.** Optimization error ($\Delta E_{	ext{opt}}$), ground-state energy error ($\Delta E$), and the variational gap ($E_{	ext{ref}} - E_0$). Fidelity is also used as a diagnostic.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison involves varying circuit depth (from product-state to hardware-efficient ansatz) and the model parameters ($\Gamma, \lambda$) defining the Hamiltonian.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on exact statevector simulation to isolate the intrinsic landscape from finite-shot noise, and it distinguishes between optimization difficulty and variational expressivity limits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the two ansatz architectures (product-state vs. hardware-efficient) used for the different models.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically benchmarks optimizers across a hierarchy of frustrated spin models (Q1, Q2, Q3), characterizing the landscape geometry and quantifying the sources of error in VQE performance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We benchmark eight classical optimizers for exact-statevector VQE calculations on a controlled hierarchy of frustrated spin models, ranging from a diagonal Ising glass to transverse-field Ising and anisotropic Heisenberg models. The benchmark includes local, stochastic-gradient, evolutionary, covariance-adaptation, and swarm-based optimization methods under matched function-evaluation budgets. To understand their performance beyond final energies, we characterize the underlying Hamiltonian--ansatz landscapes in terms of local minima, gradients, curvature, and ground-state reachability. We use simple variational circuits, from an $R_y$ product-state ansatz for the diagonal model to shallow $R_y$--CNOT hardware-efficient circuits for the noncommuting models, and study how increasing circuit depth changes their expressivity, reachability, and optimization geometry. We find that optimizer performance changes substantially across the model hierarchy and is closely connected to landscape structure, while the variational gap represents a separate source of error. These results show how classical optimization, variational expressivity, and landscape geometry jointly determine VQE performance for frustrated spin models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents QILP-0, a method to construct 'observational declarative twins' for quantum circuits, aiming to solve the interpretability problem in QML. It builds a formal, logical theory that reconstructs observed quantum behavior by tracking observables through a defined scope. Validation on large-scale qubit systems and image data confirms the theory's ability to achieve perfect, conflict-free reconstruction.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel, logically rigorous framework for quantum circuit interpretability that moves beyond simple learned representations by grounding the explanation in traceable, discrete logical statements derived directly from observable measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the opacity in Quantum Machine Learning (QML) by developing a method to reconstruct an explicitly scoped, traceable declarative theory of a quantum circuit's observable behavior.

**Main result.** The proposed QILP-0 theory successfully achieved complete, conflict-free reconstruction of observed relations with strict accuracy of one in both native-discrete (Bars & Stripes) and continuous (Low-Depth MNIST) validation settings.

**Method.** It introduces QXymb, a framework culminating in QILP-0, which constructs a finite multi-valued propositional logic program by traversing observables incrementally and mapping latent structure deterministically back to original observable columns.

**Model / system.** The system involves general quantum circuits, specifically utilizing Pauli observables for the QILP-0 specialization. Validation tests include product and grid-CZ embeddings up to 100 qubits and low-depth MNIST digit-0/1 instances.

**Key observables.** Observable responses, reference-relative coverage, and the resulting finite multi-valued propositional logic program (the 'observational declarative twin').

**Important parameters / regimes.** Observational scope, structural grading, observational reference horizon, and the number of qubits (16 to 100).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method explicitly separates logical exactness from numerical/backend uncertainty, retaining the latter as audit metadata. It does not claim to reconstruct the complete quantum behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QXymb framework, details the QILP-0 specialization for constructing declarative twins, outlines the incremental traversal and discretization process, and validates the method using two complementary settings: exhaustive Bars & Stripes experiments and Low-Depth MNIST analyses.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper introduces QXymb, a general framework for constructing observational declarative twins of quantum circuits, and develops QILP-0, its first complete order-0 specialization. QILP-0 constructs a finite multi-valued propositional logic program from observed circuit behaviour within a declared observational scope.   The pipeline traverses a declared family of quantum observables incrementally according to a reproducible structural grading and a declared observational reference horizon. Progress is quantified through reference-relative coverage against a fixed target-independent reference. Observable responses are organized through target-independent geometry, while retained latent structure is mapped deterministically back to original observable columns before symbolic processing, preserving observational semantics and provenance.   Selected observable profiles are converted into a finite relation through admissible target-independent discretization. The target is used only afterwards to audit twin-admissibility and induce the declarative theory. A theory is certified as an exact observational declarative twin when it completely and correctly reconstructs the resulting finite task-conditioned discrete relation. Logical exactness is therefore separated from numerical, backend, provider, and discretization uncertainty, which is retained as audit metadata.   Validation uses two complementary QML settings. Exhaustive Bars & Stripes experiments compare product and grid-CZ embeddings from 16 to 100 qubits and exercise the native-discrete branch. Low-Depth MNIST analyses all 14,708 digit-0/1 instances before and after a trained variational quantum transformation and exercises continuous discretization. In every reported relation, the induced QILP-0 theory achieves complete, conflict-free reconstruction with strict accuracy equal to one.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates quantum antidipolar systems in 2D, analyzing both single-layer and bilayer geometries. It calculates the equation of state and structural properties, finding that the system undergoes a first-order liquid-solid transition. Furthermore, it details how tuning the inter-layer separation allows one layer to significantly influence the superfluidity and energy of the other layer.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the complex physics of dipolar quantum gases in reduced dimensions, which is a major frontier in AMO physics, particularly concerning phase transitions and interlayer coupling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the properties of two-dimensional (2D) quantum systems interacting via antidipolar forces, examining single-layer and bilayer configurations.

**Main result.** The system exhibits liquid-solid phase transitions at lower densities than expected for standard dipolar systems, and the bilayer structure shows tunable inter-layer coupling effects on superfluidity and energy.

**Method.** The research employs advanced quantum many-body techniques, including Diffusion Monte Carlo (DMC) and the calculation of expectation values using Jastrow-Slater trial wave functions.

**Model / system.** The model consists of particles with tunable antidipolar interactions, confined to 2D planes, potentially forming a bilayer structure. The Hamiltonian includes terms for intra-layer and inter-layer dipole-dipole interactions.

**Key observables.** Equation of state (EOS), structural properties, liquid-solid transition points, superfluid fraction ($f_s$), and condensate fraction ($f_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inter-layer distance ($h$), interaction strength ratio ($C_{12}/(E_0r_{12}^0)$), and particle density ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations like the Feynman approximation for excitation spectra and assumes the system can be described by specific Jastrow-type trial wave functions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate simulation snapshots of liquid and solid phases, the inter-particle potential, the energy per particle vs. density for the bilayer, and the superfluid/condensate fractions vs. inter-layer distance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the single-layer liquid-solid transition, then extending the analysis to the bilayer geometry to study how tuning the inter-layer distance modifies the system's thermodynamic and superfluid properties.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Particles with magnetic moment can be polarized, and rapidly rotated, employing a magnetic field such that the dipolar interaction among them changes sign, becoming antidipolar and thus isotropically attractive in a plane. Polar molecules can also be manipulated using microwave dressing fields to invert the sign of the dipole-dipole interaction. In this work, we study a two-dimensional system of antidipolar particles by calculating its equation of state and structural properties. The system behaves as a liquid even for scattering lengths significantly greater than the dipolar length. For large enough densities, the system transitions to a solid with one particle per lattice site via a first-order phase transition at a significantly smaller density than its dipolar counterpart. Moreover, motivated by the recent realization of a strongly axially trapped, bilayer geometry [Science 384, 546-551 (2024)], we study the properties of the bilayer liquid phase as the inter-layer distance is tuned, and provide the range of parameters where one layer can influence the properties of the other.

</details>

**Authors:** Alex Ben Ishay, Yuval Eyal, Yuval Cohen, Nati Erez  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00825v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `QC/QI experiment` **3/5**

**Summary.** This work applies the QAOA algorithm to optimize gas throughput in complex pipeline networks, transforming the continuous physical problem into a discrete QUBO format. The authors demonstrate the feasibility of this approach by running the circuit on a trapped-ion quantum processor, achieving near-optimal results with surprisingly shallow circuit depth. This establishes a tangible, end-to-end quantum-assisted optimization workflow for critical infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, real-world engineering optimization problem (gas flow) that is successfully mapped onto a quantum algorithm (QAOA) and tested on current hardware, serving as a proof-of-concept for quantum computation in industrial physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computationally hard problem of maximizing gas throughput in natural gas transmission networks subject to complex hydraulic and operational constraints.

**Main result.** The QAOA approach successfully recovered near-optimal solutions on a trapped-ion quantum processor using only $p=2$ layers, demonstrating feasibility with significantly shallower circuits than simulated.

**Method.** The problem is mapped to a QUBO formulation and solved using the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA), comparing simulator results ($p=30$) with direct hardware execution ($p=2$).

**Model / system.** The physical system is a natural gas pipeline network modeled as a graph, governed by the Panhandle-B hydraulic equation. The optimization is framed by a cost Hamiltonian combining delivery objectives and physical constraint penalties.

**Key observables.** Maximum gas throughput, physically valid nodal pressure assignments, and the probability distribution of candidate solutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of QAOA layers ($p$), the pressure discretization step ($\Delta\pi$), and the physical parameters of the gas network (e.g., $P_s=70$ barg).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximations involve discretizing continuous nodal pressures and approximating the non-linear Panhandle-B flow equation using a polynomial fit.

**Figures summary.** The figures illustrate the QAOA circuit diagram and show the comparison between simulated and hardware-derived candidate solutions relative to the continuous classical optimum.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the complex optimization problem, derives the QUBO/cost Hamiltonian, details the QAOA framework, presents simulator results, and concludes with an end-to-end demonstration on the IonQ Forte-1 quantum processor.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Maximizing gas throughput in transmission networks under hydraulic and operational constraints is a combinatorial problem whose complexity grows exponentially with network size, making it computationally intensive to solve exactly. This paper addresses the graph-based optimization problem by optimizing nodal-pressure assignments under the Panhandle-B hydraulic equation. By framing the problem as a search over discretized nodal-pressure assignments coupled with a cost Hamiltonian that encodes both the delivery objective and physical-constraint penalties, we establish a unified formulation suitable for the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA). The mathematical model is adapted to a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) formulation and implemented using the Classiq quantum software platform. In simulator-based experiments, QAOA recovered the maximum-throughput valid operating point, consistent with classical exhaustive evaluation and classical hydraulic simulation reference solutions. A distinctive contribution of this work is the end-to-end execution of a reduced problem instance on the IonQ Forte-1 trapped-ion quantum processor. Remarkably, the hardware implementation used only $p=2$ QAOA layers, substantially fewer than the $p=30$ layers used in the simulator-based study. Despite this significant reduction in circuit depth, the QPU produced physically valid and interpretable candidate solutions that bracketed the continuous classical optimum, with each located within one pressure-discretization step of it. These results demonstrate that meaningful gas-network optimization behavior can be obtained using considerably shallower QAOA circuits than initially expected and provide an end-to-end proof of concept for near-term quantum-assisted gas-network optimization.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the creation of double-membrane resonators within an optical microcavity using 3D direct laser writing. By engineering mechanical mode hybridization, the researchers achieved enhanced collective optomechanical coupling, establishing a scalable platform for studying multimode cavity optomechanics. The combination of advanced nanofabrication and rigorous theoretical modeling makes this system highly relevant for quantum sensing and quantum light-matter interaction studies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly tunable, scalable platform for cavity optomechanics by engineering mechanical coupling, which is crucial for studying collective dynamics and realizing complex quantum light-matter interactions beyond simple two-mode systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to overcome the limitation of optomechanical systems being restricted to only two coupled membranes by establishing a scalable platform for multimode cavity optomechanics.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated double-membrane resonators in a fiber-based microcavity using 3D direct laser writing, achieving enhanced collective optomechanical coupling strengths up to $g_{col}/2\pi \approx 0.1$ MHz.

**Method.** The study combines experimental fabrication using 3D direct laser writing with theoretical modeling using finite-element methods (FEM) and transfer-matrix calculations to predict and analyze coupling strengths.

**Model / system.** The system is a fiber-based Fabry-Perot microcavity containing monolithically integrated double-membrane resonators. The physics involves mechanical mode hybridization induced by physical coupling legs, leading to distinct center-of-mass and breathing normal modes.

**Key observables.** Collective optomechanical coupling strengths ($g_{col}$), mechanical mode splitting ($J_{mech}$), and cavity finesse ($F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Membrane thickness ($t_{mem}$), membrane-to-membrane spacing ($s_{mem}$), and the angle of the connecting support legs ($	heta_{leg}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the transfer-matrix formalism treating the system as a 1D multilayer cavity, and mechanical simulations required correcting the dynamic modulus by a scaling factor (0.88).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the system schematic, SEM images of the fabricated structure, mode diagrams showing hybridized modes, and cross-sectional cuts mapping the wavelength dependence of collective coupling strengths and cavity finesse.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from demonstrating the fabrication of double-membrane resonators via DLW, to modeling the mechanical mode hybridization using FEM, and finally characterizing the enhanced collective optomechanical coupling and its wavelength dependence using transfer-matrix calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optomechanical membrane-in-the-middle systems that comprise several coupled compliant membranes offer enhanced optomechanical coupling and collective dynamics. Recent realizations were fundamentally restricted to two membranes and to limited structural optimization of the involved membranes due to their intricate fabrication and experimental integration. Here, we present a fiber-based Fabry-Perot microcavity incorporating monolithically integrated double-membrane resonators fabricated by 3D direct laser writing. We realize controllable mechanical mode hybridization of membranes with coupling rates of up to $J_{\mathrm{mech}}/2π= 0.16$ MHz, exceeding the mechanical linewidths. We demonstrate enhanced collective optomechanical coupling of the membrane stack's breathing mode, reaching collective coupling strengths of up to $g_{\mathrm{col}}^{(-)}/2π\approx 0.1$ MHz. Our transfer-matrix calculations predict further substantial enhancements with realistic reductions of membrane thickness and spacing, and a larger number of membranes. Our results establish direct laser-written membrane arrays as a scalable platform for multimode cavity optomechanics, combining tunable mechanical interactions, enhanced collective optomechanical coupling, and scalability towards larger mechanically coupled resonator systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an Electron-informed Machine Learning Molecular Dynamics (EMLMD) framework to overcome the computational barriers in simulating light-induced dynamics. By training ML potentials on high-fidelity rt-TDDFT data, the method accurately captures excited-state electron-phonon coupling. This allows researchers to study complex phenomena like photoinduced phase transitions over extended timescales with first-principles accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** The integration of electronic structure information (like $T_e$) directly into the force field via ML is highly relevant for open quantum systems, as it allows the simulation of non-equilibrium energy flow and coupling mechanisms beyond simple thermal assumptions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Direct first-principles simulation of nonadiabatic coupled electron-nuclear dynamics following electronic excitation is computationally prohibitive for studying photoinduced phenomena in materials.

**Main result.** The developed EMLMD framework successfully simulates large-scale, long-time dynamics, revealing that the photoinduced structural transition in bismuth is driven by electronic modulation rather than just lattice heating.

**Method.** The authors propose an Electron-informed Machine Learning Molecular Dynamics (EMLMD) framework, which trains a machine learning potential using high-precision rt-TDDFT data to accurately capture excited-state potential energy surfaces.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on photoexcited materials, demonstrated with Bismuth undergoing a structural phase transition and Selenium undergoing photoamorphization. The model involves coupling electronic structure information (like electron temperature) into the ML potential governing atomic motion.

**Key observables.** Structural order parameter ($\gamma$), electron temperature ($T_e$), lattice temperature ($T_{bath}$), time- and momentum-resolved phonon dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Excitation energy ($\hbar\omega = 1.55$ eV), environment temperatures (50 K, 100 K, 300 K), simulation timescales (picoseconds to nanoseconds).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on physically informed dimensionality reduction to approximate the full electron distribution and uses the frozen-phonon approximation for calculating potential derivatives.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the EMLMD workflow using DFT/rt-TDDFT training data, show the time evolution of the structural order parameter ($\gamma$) under different excitation conditions, and map out environment-temperature-dependent phonon dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the computational bottleneck of nonadiabatic dynamics. It then details the EMLMD framework, contrasting it with TTM and TDBE. The core of the work presents large-scale simulations on Bismuth and Selenium, demonstrating the method's capability to resolve complex, coupled dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonadiabatic coupled electron-nuclear dynamics upon electronic excitation underpin the microscopic mechanism and rational modulation of diverse photoinduced functional phenomena in materials, yet their direct first-principles simulations remain computationally demanding. Here we develop a framework for nonadiabatic excited-state machine-learning molecular dynamics (EMLMD) simulations, where the nonequilibrium electronic information upon photoexcitation such as electron temperature is rigorously calibrated from high-precision real-time time-dependent density functional theory (rt-TDDFT) benchmark simulations, enabling accurate reconstruction of excited-state potential energy surfaces (PES). This framework natively incorporates the excited-state electron-phonon couplings and intrinsically captures photoinduced phonon anharmonicity, both of which are missing in standard machine learning molecular dynamics, thus delivering first-principles-level accuracy for excited-state atomic evolutions. Large-scale EMLMD simulations resolve time- and momentum-resolved phonon dynamics in photoexcited materials, directly uncovering the competition between photogenerated coherent phonons and thermal phonons during photoinduced phase transition of bismuth. It also simultaneously resolves elusive atomic-scale microscopic dynamics and global structural rearrangement for selenium photoamorphization. Balancing high accuracy and efficiency, EMLMD offers a versatile paradigm to tackle key challenges in the study of complex excited-state molecular dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a rigorous framework for analyzing the limitations of low-dimensional models applied to adaptive biological dynamics. It demonstrates that model reduction techniques can lead to qualitatively different inferred system structures, and that certain thermodynamic quantities, like irreversibility, cannot be determined solely from the measured scalar response. The work establishes a clear boundary between model-dependent artifacts and fundamental physical invariants.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework developed for distinguishing between true system invariants and artifacts of model reduction is highly relevant for any field using low-dimensional approximations (like effective Hamiltonians or coarse-grained descriptions) of complex, high-dimensional physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses ambiguities in low-dimensional modeling of adaptive biological dynamics, specifically determining which inferred dynamics are truly determined by measurements versus those introduced by model reduction or partial observation.

**Main result.** The pole classification of a reduced model is not invariant under changes in the observation protocol (e.g., moment matching vs. window fitting). Furthermore, the scalar response spectrum cannot identify the path-space irreversibility rate in the exact-adaptation limit.

**Method.** The authors use pole-zero geometry analysis, stochastic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck theory, and systematic model reduction techniques to analyze the relationship between high-order systems and their low-dimensional approximations.

**Model / system.** The core system is sensory adaptation, modeled using a two-state system involving sensory output, a memory variable, and the stimulus, often analyzed via transfer functions derived from Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes.

**Key observables.** Pole locations (real vs. complex), relative degree, cross-diffusion terms in the spectrum, and the path-space irreversibility rate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of time constants, $ho = 	au_m / 	au_x$, and the memory parameter $\kappa$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing results from different model reduction procedures (e.g., low-frequency moment matching vs. finite-window fitting) and assuming the applicability of specific reduced OU models for deriving bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of pole classification on the reduction method, the frequency response of the system, and the separation between the deterministic response and the hidden cross-diffusion channel.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first demonstrating the non-invariance of pole classification under model reduction, then connecting stochastic identifiability to adaptation by analyzing how cross-diffusion terms drop out of the scalar spectrum under specific memory limits, and finally establishing bounds on irreversibility.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sensory adaptation provides a concrete setting in which low-order system identification can fail qualitatively. We show that one fixed higher-order adaptive system composed entirely of real first-order relaxation modes can be reduced to opposite sides of the second-order pole boundary: low-frequency moment matching gives $ρ_{\rm moment}=4.50$, whereas finite-window fitting gives $ρ_{\rm window}=3.31$, and the inferred pole class changes further with sampling protocol. Thus the real-versus-complex classification of a reduced model is not itself reduction invariant. We then use the general two-state spectrum to connect stochastic identifiability to adaptation: for nontrivial coupling and one-state observation, cross diffusion drops out of the scalar spectrum when the hidden state has no self-relaxation. In the adaptive model, this condition is precisely the integral-memory limit that produces exact adaptation, while leaky memory restores spectral sensitivity. For the exact-adaptation model, the Gaussian path-space irreversibility nevertheless depends on the hidden cross-diffusion channel. Hence $\{H,S_x\}$ does not determine the irreversibility rate. Independently, for a specified all-even reduced two-state drift with $ρ<4$, the drift-only lower bound is $σ\ge τ_x^{-1}(4/ρ-1)$. Published \textit{E.~coli} and \textit{C.~elegans} responses provide biological examples of these limits. The distinction established here between transfer-function invariants, reduction-dependent properties, and hidden-state quantities provides a concrete framework for evaluating the limitations of low-dimensional models of adaptive biological dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes pattern formation in reaction-diffusion systems coupled with chemotaxis. The authors demonstrate that the strength of chemotaxis can lead to nonmonotonic behavior, causing re-entrant transitions between patterned and uniform states. They use linear stability analysis and numerical simulations to map out these complex control regimes and morphological transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical pattern formation, the concept of nonmonotonic control and re-entrant transitions driven by an external coupling (chemotaxis) is relevant to understanding phase transitions in complex quantum or open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how pattern formation in two-species reaction-diffusion systems is controlled by the strength of chemotaxis, specifically looking for nonmonotonic dependencies.

**Main result.** Chemotaxis can induce a striking nonmonotonic and re-entrant dependence of pattern formation on its strength, and it can also drive morphological transitions between spot and stripe patterns.

**Method.** The analysis employs linear stability analysis (LSA) around uniform states to determine the selected wavevector ($k_c$), supplemented by direct numerical simulations (DNS) using pseudo-spectral methods.

**Model / system.** The model is a generic two-species reaction-diffusion system incorporating mutual chemotaxis, exemplified by the chemotactic extension of the Selkov model for glycolysis in two dimensions.

**Key observables.** Pattern selection wavevector ($k_c$), pattern amplitudes ($\Delta\phi, \Delta\psi$), and the dependence of these on chemotaxis couplings ($\xi_1, \xi_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chemotaxis couplings ($\xi_1, \xi_2$), the strength of chemotaxis ($\xi$), and the system size ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linear stability theory and approximations for determining $k_c$; DNS studies are performed for specific parameter sets.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the nonmonotonic dependence of $k_c$ on $\xi$ for different chemotaxis regimes (reciprocal vs. nonreciprocal), and show corresponding variations in pattern amplitudes ($\Sigma\phi, \Sigma\psi$) across various $\xi$ values.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by setting up the reaction-diffusion system, performing LSA to find $k_c$'s dependence on chemotaxis strength, and then confirming these theoretical predictions and observing pattern transitions (spot/stripe) via DNS.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate pattern formation in generic two-species reaction--diffusion systems with chemotaxis. We show that chemotaxis can give rise to a striking nonmonotonic dependence of pattern formation on its strength, opening the possibility of re-entrant transitions between patterned and homogeneous states controlled by chemotaxis. In certain regimes, chemotaxis also induces additional heretofore unexplored instabilities. Furthermore, varying the strength of chemotaxis can drive morphological transitions between spot and stripe patterns.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes that photonic fusion operations not only determine success rates but also dynamically shape the partial distinguishability of the resulting quantum state. By analyzing this transformation, the authors show that protocols optimized for fidelity or success might fail to account for detrimental changes in distinguishability. This mandates that distinguishability propagation be a core metric in designing robust photonic quantum circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative framework for assessing the quality of quantum operations in photonic systems by tracking the evolution of coherence/indistinguishability, which is crucial for scaling up quantum networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates a novel characterization of photonic fusion operations: how they dynamically transform partial distinguishability, moving beyond simple metrics like success probability or resource cost.

**Main result.** Different fusion protocols induce markedly different transformations in partial distinguishability, meaning this property must be considered alongside success probability when evaluating quantum protocols.

**Method.** The authors analyze various fusion protocols using theoretical models (OBB/OBBell) to track the evolution of distinguishability in heralded entangled states.

**Model / system.** The system is photonic quantum information processing, focusing on photonic fusion operations (Bell measurements) applied to states suffering from partial distinguishability due to imperfect photon sources or channels.

**Key observables.** Partial distinguishability, fidelity of the remaining state, average error reduction factor, and success probability.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The distinguishability parameter ($\epsilon$), the fusion protocol used, and the heralding outcome.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis utilizes the Orthogonal Bad Bit (OBB) and Orthogonal Bad Bell (OBBell) models, which simplify the continuous nature of realistic mode mismatch.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Bell-state infidelity ($\epsilon'$) vs. distinguishability ($\epsilon$) for various protocols, and compare success probability with error reduction factors for modified protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of characterizing fusion operations beyond success probability, develops error models (OBB/OBBell), analyzes multiple protocols by tracking distinguishability transformation, and concludes that this dynamic property must be accounted for.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photonic fusion operations are a central primitive in photonic quantum information processing and are commonly characterized by their success probability and resource cost. Here, we identify an additional way in which different implementations of fusion operations differ, namely in how they transform partial distinguishability. We consider distinguishability originating both from imperfect entangled resource states and from imperfect single photons. We find that different successful fusion events can induce markedly different distinguishability transformations depending on the fusion protocol, heralding outcome, and error model. Under a collective resource-state error model, we identify a modification to an existing fusion protocol that improves the fidelity of the remaining state on average. Generally, however, these transformations adversely affect distinguishability, and many successful fusion outcomes are accompanied by distinguishability degradation. Our results show that partial distinguishability is dynamically shaped by interference and measurements and should be considered alongside overall fusion success probability when evaluating photonic fusion protocols.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates 5D CFTs (Free-scalar and Yang-Lee) regularized on the Fuzzy Four Sphere. By employing advanced numerical techniques like DMRG, the authors demonstrate continuous phase transitions and calculate key conformal dimensions. This provides a theoretical framework for understanding critical behavior in complex quantum field theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of numerical methods like DMRG to study critical phenomena and CFTs on non-trivial manifolds like the fuzzy sphere provides advanced techniques applicable to strongly correlated quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper constructs and analyzes 5D Free-scalar and Yang-Lee Conformal Field Theories (CFTs) regularized on the Fuzzy Four Sphere.

**Main result.** The study confirms the existence of continuous phase transitions and conformal towers in these models, yielding scaling dimensions consistent with established CFT results.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced numerical techniques like Exact Diagonalization (ED) and Density Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) to compute energy spectra and scaling dimensions.

**Model / system.** The models are defined on a fuzzy 4-sphere ($S^4$) and include a free-scalar CFT (Ising model) and a Yang-Lee CFT, both governed by specific Hamiltonians derived using $SO(5)$ invariance.

**Key observables.** Scaling dimensions ($\Delta_\phi, \Delta_{\phi^3}$), energy spectra ($E_n - E_0$), and the observation of continuous phase transitions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling constant $h_x$ (for phase transition tuning) and the fermion number $N_e$ (for DMRG calculations).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the fuzzy sphere regularization method and assumes the applicability of CFT correspondence between the low-energy spectrum and the continuum theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagrams for the Yang-Lee model, energy level splittings at critical points, and the cost functions used to match calculated spectra to CFT predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the construction of the 5D CFTs on the fuzzy $S^4$, details the Hamiltonians for the free-scalar and Yang-Lee models, and then applies ED and DMRG to calculate spectra, observe phase transitions, and extract scaling dimensions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-commutative sphere regularization of CFT has become a powerful tool for computing conformal data of 3d CFTs and was recently extended to 4d CFTs. In this work we further construct 5d free-scalar CFT and Yang-Lee CFT on a fuzzy 4-sphere. We observe a continuous phase transition and conformal towers in these examples. We obtain the first three scalar primaries of the 5d free-scalar CFT, furthermore, we compute the 5d Yang-Lee $Δ_φ,Δ_{φ^3}$ with $N_e=10$ fermions using exact diagonalization (ED) and $N_e=20$ fermions using density matrix renormalization group (DMRG), obtaining relatively good agreement with both $ε$-expansion and the Gliozzi truncated bootstrap result.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how the mechanical durability of cohesive granular materials changes when subjected to repeated vibration. By using a mixed-grain system, the authors successfully decoupled the influence of individual bond strength from the load-bearing network structure. They found that the failure lifetime scales exponentially with the cohesive bond fraction, proving that the network connectivity, rather than just the material's stiffness, dictates its resistance to fatigue.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on granular mechanics, the concept of separating intrinsic material properties (bond strength) from emergent structural properties (network connectivity) under external driving (vibration) echoes concepts in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and the study of failure in complex systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to experimentally separate the contributions of individual interparticle bond strength versus the load-bearing network structure (bond number) to the mechanical durability of cohesive granular materials under cyclic loading.

**Main result.** The failure lifetime ($N_f$) exhibits a strong, non-linear dependence on the cohesive bond fraction ($\alpha$), following an activated-like scaling law ($\ln N_f \propto \alpha^2/G$), demonstrating that durability is governed by the network structure even when macroscopic rigidity saturates.

**Method.** The researchers used a mixed granular system subjected to controlled pre-cracking and repeated sinusoidal vibration, measuring the number of cycles until failure ($N_f$).

**Model / system.** The system is a mixed granular material composed of cohesive and noncohesive grains, modeled using a mean-field approach where the cohesive bond fraction ($\alpha$) is the primary control parameter. Failure is analyzed using an activated-like scaling law.

**Key observables.** Failure lifetime ($N_f$), crack width ($w$), Young's modulus, and the scaling relationship $\ln N_f \propto \alpha^2/G$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mixing ratio ($\alpha$), peak acceleration normalized by gravity ($G$), and the pre-crack depth.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a mean-field picture assuming random mixing of grains, and the scaling law itself is a phenomenological description.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the time evolution of crack development under vibration, confirming that crack growth onset shifts with $\alpha$. Crucially, Figure 4 demonstrates the collapse of $N_f$ data onto a single exponential relation when plotted against $\alpha^2/G$, validating the scaling law.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting the difficulty in separating bond strength from network effects. It then details the experimental setup using mixed grains and vibration. The core of the work involves fitting the failure lifetime data to an activated-like scaling law, which successfully collapses the data, leading to the conclusion that bond number controls durability independently of stiffness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cohesive granular materials derive their mechanical stability not only from the strength of individual interparticle bonds but also from the number of bonds forming the load-bearing network. However, these two effects are difficult to separate experimentally because conventional control parameters, such as liquid content, generally alter both simultaneously. Here, we use a mixed granular system composed of cohesive and noncohesive grains to control the cohesive bond number while keeping the bond strength approximately unchanged. We investigate the failure lifetime under repeated vibration and find that the number of cycles to failure, $N_f$, depends strongly on the mixing ratio $α$. The data are well collapsed by $\ln N_f \propto α^2/G$, where $α^2$ represents the fraction of cohesive contacts and $G$ is a peak acceleration normalized by gravitational acceleration. Remarkably, although the Young's modulus is nearly independent of $α$ above the rigidity threshold, the lifetime continues to increase strongly with $α$. This demonstrates that mechanical rigidity and durability against repeated perturbations exhibit distinct dependences on the cohesive network. These results identify bond number as a key control parameter for the durability of cohesive granular materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses Dynamical Mean-Field Theory to examine the nonlinear magnetoelectric effect in correlated altermagnets. The authors demonstrate that the NMEE serves as a robust indicator of the altermagnetic order, vanishing when the system becomes paramagnetic. The results highlight that tuning parameters near the magnetic phase boundary can maximize this measurable response.

**Why it may be interesting.** The self-consistent calculation of a nonlinear response (NMEE) in a correlated magnetic state provides a rich example of how many-body physics dictates measurable optical properties, linking electronic structure to macroscopic response.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the optical nonlinear magnetoelectric effect (NMEE) in a strongly correlated altermagnet.

**Main result.** The NMEE is found to be finite in the altermagnetic phase and vanishes in the paramagnetic phase, establishing it as a sensitive probe of correlated altermagnetic order.

**Method.** The research employs Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT) self-consistently to calculate the NMEE using interacting Green's functions.

**Model / system.** The system is a correlated altermagnet modeled by an interacting lattice Hamiltonian including an onsite Hubbard repulsion term (U). The structure is two-sublattice and spin-dependent.

**Key observables.** Optical Nonlinear Magnetoelectric Effect (NMEE), spin-resolved spectral structure, local magnetization, and the characteristic altermagnetic spin-splitting scale.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength (U/W) and temperature (T/W), particularly near the interaction-driven magnetic phase boundary.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach self-consistently determines the magnetic order, electronic spectrum, and nonlinear response, avoiding limitations of effective band descriptions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral evolution with interaction strength, the NMEE response across different parameter regimes, and the temperature dependence of magnetization and NMEE.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure introducing the model, presenting main results concerning spectral properties, interaction strength dependence, and temperature dependence, concluding with the physical implications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the optical nonlinear magnetoelectric effect (NMEE) in a strongly correlated altermagnet using dynamical mean-field theory. Unlike effective band descriptions with an imposed spin splitting, our approach determines the altermagnetic order, electronic spectrum, and optical nonlinear response self-consistently. We find that the NMEE is finite in the altermagnetic phase and vanishes in the paramagnetic phase. Its frequency dependence reflects the spin-resolved spectral structure and provides an estimate of the characteristic altermagnetic spin-splitting scale. Interaction and temperature tuning produce qualitatively different behavior: at low temperature, reducing the interaction strength toward the interaction-driven magnetic phase boundary enhances the response, whereas increasing the temperature suppresses it and drives it to zero above the critical temperature. These results establish the optical NMEE as a probe of correlated altermagnetic order and suggest that tuning parameters such as pressure, strain, or chemical substitution toward an interaction-driven phase boundary may provide a promising route to maximizing the response.

</details>

**Authors:** Yuhang Xie, Yanjun Chu, Yushan Ding, Shao-Ming Fei  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00534v1>

**Summary.** This paper advances the theory of quantum imaginarity detection by introducing a rigorous enhancement based on the spectral norm of the witness operator. It derives a new detection criterion, $	ext{Tr}[Who] > \| 	ext{Re}(W) \|_\infty$, which significantly improves the ability to identify imaginary quantum states. The authors classify all relevant witness operators based on this spectral norm bound.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a fundamental theoretical tool for quantifying and detecting non-classical resources like imaginarity, which has direct implications for understanding the operational boundaries of quantum information processing beyond standard coherence measures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to improve the detection of quantum imaginarity—a resource quantified by the imaginary component of quantum states—using prior knowledge of spectral norms of witness operators.

**Main result.** The knowledge of the spectral norm, $\| 	ext{Re}(W) \|_\infty$, provides a rigorous and enhanced detection criterion for imaginary states, leading to a classification of all imaginarity-witness operators into four distinct families.

**Method.** The authors derive a new detection criterion by establishing an upper bound on the expectation value of an imaginarity witness over all real states, which is given by the spectral norm of the real part of the witness operator.

**Model / system.** The study concerns quantum states ($ho$) in a $d$-dimensional Hilbert space, distinguishing between real states ($\mathcal{R}$) and imaginary states ($\mathcal{D} \setminus \mathcal{R}$). Detection relies on Hermitian 'imaginarity witnesses' ($W$).

**Key observables.** The expectation value $	ext{Tr}[Who]$, the spectral norm $\| 	ext{Re}(W) \|_\infty$, and the trace $	ext{Tr}[W]$ are key observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral norm $S = \| 	ext{Re}(W) \|_\infty$ acts as a crucial parameter defining the enhanced detection regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the existence of a suitable upper bound $S$ derived from the spectral norm of $	ext{Re}(W)$ to enhance the standard detection criterion $	ext{Tr}[Who] < 0$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between the traditional detection criterion ($	ext{Tr}[Who] < 0$) and the enhanced criterion ($	ext{Tr}[Who] > S$), and map out the relationship between various bounds ($\mu_{\max}$, $\| 	ext{Re}(W) \|_\infty$, $	ext{Tr}[W]$) for different state types.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, derives the spectral norm bound, establishes the enhanced detection criterion, classifies the witnesses into four families, and proves theorems regarding the completeness and finite completeness of the resulting detection scheme.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum imaginarity is an essential physical resource that underpins key functionalities of modern quantum technologies. We improve imaginarity witnessing via the prior knowledge of imaginarity-witness operators. To this end, we derive a rigorous upper bound on the maximum expectation value of an imaginarity witness operator over the set of all free (real) quantum states, which is given by the spectral norm of the real component of the corresponding witness operator. We demonstrate via detailed examples that this bound substantially improves imaginarity detection. We further classify all imaginarity-witness operators into four distinct families based on this bound. For these four witness classes, we perform a comprehensive analysis of their completeness and finite completeness, the joint detection of shared imaginary quantum states by different witnesses, and the conditions for distinct witnesses to identify identical imaginary states. Our results advance the fundamental understanding of imaginarity detection and offer useful insights for both theoretical studies and experimental implementations of quantum imaginarity.

</details>
