**Summary.** This paper proposes a method to achieve highly sensitive, loss-tolerant quantum distributed sensing using continuous-variable entangled states. By incorporating large-gain Optical Parametric Amplification (OPA), the scheme maintains high quantum metrology sensitivity even when subjected to significant channel losses. The results provide quantitative benchmarks showing that this OPA-assisted approach outperforms traditional methods, offering a practical pathway for quantum sensing in noisy environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and quantum information, providing concrete, quantitative guidelines for designing practical quantum sensors that can operate reliably in real-world, lossy fiber optic or integrated photonic environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enhance the sensitivity of quantum distributed sensing in the continuous-variable (CV) regime, ensuring robustness against unavoidable transmission losses and detection inefficiencies.

**Main result.** Introducing large-gain Optical Parametric Amplification (OPA) significantly improves the quantum sensing sensitivity for both two-mode (EPR) and four-mode (cluster) entangled states compared to schemes without OPA.

**Method.** The authors calculate the quantum sensitivity using the error-propagation formula and establish the ultimate bound via the Quantum Cramér-Rao Bound (QCRB) for various entangled states.

**Model / system.** The study models quantum distributed sensing using CV states, specifically analyzing two-mode Einstein–Podolsky–Rosen (EPR) and four-mode cluster states. The physical process involves generating entanglement, transmitting it through a lossy channel, and enhancing the signal via OPA before measurement using balanced homodyne detection.

**Key observables.** Phase estimation sensitivity ($\sigma_{	heta_1}^2$), Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), and the Quantum Cramér-Rao Bound (QCRB).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameters ($r$), OPA gain ($e^{r_3}$), channel efficiencies ($\eta$), and the unknown phases ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the small-phase limit ($	heta \ll 1$) and uses the beam-splitter model to incorporate loss and detection inefficiency.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup for loss-tolerant phase sensing using OPA and compare the average sensitivity ($\sigma_{avg}$) and QCRB for OPA-assisted vs. non-OPA schemes, and for different entangled state configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the problem of loss in CV sensing, then developing the theoretical framework using OPA-assisted entanglement (EPR and cluster states), deriving sensitivity formulas, and finally comparing the performance against single-mode states and calculating the ultimate QCRB bound.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum metrology exploits quantum states to achieve an estimation sensitivity beyond classical limits. In the continuous-variable (CV) regime, the squeezed state has been used to implement deterministic quantum sensing, but the quantum metrology sensitivity of this state is significantly affected by losses or detection inefficiencies, which restrict its applications. In this work, quantum distributed sensing is proposed using optical parametric amplified multimode entanglement generated from squeezed states. It is found that the sensitivity is robust to loss or detection inefficiency when large-gain optical parametric amplification (OPA) is introduced, where a two-mode Einstein--Podolsky--Rosen-entangled state and a four-mode cluster state are exploited for analysis. The quantum sensitivity is greatly improved compared to that without OPA in almost all loss scenarios. The quantum Fisher matrix is calculated for both states to obtain the optimal bound in comparison with our scheme, and it is found that even with a small or moderate OPA gain, quantum sensing can be improved compared with the traditional scheme. The states are also compared with corresponding single-mode squeezed states, finding the parameter ranges where entangled states perform better. This study provides a method for realizing large-scale quantum metrology in real-world applications despite losses or detection inefficiencies.

</details>

### [Quantum time-flip beats adaptive metrology: Asymptotic benefit, activation, and unsimulability](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01175v1)

**Authors:** Gaurang Agrawal, Pritam Halder, Aditi Sen De  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01175v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Indefinite Time-Directed Metrology (ITDM) using quantum time-flip operations to enhance quantum estimation precision. It rigorously proves that these time-flip assisted strategies overcome the asymptotic limitations found in standard adaptive or causal strategies. This work establishes time-reversal symmetry breaking as a genuine, quantifiable resource in quantum metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** It establishes a new resource—indefinite time direction—for quantum metrology, showing how non-causal operations can break fundamental asymptotic limits in precision measurement.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if quantum time-flip (TF)-assisted strategies can overcome the asymptotic equivalence limit in quantum metrology where advantages of adaptive and causal-superposition strategies disappear as the number of channel uses increases.

**Main result.** TF-assisted protocols achieve Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) strictly exceeding conventional strategies, demonstrating 'metrological activation' by achieving Heisenberg scaling where others are restricted to standard scaling.

**Method.** The authors use semidefinite programming (SDP) and the iterative See-saw (ISS) method to rigorously calculate the maximum achievable QFI for various causal strategies.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes quantum metrology involving estimating a parameter $\theta$ using $N$ uses of a parametrized quantum channel $A_\theta$, considering noise models like Depolarizing or Bit-flip noise.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), scaling regimes (Standard vs. Heisenberg).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of channel uses ($N$), noise strength ($p$), and the time-flip assistance itself.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on formalisms like MOP and ISS, which have computational scaling limitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of optimal QFI vs. noise parameters for different strategies, and plots confirm the transition from standard to Heisenberg scaling using the $\gamma_2$ coefficient.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops formalisms (MOP, ISS) to calculate QFI under various causal constraints (Parallel, Adaptive, Causal Superposition) and then introduces time-flip assistance to show a quantifiable, non-asymptotically vanishing advantage.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum metrology, adaptive and causal-superposition strategies are proven to be beneficial over parallel schemes for a finite number of channel uses, but their advantages disappear in the asymptotic limit. We show that quantum operations with indefinite time direction, specifically, time-flip (TF)-assisted strategies, referred to as indefinite time-directed metrology (ITDM), can overcome this asymptotic equivalence. Using semidefinite programming, we rigorously demonstrate that TF-assisted protocols can achieve quantum Fisher information (QFI) strictly exceeding the maximum value attainable by parallel, adaptive, and causal-superposition strategies, both for finite and asymptotically many channel uses. Moreover, we identify a class of Pauli noise channels for which ITDM achieves Heisenberg scaling, while all parallel, adaptive, and causal-superposition strategies remain restricted to standard scaling. We call this phenomenon as metrological activation. Interestingly, this activation can be used to exhibit that the quantum time-flip and transposition supermaps cannot be simulated by conventional quantum circuits or causal-superposition strategies using any finite number of channel queries, thereby establishing indefinite time direction as a genuine resource for quantum metrology.

</details>

**Authors:** Vivek Dixit, Vaibhaw Kumar, Kentaro Ohno, Alberto Maldonado Romo, Larry Bowden  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00881v1>

Transportation contracts form a compatibility graph G, whose complement ¯G is solved as a Maximum Independent Set (MIS) instance. The hybrid solver then alternates between deterministic classical reduction, which fixes provably-optimal vertices, and hardware-aware QAOA, whose measured marginal probabilities pi rank the remaining vertices; the highest-scoring vertex is added to the independent set and its closed neigh- borhood N[v] is removed. The reduced graph re-enters the loop until no vertices remain, at which point the accumulated set Stotal, i.e., the constructed set of mutually compatible contracts, is returned.</sub>

At a small budget (center) some interactions exceed the budget and the represented graph is disconnected; increasing kswap (right) yields a single connected component, and the smallest such budget is retained. Solid arcs denote interactions realizable within the budget; dashed arcs denote interactions beyond it. Among the seeds that achieve connectivity, the embedding with the smallest two-qubit...</sub>

</details>

The non-uniform distribution of marginals provides the vertex- selection information exploited by the proposed reduction strategy.</sub>

**Summary.** This work proposes a quantum-classical hybrid solver to tackle the computationally hard Maximum Independent Set (MIS) problem, using natural gas contract selection as an industrial motivation. The method iteratively simplifies the problem using classical graph theory before applying QAOA on the remaining kernel using current quantum hardware. It demonstrates that this hybrid approach effectively handles large, real-world graph structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology of combining deterministic classical graph reduction with quantum optimization (QAOA) to tackle large, structured optimization problems is highly relevant for developing scalable quantum algorithms for complex physical constraints.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the NP-complete problem of finding the Maximum Independent Set (MIS) on a graph, framed initially by selecting compatible natural gas transportation contracts.

**Main result.** The developed quantum-classical hybrid solver achieved strong performance on benchmark instances, reporting an average approximation ratio of 0.996 on QOBLIB and successfully reducing industrially motivated graphs.

**Method.** It employs a hybrid quantum-classical framework that combines iterative classical graph reduction techniques with quantum optimization using the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA).

**Model / system.** The problem is formulated as an MIS problem, which is mapped to a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) model and subsequently to an Ising Hamiltonian for execution on NISQ quantum hardware.

**Key observables.** Average approximation ratio (e.g., 0.996), ability to recover optimal solutions, and circuit metrics like two-qubit depth and CZ count.

**Important parameters / regimes.** NISQ hardware constraints, graph sizes up to 900 contracts, and QAOA parameters ($p$, $k_{swap}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum workload is assumed to scale with qubit count rather than problem size, and the classical reduction process is guaranteed to preserve at least one maximum independent set.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall hybrid quantum-classical workflow, showing the alternation between classical reduction and hardware-aware QAOA execution.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the formulation of the MIS problem as a QUBO, describes the hybrid solver pipeline involving classical reduction rules and quantum optimization via QAOA, presents results on standard benchmarks (QOBLIB), and discusses hardware-aware circuit synthesis for superconducting processors.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Selecting mutually compatible natural gas transportation contracts is a practically important optimization task in which operators must choose from many candidate agreements subject to temporal, infrastructural, and flow-related constraints. As the number of candidates grows, the resulting search space becomes difficult to explore exhaustively. We study a pairwise abstraction of this task, formulated as a Maximum Clique problem on a contract-compatibility graph, or equivalently as a Maximum Independent Set (MIS) problem on the complement graph.   Building on recent work, this paper studies a quantum-classical framework for solving large-scale MIS instances within the limitations of noisy quantum hardware. The approach combines iterative classical graph reduction with quantum-guided optimization to progressively simplify the search space while maintaining high solution quality. This enables large candidate spaces to be reduced to smaller subproblems that are more suitable for execution on current quantum computers.   We evaluate the approach on fifteen benchmark instances from the Quantum Optimization Benchmarking Library (QOBLIB), obtaining an average approximation ratio of 0.996 and recovering optimal solutions for fourteen instances, including graphs with up to 186 vertices. We further evaluate the algorithm on six synthetic pairwise contract-compatibility graphs containing up to 900 contracts, where the proposed method achieves an average approximation ratio of 0.989 and obtains optimal solutions in four cases. These experiments demonstrate the ability of the hybrid MIS solver to reduce industrially motivated graphs. The pairwise abstraction serves as the first step of a two-stage screening procedure that narrows the candidate contracts to a smaller set of mutually compatible ones, which can then be verified against pipeline-capacity constraints.

</details>

### [Towards unsupervised representation learning for quantum data: quantum models with inference and generation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00372v1)

**Authors:** Robin Lorenz, Eric Brunner, Marcello Benedetti  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00372v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **2/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.00372_figures/2609.00372_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 4: Schematic Venn diagram of how the sets CX-PPT, PPT and DX-PPT sit within the set of all states S(X ⊗Y); the question marks indicate which intersections are not known to definitely be (non-)empty; ‘classical’ denotes the subset of separable states that can be written as a convex combination of pairwise orthogonal pure product states.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.00372_figures/2609.00372_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 4 visualises the hierarchy of classes of states described above, including the open ques- tions concerning which intersections are (non-)empty. It seems interesting that LS-ambiguous states lie—in terms of the cardinality of the sets and the degree of entanglement—in between those based on OP and JP, both of which do not yield non-trivial models. With a view to quantum representation learning, it is natural to see the size of sets of ambiguous states and the correlations between X and Y they can describe, in particular the degree of entanglement they cover, as kinds of inductive biases one may choose accordingly, given a problem that quantum representation learning is to be applied...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a rigorous mathematical framework for unsupervised representation learning when the data consists of quantum states rather than classical measurements. It addresses the fundamental challenge posed by the lack of a standard factorization rule for quantum states. The work characterizes the resulting 'ambiguous states' and shows that physically realizable quantum correlations are constrained by entanglement criteria like the PPT condition.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical structure for applying modern ML techniques (like variational inference) to quantum data, which is crucial for developing quantum machine learning algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a mathematical framework to generalize unsupervised representation learning, concepts central to modern machine learning, to the quantum domain.

**Main result.** It characterizes the necessary structure of quantum models with inference and generation, showing that non-trivial models must involve non-linear maps, and links the existence of such models to the Positive Partial Transpose (PPT) criterion.

**Method.** The authors define quantum models using joint quantum states over visible and latent systems and employ State-over-Time (SOT) maps to generalize the factorization properties of classical probability distributions.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum states (density operators) defined over visible and latent subsystems, which are assumed to be finite-dimensional. The core mathematical tools are SOT maps and the concept of 'ambiguous states' arising from factorization constraints.

**Key observables.** Ambiguous states, joint quantum states, and the positive-partial-transpose (PPT) criterion.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the SOT maps (e.g., Leifer-Spekkens construction) and the resulting correlation type (e.g., PPT states).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the existence of a suitable notion of factorisation (via SOT maps) to mimic the classical chain rule, and later proves that non-trivial models require non-linear maps.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the generic setup of classical representation learning, showing the relationship between inference, generation, and the latent/visible spaces.

**Paper structure.** The paper first contrasts the quantum challenge with classical ML, then develops the SOT map framework, analyzes specific SOT map types (Leifer-Spekkens, etc.), establishes a 'no-go' result requiring non-linearity, and finally characterizes ambiguous states in relation to PPT criteria.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

With quantum sensors, simulators and networks emerging, a future of quantum technology may produce quantum states as data---that is, coherently rather than as classical measurement records---thus motivating the study of suitable quantum generalisations of modern machine learning, including the automated, unsupervised extraction of useful representations. Two ingredients are central to the latter: inference, mapping observations to latent representations, and generation, mapping latent states back to synthetic data. Both are related to each other and to joint distributions for training models by the chain-rule of classical probability theory. The fact that quantum states however lack such universal, standard factorisation property thus poses a challenge. Here we develop a conceptual and mathematical framework for unsupervised representation learning from quantum data. Models are joint quantum states over visible and latent systems; state-over-time maps provide a notion of factorisation into a marginal state and inference (generation) channel; models with inference (generation) are ambiguous states---states for which such factorisation obtains---subject to a further consistency condition on extended inference maps as data extension. These stipulations are restrictive: we show that non-trivial models must feature non-linear such maps to the extended space. For three representative state-over-time maps, we completely characterise the ambiguous states, uncovering a hierarchy tied to the positive-partial-transpose (PPT) criterion from entanglement theory. Notably, the Leifer-Spekkens construction supports inference and generation exactly for model classes of PPT states, thus allowing genuinely quantum visible-latent correlations. We also formulate quantum counterparts of exact and approximate inference training, explore weaker notions of data extension and sketch a future research programme.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an optimal control strategy to maximize charge transfer in diffusion-limited electrochemical systems. By employing optimal control theory, the authors prove that the best strategy is a 'bang-ride' current profile, which significantly outperforms constant-current operation. This provides fundamental, geometry-independent upper bounds on achievable performance gains in energy storage devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on electrochemistry, the mathematical framework of optimal control theory applied to diffusion-limited processes shares conceptual links with open quantum systems where external driving fields or dissipation rates must be optimized to maximize coherence or transfer.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to optimize material transport, specifically charge transfer in electrochemical systems, when the process is limited by the rate at which reactants can diffuse to the boundary.

**Main result.** The optimal control protocol follows a 'bang-ride' structure, achieving a system-independent upper bound capacity gain of approximately 27% over the best constant-current operation.

**Method.** Optimal control theory is applied to the diffusion equation, treating the current as the control variable to maximize transferred charge subject to a minimum boundary concentration constraint.

**Model / system.** The model is based on the diffusion equation governing concentration $c(x, t)$ in semi-infinite or finite domains, applied to electrochemical systems like Li-ion batteries or NaK anode stripping.

**Key observables.** Boundary concentration $c(0, t)$, Areal Capacity $Q$, and the optimal current profile $j(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical boundary concentration threshold ($\chi c_0$), diffusion coefficient $D$, and the total time $T$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system is governed purely by diffusion limitation, neglecting kinetic, thermal, or degradation effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the optimal 'bang-ride' current profile and the corresponding boundary concentration evolution for various electrochemical setups (e.g., Li electrodeposition).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical framework using the diffusion equation, derives the optimal control problem, and solves it analytically for semi-infinite domains, extending the results to finite and multilayer systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Transport in certain systems fails when diffusion cannot replenish or remove material from a boundary rapidly enough, causing the boundary concentration to reach a critical minimum or maximum. Motivated by experiments in electrochemical systems that demonstrate this diffusion-limited failure of charge transport, we determine dynamic current protocols that maximize charge transfer subject to a prescribed concentration constraint. The optimal protocol has a "bang-ride" structure: the maximum feasible current is used until the boundary concentration reaches its critical value, after which the current is progressively reduced to maintain that value. For semi-infinite domains, we derive the optimal currents analytically and obtain system-independent upper bounds on their improvement over constant-current operation. We also extend the framework to finite and multilayer domains and show that the same formulation applies to both charging and discharging.

</details>

**Authors:** M. Pszota, P. Ván  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00317v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper reformulates higher-gradient gravity by deriving its field equations from the Second Law of Thermodynamics applied to a self-gravitating fluid. This thermodynamic approach successfully bypasses the Ostrogradsky instability inherent in standard Lagrangian formulations. The resulting theory is ghost-free and provides novel physical identities, linking gravity to fundamental thermodynamic principles.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of entropy principles and gradient flow dynamics to stabilize a classical field theory is a powerful analogy to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, which is central to understanding open quantum systems and dissipation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Higher-gradient modifications of Newtonian gravity, while removing point-mass singularities, suffer from the Ostrogradsky instability when treated via standard Lagrangian dynamics.

**Main result.** The field equations can be derived from the Second Law of Thermodynamics applied to a self-gravitating fluid, yielding a ghost-free, first-order relaxation equation that naturally incorporates Newton's law and dissipation-free couplings.

**Method.** The authors derive the field equations by treating the potential as a thermodynamic variable and enforcing the local entropy balance ($\sigma_s \ge 0$), leading to a gradient flow description.

**Model / system.** The model is a modification of Newtonian gravity involving higher-gradient terms, analyzed through the lens of non-equilibrium thermodynamics applied to a self-gravitating fluid.

**Key observables.** The energy current, pressure tensor, and the stability condition derived from the entropy density (concavity).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The amplitude sum rule $\sum \alpha_i = -1$ constrains the internal length scale below $4 	imes 10^{-5}$ m.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes the system is in mechanical equilibrium ($\Pi = 0$) for simplified results, and the dynamics are governed by a first-order relaxation equation rather than a standard action principle.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the instability issue in higher-gradient gravity. It then reformulates the dynamics using the Second Law of Thermodynamics, showing the resulting equations are equivalent to the stationary limit of a first-order relaxation equation. Finally, it analyzes the constraints imposed by this thermodynamic structure on the theory's parameters.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Higher-gradient modifications of Newtonian gravity remove the point-mass singularity, but their Lagrangian dynamics is haunted by the Ostrogradsky instability. We show that the same field equations follow from the Second Law of thermodynamics applied to a self-gravitating fluid with a weakly nonlocal state space, and that on this route the instability is absent by construction: the potential obeys a first-order relaxation equation that is a gradient flow, whose Lyapunov function is the entropy density, and whose relaxation spectrum is negative definite at every wavenumber precisely when the entropy is concave. The derivation also yields the pressure tensor and the energy current, an exact identity that reproduces Newton's force law without further input, and a dissipation-free cross-coupling to the bulk viscous pressure that no variational principle can produce. Regularity constrains the Yukawa-type corrections by an amplitude sum rule, $\sum_iα_i=-1$, so that laboratory tests of the inverse-square law bound the largest internal length below $4\times10^{-5}$~m, and identify the complex-root regime as a classically consistent Lee--Wick-like sector.

</details>

The low-temperature trajectory shows slowly varying phase textures, whereas the high-temperature trajectory exhibits stronger phase fluctuations. This supports the interpretation that the dominant change across the transition is associated with phase fluctuations rather than large-scale amplitude ordering.</sub>

Both quantities show weak size dependence in the low-temperature algebraic regime. The size dependence becomes pronounced near the transition region.</sub>

<img src="2609.00721_figures/2609.00721_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 4: Helicity-modulus-like quantity obtained from the ordinary linear-response formula in Eq. (91). This quantity is defined by applying a Peierls-type twist to the real-space nonlocal representation of the fractional quadratic energy, thereby assuming an analytic δ2 response. In the low-temperature regime, the response grows strongly with system size and does not approach a finite size-independent value. This shows that the ordinary helicity modulus is not an appropriate finite stiffness for the σ = 1 fractional model.</sub>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2609.00721_figures/2609.00721_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>

Although Ycusp remains finite at each finite system size, it decreases with increasing L in the low-temperature regime. Its temperature and size dependence closely resemble those of ⟨|m|2⟩. Therefore, Ycusp does not behave as an independent stiffness analogous to the helicity modulus of the two-dimensional XY model.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a 1D fractional U(1) Ginzburg-Landau model to study its phase transition behavior. Using stochastic dynamics, the authors show that while the correlation scaling mimics the BKT transition, the system lacks the characteristic jump in the helicity modulus seen in 2D systems. This highlights how long-range interactions fundamentally alter the nature of phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a theoretical framework for understanding how long-range interactions (fractional Laplacian) modify classic critical phenomena like the BKT transition, offering insights applicable to systems with non-local dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the nature of phase transitions, specifically BKT-like correlation scaling, in a one-dimensional fractional U(1) Ginzburg-Landau model.

**Main result.** The transition exhibits BKT-like behavior in correlation scaling, evidenced by finite-size scaling collapse, but it notably lacks the universal jump in the helicity modulus characteristic of the 2D XY model.

**Method.** The authors employ stochastic Gross-Pitaevskii dynamics to sample the equilibrium Gibbs measure and analyze various correlation functions and twist responses.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional complex field model governed by a fractional U(1) Ginzburg-Landau Hamiltonian, featuring a nonlocal quadratic term with Fourier multiplier |k|^sigma, focusing on the marginal case sigma=1.

**Key observables.** Correlation function C(r), Binder ratios, effective exponents, and spectral twist responses.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical exponent sigma, the temperature T, and the system size L, with a focus on the marginal case sigma=1.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on defining a spectral twist response due to the nonlocal operator, and it contrasts its findings with the known universal jump of the 2D XY model.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the analysis involves showing size-independent ratios at low T and collapse using a BKT-type variable.

**Paper structure.** The paper defines the model and observables, describes the stochastic dynamics used for sampling, presents numerical results analyzing scaling, and concludes by interpreting the findings regarding the nature of the transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a one-dimensional fractional $U(1)$ Ginzburg--Landau model whose quadratic part has Fourier multiplier $|k|^σ$, focusing on the marginal case $σ=1$. This dispersion yields logarithmic spin-wave fluctuations, suggesting BKT-like behavior despite the one-dimensional setting. We sample the equilibrium Gibbs measure using stochastic Gross--Pitaevskii dynamics and analyze correlation functions, dimensionless ratios, effective exponents, and twist responses. The correlation function shows a low-temperature algebraic branch with a temperature-dependent exponent, while the high-temperature regime exhibits a nonlocal-kernel-induced tail consistent with $C(r)\sim r^{-2}$. The correlation and Binder ratios are nearly size independent at low temperature and collapse with the BKT-type variable $(T-T_{\rm BKT})(\log L)^2$; finite-size effects set in around $T\simeq0.35\text{--}0.4$, consistent with $T_{\rm BKT}\simeq0.35$. Unlike the two-dimensional XY model, twist responses do not yield a finite helicity modulus: the ordinary linear-response quantity grows with system size, whereas the cusp twist response scales as $L^{-η(T)}$, like the squared zero-mode order parameter. Thus, the transition is BKT-like in correlation scaling, but lacks a universal helicity-modulus jump.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper systematically analyzes the entanglement generated by coherently controlling quantum processes, specifically the quantum switch and the time-flip. By treating the control and target as a bipartite system, the authors derive conditions under which entanglement is generated, even when dissipation is present. The findings offer a deep, analytical understanding of how channel order and directionality can be harnessed to manipulate quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, analytical framework for quantifying how entanglement can be engineered by controlling the order or direction of quantum operations, which is fundamental to quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to analytically characterize the entanglement generation capability of two key coherently controlled quantum processes: the quantum switch and the quantum time-flip.

**Main result.** The entanglement generated depends critically on the coherence of the control process and the distinguishability of the ordered evolutions; maximal entanglement can persist even in the presence of dissipation.

**Method.** The analysis involves treating the control and target as a bipartite system, deriving exact expressions for entanglement (concurrence) for unitary channels, and extending the analysis to various dissipative channels.

**Model / system.** The model uses a bipartite system consisting of a control qubit and a target system, where processes are modeled by quantum channels (supermaps). The processes analyzed are the quantum switch (combining two channels) and the quantum time-flip (reversing input-output direction).

**Key observables.** Entanglement (quantified by concurrence), coherence-entanglement conservation relation, and stationary states of damping channels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The distinguishability overlap parameter $s$, and the parameters governing the specific damping channels ($\gamma_1, \gamma_2, p_1, p_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes pure product inputs and crucially retains the control system rather than measuring or discarding it.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the quantum switch first, deriving general principles and specific results for unitaries and dissipative channels, followed by an analogous study for the quantum time-flip.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analytically characterize entanglement generation by two paradigmatic coherently controlled quantum processes, the quantum switch and time-flip. Retaining rather than measuring or discarding the control, we treat the control and target as the bipartite system and assume pure product inputs, so that any output entanglement reflects the entangling capability of the process. For the switch of qubit unitaries, we derive exact expressions for entanglement, together with a geometric characterization and a coherence-entanglement conservation relation. We then extend our analysis to binary random unitary, Pauli, amplitude damping, and generalized amplitude damping channels. We prove that switch of channels commuting under composition cannot entangle a separable input. Yet maximal entanglement is possible even with dissipation, e.g., an entanglement-breaking damping channel switched with a bit-flip can transform a product input into a maximally entangled state. For two generalized amplitude damping channels, a common stationary state prevents entanglement, while different stationary populations can enable it. We present an analogous study for the time-flip of unitary and binary random unitary channels. Our findings provide a systematic analysis of how much entanglement can be generated by coherent control of channel order or input-output direction.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a novel, noninvasive technique using superconducting microwave resonators to measure the electrodynamic response of fragile kagome superconductors. By analyzing the temperature-dependent shift in the resonance frequency, the authors successfully extracted the superfluid density, finding linear low-temperature behavior indicative of a nodal superconducting gap.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of microwave circuits to probe superconducting properties provides an excellent analogy for studying dissipation and gap structure in mesoscopic quantum systems, linking superconducting physics to circuit QED concepts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to determine the superconducting pairing symmetry (gap structure) of unconventional superconductors, specifically in kagome superconductors, which is crucial for understanding their microscopic pairing mechanism.

**Main result.** The superfluid density exhibits linear temperature dependence at low temperatures, which is a strong indicator of a nodal gap structure in the superconductor.

**Method.** The study uses superconducting microwave resonators to noninvasively probe the electrodynamic response of fragile, thin superconducting flakes integrated into hybrid microwave circuits.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a layered van der Waals kagome superconductor ($	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$) flake coupled into a microwave resonator. The analysis models the resonance frequency shift based on the flake's kinetic inductance, which is proportional to the superfluid density ($n_s$).

**Key observables.** Temperature-dependent resonance frequency shifts ($\Delta f/f_0$), superfluid density ($n_s(T)$), and the quality factor ($Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low-temperature linear dependence of $n_s(T)$, and the ability to suppress parasitic losses from two-level systems (TLS).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the frequency shift is dominated by the intrinsic superfluid response, and that parasitic TLS losses can be modeled and subtracted.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hybrid microwave resonator device architecture, the crystal structure of $	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$, and the temperature dependence of resistance and frequency shifts.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the difficulty of probing gap structure in 2D materials to introducing the microwave resonator platform. It details the noninvasive coupling technique, analyzes the frequency shift by separating quasiparticle and TLS contributions, and concludes by extracting the low-temperature $n_s(T)$ to infer the nodal gap structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unconventional superconductivity is a hallmark of exotic quantum matter, where determining the pairing symmetry is essential for uncovering its microscopic origin. Kagome superconductors provide a fertile landscape for emergent phenomena arising from strong electronic correlations and nontrivial band topology, yet their superconducting pairing symmetry remains elusive. The superconducting gap structure is commonly probed via electrodynamic response, but such measurements are inapplicable to thin flakes due to their small volume and delicate nature. Superconducting microwave resonators offer a coherent and highly sensitive platform for probing electrodynamic responses, with versatile designs that enable incorporation of diverse materials and geometries. Here, we integrate flakes into microwave circuits, enabling noninvasive access to the superfluid response through contactless coupling that preserves structural integrity. By engineering the device geometry to suppress parasitic two-level-system losses that dominate dissipation in microwave circuits, we isolate the intrinsic material response. Remarkably, the temperature-dependent superfluid density exhibits linear behavior at low temperatures, consistent with a nodal gap structure. Our approach establishes a noninvasive platform for probing electrodynamic response of fragile superconducting flakes while advancing hybrid microwave architectures for quantum technologies.

</details>

**Authors:** Idriss Hank Nkouatchoua Ngueya, Marcin Łobejko  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00321v1>

**Summary.** This paper develops a general theory to compare the performance of various quantum Otto engines by factoring the power into a work term and an implementation-specific time scale. It demonstrates that the maximum operating speed is fundamentally limited by the thermalization times of the hot and cold baths, regardless of the specific cycle used.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general framework for comparing non-equilibrium quantum processes, showing how fundamental thermodynamic limits constrain the speed achievable by different physical implementations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to systematically compare the operational speed (power output) of different quantum realizations of the Otto engine, despite them achieving the same thermodynamic efficiency.

**Main result.** The power of any quantum Otto engine can be factorized into a characteristic work (dependent on internal frequencies) and an implementation-specific characteristic time. The maximum speed is universally limited by the sum of the hot and cold bath thermalization times.

**Method.** The analysis uses a factorization approach ($P = W/T^*$) to separate thermodynamic and dynamical aspects, comparing four distinct dynamical realizations (e.g., four-stroke, always-on, Floquet-modulated).

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum heat engine, modeled using qubits coupled to thermal baths, analyzed via master equations and Hamiltonian dynamics for various cycle implementations.

**Key observables.** Power output ($P$), characteristic work ($W$), characteristic time ($T^*$), and efficiency ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strengths ($	au, \Omega$), internal frequencies ($\omega$), and dimensionless ratios characterizing the cycle dynamics ($s_{LT}, s_F, s_\pi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is the factorization $P = W/T^*$, allowing the comparison to focus solely on the characteristic time $T^*$. Various approximations are used depending on the cycle (e.g., slow modulation, fast oscillation limit).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the four-stroke model and the Floquet-inspired model for a single qubit, and the two-qubit realization, comparing power scaling in different regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general framework by factorizing power, then analyzes specific dynamical realizations (four-stroke, Floquet, always-on, on-off) by deriving their respective characteristic times and asymptotic scaling laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A general thermal machine is characterized by distinct time or energy scales: temperature, coupling to the heat baths, internal free dynamics and interactions, and external driving. We address the question: what parameters determine the speed of a given engine, and how can they be used to reliably compare different types of engine? Specifically, we consider four realizations of the Otto engine, each operating with the same Otto efficiency, and aim to characterize and compare their power outputs. The main insight of this paper is that, irrespective of their very different dynamical implementations, the power of each engine can be factorized into a common characteristic work and an implementation-specific characteristic time. The characteristic work depends only on the internal frequencies of the machine and the temperatures, whereas the characteristic time depends on the coupling strength to the baths and on the implementation-specific driving. This observation allows us to compare the power of different engines through their characteristic times and thereby reduce the relevant parameter space to parameters that capture the dynamical aspects of engine performance. Despite different dynamical implementations, this framework reveals simple common features. In all cases, the maximum operating speed is limited by a common timescale given by the sum of the characteristic thermalization times of the hot and cold baths. Moreover, the four engines fall into two distinct asymptotic classes: implementations in which driving and thermalization occur simultaneously exhibit quadratic scaling, whereas those in which work extraction and thermalization alternate exhibit linear scaling. These results expose general dynamical features that determine the operational speed of quantum Otto engines.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves a general theorem connecting two distinct ways collective behavior can emerge in 1D spin chains: spontaneously via competing interactions, and externally via a field. It establishes an exact, Hamiltonian-level mapping between complex range-two interactions and simpler range-one interactions for both continuous and discrete spin systems. The work also frames this discovery within a novel model of human-AI scientific collaboration.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical mapping provides a powerful tool for solving complex 1D lattice models, which is foundational knowledge for understanding quantum many-body systems and their classical limits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to connect two fundamental mechanisms of collective behavior in statistical physics: those arising spontaneously from competing interactions (zero field) and those controlled by an external field.

**Main result.** A theorem is proven establishing a microscopic, temperature-independent mapping between a range-two Hamiltonian (with nearest and next-nearest neighbor interactions) and a simpler range-one Hamiltonian (with nearest-neighbor and single-spin potential).

**Method.** The mapping is established using exact mathematical techniques, including a recursive Householder moving frame construction for continuous spins and kinematic identities for the Potts model.

**Model / system.** The models are one-dimensional open chains of classical spins, including $O(n)$-vector spins (Ising, XY, Heisenberg) and the $q$-state Potts model. The Hamiltonians involve arbitrary inhomogeneous nearest-neighbor and next-nearest-neighbor interactions.

**Key observables.** The partition function ($Z$) and the equivalence between the two Hamiltonian forms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality $n$ (for $O(n)$ spins), the number of states $q$ (for Potts spins), and the system size $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mapping is shown to hold for arbitrary inhomogeneous interactions and finite system sizes, overcoming previous limitations in the thermodynamic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the research trajectory, showing the progression from an initial Active Hypothesis Space (AHS) to a broader space driven by Human-AI collaboration.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by first defining the two Hamiltonian forms, then proving the exact mapping for both continuous $O(n)$ spins and discrete $q$-state Potts spins, and finally framing the discovery process within a human-AI collaboration model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can an artificial intelligence (AI) generate a scientific hypothesis outside a human collaborator's active hypothesis space (AHS), and can human-AI research be organized to make such breakthroughs more likely? We document such a case while proving a theorem that connects two basic organizing mechanisms of statistical physics: collective behavior arising in zero field from competing interactions and that induced or controlled by an external field. A zero-field $O(n)$-vector open chain with arbitrary inhomogeneous nearest- and next-nearest-neighbor interaction functions $U_i(S_i\cdot{S}_{i+1})$ and $V_i(S_i\cdot{S}_{i+2})$ is microscopically, via a temperature-independent mapping at the Hamiltonian level, equivalent to a simpler $O(n)$ open chain with nearest-neighbor interaction $V_i( σ_i\cdot σ_{i+1})$ and axial single-spin potential $U_i(σ_i^z)$ for every integer $n\ge1$ and every system size $L\ge1$. The homogeneous linear specialization maps the foundational frustrated $J_1$-$J_2$ model onto the canonical $J$-$h$ field model---with $n=1,2,3$ being the Ising, XY, and Heisenberg classical spin models, respectively. An analogous theorem holds when the continuous $O(n)$ spins are replaced by the $q$-state Potts spins with the standard Potts interaction, implying a closed-form exact solution of the $J_1$-$J_2$ Potts open chain for every $q\ge2$ and every $L\ge1$. The emergence of the theorems from sustained human-AI collaboration suggests that involving AI throughout a systematic research program may incubate autonomous scientific breakthroughs.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01278v1>

**Summary.** This work solves the difficult inverse problem in Optimal Transport by proposing an exact, non-iterative method based on a double-centering operation. This technique uniquely reconstructs the true cost matrix from an observed transport plan, bypassing the need to solve the forward OT problem iteratively. It provides a powerful, general tool for inferring underlying physical costs from empirical transport data.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the topic is mathematical statistics/optimization, the underlying structure of OT is deeply connected to quantum information geometry and quantum state estimation, making the rigorous mathematical tools relevant to quantum physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the inverse problem of Optimal Transport (OT): recovering the underlying cost matrix given an observed optimal transport plan. This is challenging due to inherent gauge freedom in the cost structure.

**Main result.** The authors provide an exact, closed-form solution using a single double-centering operation to uniquely fix the gauge freedom and recover the cost matrix without iterative optimization. This method is general and extends beyond entropic regularization.

**Method.** The core method involves applying a double-centering operation to the observed plan (or its transform) to remove additive row and column gauge contributions, yielding a canonical cost matrix representation.

**Model / system.** The framework is based on Optimal Transport (OT) and its regularized version, Entropic Optimal Transport (EOT). The problem is framed as recovering the cost matrix $C$ from the transport plan $W$ that minimizes the regularized energy.

**Key observables.** The recovered cost matrix ($ar{C}'$), the temperature parameter ($\epsilon$), and diagnostics like the Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) are key observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regularization temperature $\epsilon$, the size of the marginals ($N, M$), and the noise level ($\sigma$) are important parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the existence of an invertible relation between the cost and the plan, and it is robust to multiplicative noise in the observed plan $W$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the double-centering operation on distance matrices and compare reconstruction errors based on the number of independent cycles in the observed data graph.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the inverse OT problem and the gauge freedom. It then introduces the double-centering technique as the exact solution. Subsequent sections detail its application to estimate parameters like $\epsilon$ and analyze reconstruction accuracy under partial information and noise.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optimal transport describes the most efficient way to move mass between two distributions, given a cost matrix for moving mass between each pair of locations. Entropic optimal transport, solved via the Sinkhorn algorithm, is a widely used regularized version of this problem. Its inverse problem asks the opposite question: given an observed transport plan, what cost matrix produced it? This is difficult because the cost is identifiable only up to an additive gauge freedom. Here we show that this freedom can be fixed exactly by a single double-centering operation applied to the observed plan, yielding the true cost matrix in closed form, with no iterative optimization required. When a modest number of true cost entries are known, the same approach lets us jointly estimate the temperature parameter controlling the entropic regularization, together with a diagnostic for the reliability of this estimate. We further show that the method is not specific to the entropic optimal transport, but extends to a broader class of transport models defined by an invertible relation between cost and plan.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes that the dispersion of zeros in the Green's function serves as a key diagnostic for understanding competing ordering tendencies in correlated insulators. By studying the extended Hubbard model, the authors show that the zero dispersion is governed by distinct mechanisms—spin correlations in the Mott phase and defect kinematics in the CDW phase—and that this change is discontinuous at the transition point.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of using spectral zeros, rather than quasiparticle poles, to characterize insulating states is a powerful diagnostic tool for strongly correlated systems, providing insight into competing orders.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to elucidate the physical meaning of the zeros in the Green's function for correlated insulators, specifically how the dispersion of these zeros encodes the physics of competing ordering tendencies (Mott vs. CDW).

**Main result.** The dispersion of the Green's function zeros is determined by both microscopic spin-spin correlations and defect kinematics, exhibiting a discontinuous jump across the Mott–CDW transition, confirming the first-order nature of the transition.

**Method.** The study employs analytical and numerical techniques on the extended Hubbard model, focusing on solving for the continuous low-spectral-weight solution to $	ext{Re } G[k, \omega_{	ext{GZ}}(k)] = 0$.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using the extended Hubbard model, incorporating nearest-neighbor repulsion ($V$) alongside standard Hubbard terms ($t, U$), and is analyzed in the context of competing Mott antiferromagnetism and checkerboard CDW ordering.

**Key observables.** Green's function zero dispersion ($\omega_{	ext{GZ}}(k)$), coefficients $s_{	ext{NN}}$, ground-state energy, and double occupancy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strengths ($U, V$), hopping parameters ($t, t'$), and the ratio of these parameters (e.g., $U/t$, $V/t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the $V$-extended Momentum-Mixing Hatsugai-Kohmoto (MMHK) framework and takes the zero-broadening limit for calculating the GZ dispersion.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the distinct correlated insulators (Mott vs. CDW) and show the GZ dispersion relationship, confirming the discontinuous jump across the transition.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by analyzing the GZ dispersion in the limits of pure Mott ($V=0$) and pure CDW ($U=0$) phases, establishing distinct strong-coupling mechanisms, and then analyzing the crossover region to confirm the first-order nature of the transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

While correlated insulators are devoid of low-energy quasiparticle poles, their Green functions retain clean momentum structure through zeros. However, precisely what zeros imply is not clear. By studying the extended Hubbard model both analytically and numerically, we establish a new paradigm for strongly correlated matter: the dispersion of Green function zeros is determined by both microscopic spin-spin correlations and defect kinematics. In fact, we find that the dispersion changes discontinuously across the transition between the Mott and the checkerboard charge-density wave phases as is expected for a first-order transition. This physics is robust to the inclusion of further neighbor hopping which simply fine tunes the spin-correlation or charge-defect kinematics. We conclude that it is the {\it dispersion} of the Green-function zeros that encode the physics of ordering resultant from the strong correlations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores quantum complexity dynamics in systems where the subsystems are not connected. Using both holographic models and random quantum circuits, the authors demonstrate counterintuitive effects, such as a smaller, fragmented subsystem carrying high complexity while a larger one remains simple at finite temperatures. This work sharpens the link between geometric structure and computational complexity in quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide novel insights into how geometric properties (disjointness) can drastically alter the expected thermalization and complexity dynamics of quantum systems, which is relevant for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates quantum complexity dynamics, focusing specifically on how the complexity evolves in noncontiguous (disjoint) subsystems, which is expected to reveal physics beyond the standard connected region analysis.

**Main result.** The study shows that at finite temperature, a smaller, noncontiguous subsystem can exhibit exponentially large complexity while its larger complement remains simple, an effect that vanishes in the infinite-temperature limit.

**Method.** The authors employ complementary theoretical tools from holographic duality (AdS/CFT) and the analysis of random quantum circuits to calculate quantum complexity.

**Model / system.** The models include a CFT on a spatial circle in the thermofield double state (holography) and random quantum circuits on qudits. The analysis compares the complexity of disjoint subsystems A and B.

**Key observables.** Quantum Complexity, Entanglement Entropy, and the Ryu–Takayanagi (RT) surface area/volume.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite temperature ($eta$ finite) vs. infinite temperature ($eta 	o 0$); the number of disjoint components ($m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The holographic setup assumes identical subsystems on both sides to model the symmetry of the thermofield double state.

**Figures summary.** Figures 1 and 2 illustrate candidate RT surface configurations and the implications of the black hole location relative to the entanglement wedge.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the holographic complexity for $m=2$ and $m=3$ disjoint intervals, generalizes to arbitrary $m$ to derive the complexity collapse time, and then presents the analogous results using random quantum circuits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum complexity has emerged as a natural probe of chaos, thermalization, and the black hole interior on timescales long after local observables have equilibrated. However, its time evolution has been studied almost exclusively in subsystems confined to a single connected region. We show, using complementary tools from holography and random quantum circuits, that noncontiguous subsystems composed of multiple disjoint regions give rise to qualitatively new physics compared to the contiguous case. First, at finite temperature, a subsystem occupying less than half of the total system can carry high complexity at late times, even as the complexity of its larger complement remains low. This inversion of the usual hierarchy is intrinsically thermal: it vanishes in the infinite-temperature limit, which is the regime modeled by random quantum circuits. Second, if a subsystem's complexity equilibrates at an early time, then fragmenting it into $m$ disjoint components can further reduce this timescale by a factor of $m$, a phenomenon we exhibit in both holography and random quantum circuits. These results not only sharpen the correspondence between geometric and computational notions of complexity, but also motivate the search for novel complexity phenomena in quantum dynamics.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01029v1>

**Summary.** This paper resolves a known vacuum instability issue in discrete-time Dirac models by proposing a tangent fermion discretization. The authors demonstrate that this new scheme maintains a single Dirac cone while ensuring that pair creation at the zone corner does not lead to vacuum decay in the continuum limit. This offers a stable lattice approach for studying relativistic quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for stabilizing lattice discretizations of continuum physics (like the Dirac equation), which is crucial for developing reliable numerical simulations in condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the vacuum instability of the Dirac quantum walk in discrete time, which arises from particle-hole pair creation at the zone corner.

**Main result.** By employing a tangent fermion discretization scheme and using a symmetric split-operator coupling, the model restores vacuum stability by ensuring the zone corner remains gapless and decoupled from the energy release mechanism in the continuum limit.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Cayley transformation to map quasi-energies to real energies and calculates the Schwinger effect (pair-production rate) by comparing different lattice coupling schemes.

**Model / system.** The system is a Dirac quantum walk modeled by a discrete-time quantum map. The analysis compares the original Dirac quantum walk to a novel tangent fermion discretization, which is analyzed using Floquet operators and coupling potentials.

**Key observables.** Schwinger pair-production rate ($\kappa$), quasi-energy gap ($\Delta_\pi$), and the dispersion relation across the Brillouin zone corner.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice constants ($	au, a$), mass gap ($\mu$), potential strength ($V_0$), and the smoothness parameter ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between spatially-averaged and exactly gauge-invariant split-operator couplings, and examines the continuum limit ($	au 	o 0$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy balance showing instability in the original model, the gapless crossing in the tangent fermion model, and the calculated pair production probability $T$ for different coupling schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the instability in the standard Dirac quantum walk, then introducing the tangent fermion scheme, and finally performing a detailed comparison of coupling methods (gauge invariant vs. averaged) to show how the instability is suppressed or vanishes in the continuum limit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Dirac quantum walk (a 1+1 dimensional space-time discretization of the Dirac equation) has a $2μ$ mass gap both at the center and at the corner of the quasi-energy-momentum Brillouin zone. Gupta and Short recently noted [Quantum 9, 1845 (2025)] that the Dirac vacuum can create a particle-hole pair at the zone corner with the release of an energy $2μ$ to the environment. They removed this vacuum instability at the expense of fermion doubling, the appearance of a second low-energy Dirac cone. Here we show that an alternative discretization scheme, with a tangent rather than a sine dispersion relation, offers stability while retaining a single Dirac cone. The key step is the Cayley transformation from the unit circle of Floquet eigenvalues $e^{-i\varepsilon}$ to the real line of unbounded energies $E=2\tan(\varepsilon/2)$. We compute the Schwinger effect (particle-hole pair creation in a uniform electric field) for tangent fermions and show that the pair-production rate agrees with the continuum result for a single Dirac cone. The zone corner is exactly decoupled if the scalar potential is coupled through the Hermitian generator of the quantum map. If it is coupled as a split operator, in order to preserve exact gauge invariance on the lattice, the corner does contribute - with a weight that vanishes quadratically with the lattice constants, in contrast to the Dirac quantum walk where the zone-corner instability survives the continuum limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the difficulty of sampling from Boltzmann distributions when energy measurements are noisy. The authors introduce the Poisson product estimator, an unbiased technique that outperforms existing methods under high noise. They demonstrate its utility by successfully finding the ground state energy of the H3+ ion using a gradient-free, simulated annealing approach.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, general-purpose numerical tool for quantum simulation, allowing accurate thermal sampling and optimization in settings (like ML potentials or quantum hardware) where energy measurements are inherently noisy.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is accurately sampling from the Boltzmann distribution when the energy expectation value is only available through a stochastic, noisy estimator.

**Main result.** The authors introduce the Poisson product estimator, an unbiased method that maintains accurate sampling even when noise levels are high, successfully recovering the ground-state energy of H3+.

**Method.** They propose the Poisson product estimator, which is used within a simulated annealing framework to progressively sample the system from high to low temperatures.

**Model / system.** The methods are tested on the double-well potential benchmark and applied to finding the ground state of the H3+ ion using a variational quantum circuit.

**Key observables.** Energy convergence to the FCI ground-state energy, and the shape of the sampled parameter distribution mapping the variational energy landscape.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), noise variance ($\sigma^2$), and the estimator's required upper bound ($E_{\max}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The estimator must be unbiased and non-negative; the method relies on the existence of an upper bound on the energy estimator.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares sampling accuracy across different noise levels for the double-well potential, showing the PPE's robustness. Figure 2 tracks the energy convergence of the H3+ circuit during simulated annealing.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of noisy energy sampling, introduces the Poisson product estimator as a superior alternative to the penalty method, and then demonstrates its application via simulated annealing to solve the H3+ ground state problem.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sampling from the Boltzmann distribution is central to computational physics, yet hard when the energy is known only through a stochastic estimate, such as with machine-learned molecular potentials, in variational Monte Carlo, or on quantum computers, because a noisy energy biases the sampled distribution. The penalty method of Ceperley and Dewing corrects this but requires the noise variance and becomes intractable when it is large. We introduce the Poisson product estimator, an unbiased, non-negative estimator of the Boltzmann weight that only needs an upper bound on the energy estimator and remains efficient at high noise. Using it to optimize a variational quantum circuit gradient-free, we recover the $H_3^+$ ground-state energy in a minimal basis and, by sampling rather than following a single trajectory, also map the variational energy landscape.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a scalable, unified simulation platform for comparing four major Quantum Key Distribution protocols (BB84, B92, E91, BBM92). By modeling realistic channel impairments and performing extensive statistical analysis, it allows for systematic comparison of key rates and security diagnostics like the CHSH S-statistic across varying fiber lengths.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a highly rigorous, statistically robust simulation environment for comparing fundamental quantum communication protocols, which is crucial for understanding practical quantum network feasibility.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) simulation tools are limited to single protocols and lack rigorous statistical evaluation, hindering systematic comparison.

**Main result.** The platform demonstrates that BB84 achieves the highest mean key-rate (160,045 Hz) at 25 km, and the E91 protocol successfully detects eavesdropping by showing a drop in the CHSH S-statistic from 2.12 to 1.58.

**Method.** The authors developed a unified simulation platform using Python/Qiskit that models four foundational QKD protocols (BB84, B92, E91, BBM92) under a shared impairment model.

**Model / system.** The system simulates QKD over a fiber link, incorporating impairments like attenuation, source/detector losses, polarization drift, and configurable eavesdropping attacks. It supports both a local desktop GUI and a remote web client interface.

**Key observables.** Key-rate (Hz), Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER), and the CHSH S-statistic.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fiber length (10, 25, 40 km), number of independent runs (20), and qubits per run (10000).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model primarily includes intercept-resend attacks; it does not account for dark counts, timing jitter, or multi-photon emission.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the desktop GUI for parameter entry and results visualization, and the web interface for parameter sweeps, showing confidence bands widening at longer distances.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the unified simulation platform architecture (Python/Qiskit core, dual interfaces), presents the shared impairment model, and concludes with comparative results across the four protocols and varying link lengths.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) offers information- theoretically secure key establishment grounded in the laws of quantum physics, yet its practical reach is limited by the prohibitive cost of photonic hardware and the fragmented nature of existing simulation tools. Most simulators support only a single protocol and report results from individual stochastic runs, making systematic protocol comparison and reproducible statistical inference difficult. This paper presents a unified QKD simulation platform that implements four foundational protocols BB84, B92, E91, and BBM92 within a single Python/Qiskit engine. A shared impairment model covers fiber attenuation, source and detector losses, po- larization drift, and configurable intercept-resend eavesdropping. The platform is accessible through two independent interfaces that share the same backend: a desktop application (Tkinter, Matplotlib) for local experimentation and a browser-based web client (React, Node.js/Express) for zero-install remote access. All reported results are drawn from repeated-run studies (20 independent runs, 10000 qubits each), with mean, standard deviation, and 95% confidence intervals stated throughout. At a 25 km fiber link, BB84 achieves the highest mean key-rate of 160,045 Hz, followed by BBM92 (80023 Hz), E91 (52815 Hz), and B92 (40011 Hz) ordering that tracks simulation-derived sifting efficiencies precisely. Under the E91 protocol, the CHSH S-statistic averages 2.12 at baseline and falls to 1.58 when an eavesdropper is activated, demonstrating Bell-inequality-based intrusion detection independent of QBER

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00160v1>

**Summary.** This paper studies thermal order in CFTs involving multiple scalars coupled to an O(N) vector field. Using FRG and large N techniques, the authors demonstrate that in 3D, they can construct models where a discrete symmetry remains spontaneously broken at all non-zero temperatures. This provides a theoretical framework for persistent symmetry breaking beyond standard expectations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of persistent symmetry breaking at finite temperature is a core topic in statistical mechanics and quantum field theory, directly challenging the Mermin-Wagner-Coleman-Hohenberg theorem's implications for discrete symmetries.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the phenomenon of 'thermal order,' specifically whether symmetries spontaneously broken at low temperatures can persist (remain broken) at all non-zero temperatures in quantum field theories.

**Main result.** The authors find a novel class of large N CFTs in three dimensions where a discrete symmetry is proven to be spontaneously broken at all non-zero temperatures, challenging standard intuition about thermal symmetry restoration.

**Method.** The study employs the Functional Renormalisation Group (FRG) technique and large N expansion methods, first analyzing the system in d=4-epsilon dimensions before focusing on the 3D limit.

**Model / system.** The system consists of Conformal Field Theories (CFTs) in d=4-epsilon and d=3 dimensions, involving multiple scalars coupled to an O(N) vector field, possessing symmetries like Z_2^M times S_M.

**Key observables.** Symmetry breaking patterns, thermal expectation values, and the existence/stability of fixed points in the RG flow.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of scalars (M), the dimension (d=3 or 4-epsilon), and the large N limit (M << N).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large N expansion and functional renormalization group techniques, with the continuation from (4-epsilon) dimensions to 3D being a key, though potentially problematic, step.

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes the system in (4-epsilon) dimensions using FRG to find fixed points. It then discusses the continuation of these fixed points to d=3, noting potential non-unitarity. Finally, it works directly in d=3 using large N techniques to prove persistent symmetry breaking in a specific subspace of the conformal manifold.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study thermal order in conformal field theories (CFTs) in $d=4-ε$ and $d=3$ dimensions where several scalars are coupled to an $O(N)$ vector field. In $(4-ε)$ dimensions, we consider models coupling a cubic model of $M$ scalars with $\mathbb{Z}_2^M\rtimes S_M$ symmetry (corresponding to sign flips and permutations of the scalars) to an $O(N)$ vector model. For any $M$, we find a window of $N$ in which two Wilson-Fisher-like fixed points exist. We show that the $\mathbb{Z}_2^M\rtimes S_M$ symmetry is spontaneously broken at arbitrary nonzero temperatures for $M=2$ and sufficiently large $N$ within this window, but it remains unbroken for $M>2$. Using the functional renormalisation group to continue these fixed points towards three dimensions, we find that they collide and move into the complex plane well before reaching $d=3$, suggesting that their continuation to $d=3$ does not yield unitary CFTs. We then work directly in three dimensions at large but finite $N$, with $M\ll N$, initially without assuming any permutation symmetry among the $M$ scalars. By studying the RG flow, we show that these models possess a conformal manifold up to the leading nontrivial order in the $1/N$ expansion of the beta functions (which is $\mathcal{O}(1/N)$). At each point on this manifold, the scalars split into two classes that are distinguished by how they couple to the $O(N)$ vector field. We then restrict to a subspace of the conformal manifold where there is an additional symmetry under permutations of the scalars within each class. We prove that in a domain of this subspace, all $M$ scalars acquire thermal expectation values such that the symmetry under sign flips of the scalars is spontaneously broken at all nonzero temperatures. This provides a novel class of large $N$ CFTs that exhibit a rich pattern of persistent symmetry breaking at nonzero temperatures.

</details>
