**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates the mechanism driving terahertz magnon excitation in thin magnetic multilayers. By developing an atomistic framework, the authors successfully decompose the complex interfacial spin-orbit torque into its constituent spin and orbital parts. They conclude that the orbital torque, specifically its field-like component, is the dominant microscopic mechanism enabling efficient, symmetry-controlled excitation of these confined THz spin waves.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed, atomistic, and symmetry-controlled understanding of how interfacial coupling mechanisms (orbital torques) can be engineered to control quantum dynamics (THz magnons) in condensed matter systems, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems and spintronics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to resolve the microscopic origin of the interfacial driving torque responsible for exciting terahertz (THz) magnons in nanometer-thick ferromagnets, particularly due to the complexity of coexisting spin and orbital currents.

**Main result.** The research identifies the interfacial orbital torque as the primary channel for efficiently exciting exchange-dominated THz magnons, with the field-like component being the dominant torque contribution.

**Method.** The authors developed an atomistic framework, using first-principles calculations (Kubo formalism) and atomistic spin-dynamics simulations, to explicitly separate and analyze spin and orbital torque contributions.

**Model / system.** The system involves thin ferromagnets (e.g., Co) sandwiched between non-magnetic layers, forming trilayers (NM/FM/NM). The model utilizes an effective Heisenberg Hamiltonian augmented by a Spin-Orbit Torque (SOT) term.

**Key observables.** Excitation of perpendicular standing spin-wave (PSSW) modes in the THz regime, and the spectral power densities ($	ilde{q}(f), 	ilde{g}(f)$) derived from the magnetization dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** THz pulse amplitude ($\sim 10^8$ V/m), film thickness ($N_z$ layers), and the separation of torque into field-like (M-even) and anti-damping-like (M-odd) components.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The SOT effective fields are assumed to remain close to their static values over the THz excitation timescale, and the orbital torque contribution is localized at the interfaces.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of symmetric/asymmetric trilayers under THz pulses, showing how SOTs support different PSSW modes. Subsequent figures compare mode excitation using different torque components (M-even vs. M-odd) and highlight the role of the orbital torque.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical problem (unclear torque origin), detailing the theoretical framework (atomistic decomposition of SOT), presenting the simulation results (analyzing mode excitation based on torque components), and concluding with the identification of the orbital torque as the key driver.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exchange-dominated magnons in nanometer-thick ferromagnets extend to the terahertz regime through thickness quantization of perpendicular standing spin-wave (PSSW) modes. While interfacial spin-orbit torques (SOTs) have been shown to enable the excitation of such modes, the microscopic origin of the interfacial driving torque remains unclear. In particular, the coexistence of spin and orbital currents complicates the understanding. Here, we develop and use an atomistic framework that explicitly resolves interfacial symmetries and separates spin and orbital torque contributions. Exploiting a trilayer geometry for a thin ferromagnet sandwiched between non-magnetic layers, where the symmetry-controlled polarity of the interfacial torque produces mode-selective magnon excitation as observed in the recent experiment of Salikhov et al. Nature Phys. 19, 529 (2023), we disentangle the different interfacial torque contributions. By decomposing the torque into magnetization-even (field-like) and magnetization-odd components, we identify the field-like torque as the dominant contribution responsible for the excitation. Crucially, isolating orbital and spin contributions reveals that the interfacial orbital torque provides the primary channel for the efficient excitation of exchange-dominated THz magnons in thin ferromagnets. Our results establish a microscopic basis for symmetry-engineered control of confined terahertz spin dynamics in magnetic multilayers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates magnetic quantum defects in the antiferromagnet Cr2O3 using combined spectroscopic and theoretical approaches. The researchers identified three vacancy-induced excitations, interpreting the most prominent one (E1) as a local magnetic excitation of the chromium vacancy moment. This work establishes the potential of these defect states for quantum applications by linking their energy and field dependence to fundamental magnetic interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed spectroscopic fingerprint of localized magnetic moments associated with crystal defects, which is crucial for understanding how magnetic order and local spin dynamics can be engineered in solid-state quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to identify and characterize magnetic quantum defects within the uniaxial antiferromagnetic insulator, Cr2O3, suitable for potential quantum applications.

**Main result.** The authors discovered three distinct, vacancy-induced excitations, with the lowest energy mode (E1) being interpreted as a local magnetic excitation of the chromium vacancy state, sensitive to external magnetic fields near the spin-flop regime.

**Method.** The study combines experimental techniques (polarization-resolved Raman and terahertz spectroscopy) with theoretical modeling, including defect supercell first-principles calculations and spin Hamiltonian analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Cr2O3, an antiferromagnetic insulator. The theoretical model focuses on the magnetic excitations arising from chromium vacancies (V_Cr) within the crystal structure, modeled using spin Hamiltonians and DFT calculations.

**Key observables.** Three distinct excitations (E1, E2, E3); the E1 mode energy (64 cm-1); mode softening near the spin-flop field (6T); and the dependence of mode strength on vacancy concentration.

**Important parameters / regimes.** External magnetic field (H); spin-flop field (H_SF); temperature (T); and the local magnetic excitation energy scale (tens of meV).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interpretation of the E1 mode as a local magnetic excitation is based on defect supercell calculations, and the analysis relies on specific selection rules derived from the tunneling Hamiltonian approach.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spin structure, low-energy Raman/terahertz spectra showing field dependence, and comparisons of magnetic properties (magnetization vs. E1 mode strength) between different sample types.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the general problem of defect characterization, through experimental measurements linking excitations to vacancies, and finally to detailed theoretical modeling using spin Hamiltonians and selection rules to confirm the magnetic origin of the key low-energy mode.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Point defects have been successfully utilized in various quantum technologies, serving as quantum qubits for quantum computation, single-photon emitters for quantum communication, and nanoscale sensors for quantum metrology. However, their further development faces key challenges, particularly in discovering and exploring suitable defect-host systems that meet the necessary criteria for quantum applications. Here, using polarization-resolved Raman spectroscopy and terahertz absorption spectroscopy, we discover three distinct chromium-vacancy-induced excitations in the uniaxial antiferromagnetic insulator, Cr$_2$O$_3$. These vacancy-induced excitations have an energy scale of a few tens of millielectronvolts and are twofold degenerate, and the lowest one at 64 $cm^{-1}$ is sharp and sensitive to the external magnetic field along the easy-axis direction, particularly close to the spin-flop regime around 6T, where the mode softens from 64 to 27$cm^{-1}$. Based on the defect supercell first-principles calculations, we interpret the mode at 64 $cm^{-1}$ as a local magnetic excitation of the local moment within the chromium vacancy state. Our results establish that the magnetic defect states in Cr$_2$O$_3$ have potential for quantum applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper explores generalized quantum statistics by interpolating between bosonic and fermionic partition functions using a parameter $\xi$. It demonstrates that at specific discrete values $\xi = -1/m$, the system exhibits parafermionic behavior, leading to characteristic ground state energies and a distinct grand canonical partition function. This work extends the understanding of quantum statistics beyond standard models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit mathematical derivation of parastatistics via partition function interpolation provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding generalized quantum statistics beyond simple bosons and fermions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the interpolation between ideal boson and fermion partition functions at discrete values of the exchange weight factor $\xi = -1/m$. It aims to characterize the resulting zero-temperature ground state energies and the underlying statistics.

**Main result.** The interpolation yields non-trivial ground state energies corresponding to parafermions of order $m$ at specific discrete values of $\xi = -1/m$. The grand canonical partition function confirms the existence of genuine parastatistics for these cases.

**Method.** The analysis uses recursion relations for the partition function $Z_N$ and derives the grand canonical partition function $\Xi(t)$ using generating functions and algebraic combinatorics.

**Model / system.** The model considers non-interacting particles confined in any potential possessing a discrete energy spectrum. The statistics are governed by the parameter $\xi$, which interpolates between bosonic ($\xi=1$) and fermionic ($\xi=-1$) behavior.

**Key observables.** Zero-temperature ground state energy ($E_N$), Grand Canonical Partition Function ($\Xi(t)$), and the coefficient of the $t^N$ term in the generating function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The exchange weight factor $\xi$, the number of particles $N$, and the inverse temperature $\beta$. The critical parameter regime is $\xi = -1/m$ for integer $m$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The potential is assumed to have a discrete energy spectrum. The method treats $\xi$ as a mathematical parameter, potentially leading to unphysical results.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy $E$ vs. $\beta$ for small $N$, showing deviations from the boson limit only at specific $\xi$. Table I summarizes these calculated energies for $N=2, 3, 4$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework using recursion relations for $Z_N$ and calculates the energy $E$. It then derives the general form of the grand canonical partition function $\Xi(t)$ and applies it to the specific case $\xi = -1/m$, confirming the connection to parafermionic statistics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that for any potential with a discrete energy spectrum, the well-known interpolation between ideal boson and fermion partition functions at discrete values of $ξ=-1/m$ yielded zero temperature ground state energies corresponding to $m$ fermions occupying a single quantum state. The grand canonical partition function in this case can be a result from genuine parastatistics.

</details>

**Authors:** Eric Chitambar, Sarah Hagen, David W. Kribs, Mike I. Nelson, Andrew Nemec  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00220v1>

**Summary.** This paper advances the theory of Quantum Secret Sharing by introducing 'helper' codes and, most significantly, the Programmable Access Structure (PAS). It proves that implementing a PAS requires the non-local resource of quantum steering, providing a powerful tool for designing dynamically controlled quantum information protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between QSS, quantum error correction, and the fundamental resource of quantum steering provides a deep theoretical framework for secure quantum communication protocols, which is highly relevant to quantum networking.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates Quantum Secret Sharing (QSS), extending it to include a 'helper' shareholder and introducing the concept of a Programmable Access Structure (PAS).

**Main result.** It establishes that quantum steering is the necessary resource for implementing a PAS, and it derives bounds and constructions for helper codes based on quantum erasure channels.

**Method.** The work employs information-theoretic characterizations, relating QSS to quantum erasure channels, and utilizes quantum steering to model the dynamic, third-party control of the access structure.

**Model / system.** The model involves partitioning a quantum state $\mathcal{H}_Q$ across multiple shareholders $\{B_1, \dots, B_n\}$ and a helper $A$. The core mechanism is described by an encoding isometry $W$ and is analyzed using concepts from quantum error correction.

**Key observables.** Quantum mutual information $I(e_Q : S)$, the recoverability condition $	ext{RS} \circ 	ext{Tr}_S \circ \Pi_V = \Pi_V$, and the detectability of non-local correlations via quantum steering.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parties $n$, the size of the secret space $|Q|$, and the structure of the access set $\Gamma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses primarily on pure QSS schemes, and the PAS model assumes security against an untrusted programmer due to the lack of signaling from the dealer to the programmer.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the PAS setup involving the dealer and programmer using pre-shared entanglement, and show the general QSS setup relating recovery maps to entanglement restoration.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining QSS and introducing the helper variant, to characterizing these codes via quantum erasure channels, and finally extending the framework to implement Programmable Access Structures using quantum steering.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum secret sharing (QSS) is a process in which the state of a quantum system is partitioned into multiple shares, allowing only specific subsets of shareholders to reconstruct the state, while others gain no information about its identity. In this work, we propose a variant of QSS, called ``helper QSS,'' which designates a special shareholder whose participation with any other group of parties is sufficient for recovering the state. We present different families of helper codes that function for an arbitrary number of parties and system sizes. As an application, we introduce the idea of a programmable access structure in QSS, which allows for a third-party programmer to independently choose the access structure of the code even after the shares are delivered to all the shareholders. This choice is made blindly, meaning that the programmer has no information about the secret being encoded, and it is implemented using the nonlocal process of quantum steering.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a critical assessment of the Self-Healing Diffusion Monte Carlo (SHDMC) method when applied to fermionic systems. The authors demonstrate that the standard implementation fails to converge to the correct fixed-node energy due to systematic node deterioration. They propose and validate modifications, such as emphasizing the nodal region or using localized basis sets, which are necessary for achieving accurate results.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous analysis of convergence failure and the proposal of specific algorithmic fixes (like local basis sets) are highly relevant to developing robust numerical techniques for strongly correlated quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper critically assesses the Self-Healing Diffusion Monte Carlo (SHDMC) method for improving the nodal surface of fermionic wavefunctions.

**Main result.** Standard SHDMC fails for the tested fermionic model, causing the fixed-node energy to increase monotonically; however, modifying the nodal update criterion or using localized basis sets can partially restore convergence.

**Method.** The authors employ an iterative fixed-node calculation within the SHDMC framework, comparing standard criteria against modified local criteria and localized basis sets.

**Model / system.** The study uses a one-dimensional model with periodic boundary conditions, incorporating an inversion symmetry to mimic the nodal structure of fermionic wavefunctions.

**Key observables.** Fixed-node energy ($E_{FN}$), nodal convergence (node position $	heta_N$), and the stability index ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of nodal constraint ($\epsilon$) and the choice of basis set (Fourier vs. localized).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the nodal structure can be iteratively optimized using the SHDMC procedure, which is shown to be conditionally true.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the monotonic increase of $E_{FN}$ under standard SHDMC, and show that modified criteria or localized bases allow the node position to converge toward the correct physical value.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the failure of standard SHDMC, then proposing and testing a modification focusing on the nodal region, and finally demonstrating improved convergence using localized basis sets.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the Self-Healing Diffusion Monte Carlo (SHDMC) method using a one-dimensional model with periodic boundary conditions. An inversion symmetry is introduced to mimic the antisymmetry property of fermionic wavefunctions, with the bosonic and fermionic sectors being modeled by the even and odd eigenstates, respectively. As in realistic fermionic systems, the nodal structure is only partially constrained by symmetry, making this model a non-trivial testbed for nodal optimization algorithms such as SHDMC. We show that the nodal evolution under SHDMC iterations can be cast into a dynamical system exhibiting both attractive and repulsive fixed points. In the standard formulation of SHDMC applied to this model, the fixed-node energy is found to increase upon iteration, and the node converges to a wrong value, indicating that SHDMC does not always converge to the correct solution. We further show that this problem is partially cured by modifying the nodal update criterion to give more importance to the nodal region. Achieving convergence in the general case very likely requires the use of a localized basis set, as is the case for this model.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses a symmetry-reduced Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) to study the quantum phase transition in the Interacting Boson Model. By analyzing finite-size scaling of order parameters, the authors demonstrate that the VQE approach can efficiently and accurately simulate this transition, confirming theoretical predictions for the critical point.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of quantum simulation techniques (VQE) to model a well-established nuclear structure model (IBM) provides a concrete testbed for quantum algorithms in condensed matter/nuclear physics, particularly for studying phase transitions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the quantum phase transition from the spherical $U(5)$ limit to the deformed $SU(3)$ limit within the Interacting Boson Model (IBM).

**Main result.** The symmetry-reduced Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) successfully simulates the QPT, reproducing structural observables and approaching the analytically predicted critical point $\xi_c = 8/17$.

**Method.** The study employs a symmetry-preserving, minimum-qubit VQE framework combined with finite-size scaling and thermodynamic-limit extrapolation techniques.

**Model / system.** The system is the Interacting Boson Model (IBM), which describes the low-lying collective states of even-even nuclei. The Hamiltonian is parameterized by control variables $\xi$ and $\chi$.

**Key observables.** Normalized d-boson occupation ($\rho_d$) used as the primary order parameter, ground-state energy ($E_0$), and quadrupole collectivity ($	ext{CQ}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Control parameters $\xi$ and $\chi$; the critical point $\xi_c = 8/17 \approx 0.470588$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that symmetry reduction based on rotational invariance is valid for minimizing qubits while preserving the finite-size physics of the QPT.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare VQE results against exact diagonalization benchmarks, showing convergence of finite-size indicators toward the analytic critical point $\xi_c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the IBM Hamiltonian, develops a symmetry-reduced VQE workflow, benchmarks this method against exact diagonalization results, and analyzes the finite-size scaling of order parameters to extrapolate the true thermodynamic critical point.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The spherical-to-deformed quantum phase transition of the Interacting Boson Model (IBM) is investigated using the variational quantum eigensolver (VQE). The $U(5)$--$SU(3)$ transitional Hamiltonian is studied with $χ=-\sqrt{7}/2$. We develop a symmetry-preserving, minimum-qubit VQE framework for collective nuclear models, achieving a substantial reduction in qubit requirements without compromising the finite-size quantum-phase-transition physics. The transition is characterized through the normalized $d$-boson occupation and ground-state energy derivatives. Finite-size results are found to approach the analytic critical point $ξ_c=8/17\simeq0.470588$, with an independent order-parameter extrapolation yielding $ξ_\infty=0.46986(61)$. The VQE reproduces ground-state energies and structural observables to numerical precision. These results demonstrate the potential of symmetry-reduced VQE for efficient quantum simulations of collective nuclear dynamics and quantum phase transitions.

</details>

### [Verifiable quantum advantage in extremely low depth](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01448v1)

**Authors:** Alexandru Gheorghiu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01448v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

The symbols form a path, and the mask hj lives on the j-th edge: it moves a random amount from position j to position j + 1, subtracted from cj and added to cj+1. Summing along the path cancels every mask and returns the sum.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper constructs a sampling problem that proves quantum advantage using quantum circuits with remarkably low depth ($\log \log \lambda$ or constant). It achieves this by adapting existing lattice-based proofs of quantumness, requiring only shallow circuit execution and sampling, which is a significant step toward practical quantum cryptography demonstrations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work pushes the boundary of quantum complexity theory by demonstrating that quantum advantage can be shown using extremely shallow circuits, which is highly relevant for near-term quantum hardware feasibility.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To construct a sampling problem that is solvable by extremely shallow quantum circuits, computationally hard for classical algorithms based on lattice assumptions, and efficiently verifiable.

**Main result.** The authors provide constructions showing that quantum advantage can be demonstrated using circuits of depth $O(\log \log \lambda)$ or even constant depth ($QAC^0$), without requiring mid-circuit measurements.

**Method.** The work compiles existing lattice-based proofs of quantumness (like those from Arabadjieva et al.) into low-depth quantum circuits, framing the problem as a challenge-response protocol.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum circuits operating on qubits, specifically analyzing the capabilities of shallow circuits defined by $QNC^0[\log \log]$ or $QAC^0$ gate sets. The underlying hardness relies on the Learning with Errors (LWE) problem.

**Key observables.** Acceptance probability of the quantum sampler, the depth of the required quantum circuit, and the computational advantage over classical samplers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($\log \log \lambda$ or constant), security parameter ($\lambda$), and the lattice knowledge assumption strength ($	ext{LK-}1/4$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on non-standard assumptions, including a strengthened variant of the adaptive-hardcore-bit property of LWE and the $	ext{LK-}1/4$ assumption.

**Figures summary.** The notes refer to a table comparing various quantum advantage protocols based on depth, measurements, and fan-out/fan-in, indicating the current work improves these metrics.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon previous work (BCMVV, AGGM) by focusing on reducing the circuit depth. It establishes the necessary quantum sampling problem and proves the existence of polynomial-size quantum circuits achieving the desired low depth while maintaining classical hardness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give a sampling problem that is solvable by shallow quantum circuits, hard for polynomial-time classical algorithms under lattice-based assumptions, and efficiently verifiable by a classical computer. The quantum sampler admits two implementations: one uses log-logarithmic-depth quantum circuits with one- and two-qubit gates, i.e., $\mathsf{QNC}^0[\log\log]$ circuits, while the other uses constant-depth quantum circuits with unbounded fan-in gates, i.e., $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ circuits. Our construction can be seen as compiling the Learning with Errors (LWE)-based single-round proof of quantumness of Arabadjieva et al. (2025) to very low depth. The price paid for this compilation is the reliance on less standard, though well-motivated, assumptions: in addition to the lattice knowledge assumption used by Arabadjieva et al. (2025), we require a strengthened variant of the adaptive-hardcore-bit property of LWE, for which we provide supporting evidence. Unlike previous low-depth proofs of quantumness, the quantum computation here requires no mid-circuit measurements or feed-forward: it consists only of running a shallow circuit and sampling from its output distribution. This shows that shallow quantum circuits have sufficient structure to solve certain classically hard tasks whose solutions can be verified efficiently.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates using hybrid quantum-classical neural networks to solve the CVRP, aiming to replace large, parameter-heavy classical modules with compact Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs). The authors successfully show that this replacement significantly reduces model size while maintaining competitive solution quality compared to classical baselines, suggesting a viable compression strategy for neural combinatorial optimization.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work demonstrates a practical application of quantum circuits to compress and accelerate components within complex machine learning models used for combinatorial optimization, which is a key area of ML-physics intersection.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of learning to solve the strongly NP-hard Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP) using machine learning models.

**Main result.** Replacing a parameter-heavy feed-forward module in a classical attention-based routing network with a small Quantum Neural Network (QNN) successfully reduces model parameters by 56.6% while maintaining solution quality close to the classical baseline for small to medium instances.

**Method.** The authors employ a hybrid quantum-classical neural network trained via the REINFORCE algorithm to optimize routing heuristics for the CVRP.

**Model / system.** The problem is modeled using an arc-based integer programming formulation for CVRP. The proposed model is a hybrid network where the classical encoder's feed-forward block is replaced by a variational QNN circuit utilizing rotations and CNOT gates.

**Key observables.** Objective value (route length), average relative deviation ($r_m$), and total evaluation time ($t$) when solving CVRP instances of size $n \in \{10, 20, 50, 100\}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The QNN uses a small configuration ($q=4$ qubits, $L_q=2$ layers), and performance is tested across varying instance sizes ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation noted is that the performance gap between the hybrid model and the classical baseline grows for larger instances, and the comparison does not prove quantum advantage over classical heuristics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the encoder and decoder architectures of the classical model, and Figure 3 details the integration of the QNN to replace the feed-forward block.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the CVRP as the optimization problem, details the classical attention model, proposes the hybrid QNN replacement strategy in the encoder, trains and tests the model on varying instance sizes, and concludes by comparing performance against classical solvers.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work studies hybrid quantum-classical neural networks for learning routing heuristics. Specifically, this paper asks whether small quantum neural networks can replace parameter-heavy modules inside a competitive attention-based routing model while maintaining solution quality. For the capacitated vehicle routing problem, encoder feed-forward replacement emerges as the most promising design: it reduces the number of model parameters by 56.6% while keeping the hybrid model close to the classical neural baseline at small and medium instance sizes, although the gap grows for larger instances. This work also compares to classical routing algorithms, which remain highly competitive and often superior on the fixed Euclidean test sets. Our results therefore do not indicate quantum advantage or solver dominance, but identify encoder feed-forward replacement as a viable hybrid-module compression strategy for neural combinatorial optimization.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the memory capacity of one-way quantum automata under symmetry constraints using advanced operator algebra. It develops a framework that quantifies memory by analyzing the rank of associated Hankel matrices and decomposes the system's dynamics into components related to symmetry. The key finding is that noncommutativity imposes a quantifiable, one-state penalty on the worst-case computational cost.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection drawn between algebraic structure (commutativity) and computational cost (state cost) provides a powerful, general framework for understanding resource limitations in quantum computation models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop an operator-algebraic theory to characterize the 'behavioral memory' of one-way quantum automata when subjected to compact symmetry constraints.

**Main result.** It establishes a sharp dichotomy where noncommutativity in the invariant algebra raises the worst-case classical cost by exactly one state, and it relates memory capacity to structural properties like the commutant's center and multiplicity blocks.

**Method.** The authors employ operator-algebraic techniques, defining behavior via the Hilbert-Schmidt pairing and analyzing the rank of the acceptance Hankel matrix, $eta(A)$.

**Model / system.** The model is the one-way quantum automaton, whose evolution and memory are captured by an acceptance function $f_A(w)$ and analyzed through its associated operator algebra.

**Key observables.** Behavioral Memory ($eta(A)$), Operational Strict-Cutpoint Memory ($	ext{scPFA},_R(A, 	au)$), and the rank of the acceptance Hankel matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the symmetry commutant, the dimension of the invariant algebra, and the state cost associated with commutativity vs. noncommutativity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the scope of strict-cutpoint language recognition by real PFAs, and the theory distinguishes between different comparison classes of quantum channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the memory measure using Hankel matrices, deriving an exact formula for the linear memory dimension via isotypic decomposition, and then analyzing the structural cost implications based on the algebraic properties (commutativity vs. noncommutativity) of the symmetry-constrained invariant algebra.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Under compact symmetry, observable behavior reduces to an invariant operator algebra, but its dimension is not yet classical memory: some coordinates are dynamically frozen, some invisible to threshold tests, and some already classical. We develop an operator-algebraic theory that separates these effects through three filters. For one automaton, behavior is the Hilbert--Schmidt pairing between prefix-reachable states and suffix-observable effects, whose rank equals the real Hankel rank without controllability or observability assumptions. Maximizing this invariant over a symmetry-constrained dynamical class gives a structural capacity controlled by the symmetry commutant: its center stores isotypic populations frozen by reversible dynamics, its traceless multiplicity blocks carry movable noncommutative coordinates, dissipation removes the unary spectral loss inside those blocks, and covariant mobility releases relative populations subject to component conservation. Operational realization then determines which surviving coordinates force probabilistic states. For a fixed nontrivial invariant readout, full mobility gives an exact dichotomy in worst-case state cost: a commutative invariant algebra costs exactly its dimension, whereas a noncommutative multiplicity block raises the unrestricted cost by exactly one state. Thus noncommutativity has a one-state worst-case classical price. The known four-letter quadratic-plus-one law at trivial symmetry is the fully mobile endpoint of this principle. Schur--Weyl duality further shows that different preserved symmetries on the same tensor-power Hilbert space can change the worst memory scale from polynomial to exponential, while fixed-weight modules give an exact Catalan law at half filling, with structural capacity equal to the Catalan count minus its central-sector correction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously classifies all 'nice' Vertex Operator Algebras and Superalgebras with central charge $c=1$. It proves that these algebras must arise either from rank-1 integral lattices or as orbifolds of such lattices. This classification has deep implications for understanding the chiral algebras that form the holomorphic sector of rational $c=1$ Conformal Field Theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the rigorous classification of low-dimensional CFTs (like $c=1$) provides fundamental constraints on quantum field theories, which informs the understanding of boundary conditions and topological order relevant to quantum information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a mathematically rigorous classification of 'nice' $c=1$ Vertex Operator Algebras (VOAs) and Vertex Operator Superalgebras (VOSAs). Physically, this translates to classifying the chiral algebras arising in the holomorphic sector of rational $c=1$ Conformal Field Theories (CFTs).

**Main result.** The classification states that any such VOA must be isomorphic to a lattice VOA ($	ext{V}_L$) or an orbifold thereof ($	ext{V}_L^G$). The list includes specific examples like $	ext{V}_{2m}$ and three exceptional algebras ($	ext{V}T, 	ext{V}O, 	ext{V}I$).

**Method.** The mathematical approach relies on showing that these VOAs are Tannakian, using criteria based on the vacuum character and the structure of simple modules. Physically, the classification is linked to viewing the VOA as the boundary theory of a 3D Topological Quantum Field Theory (TQFT).

**Model / system.** The primary mathematical objects are $c=1$ VOAs/VOSAs. Physically, the context is the study of rational bosonic or fermionic CFTs, particularly those whose non-identity primaries have positive conformal dimension.

**Key observables.** The vacuum character ($	ext{ch}_V(	au)$) and the function $P_V(	au)$ (related to modules with integer conformal dimension) are key diagnostic tools.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The central charge $c=1$ is the defining parameter. The classification depends on the structure of the associated finite symmetry group $G$ and the lattice rank.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The classification is restricted to 'nice' VOAs (strongly rational and pseudo-unitary). The full classification of CFTs is reserved for a companion paper.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the conformal moduli space of unitary 2D CFTs with $c=1$. Figure 2 illustrates the physical proof sequence: TQFT $	o$ Chern-Simons theory $	o$ VOA boundary.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the mathematical criterion for nice VOAs using the vacuum character, proving that they must be lattice VOAs or orbifolds. It then connects this structure to the physical realization as chiral algebras in $c=1$ CFTs, with the full classification of full CFTs deferred to a companion work.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For mathematicians: In this first in a series of two papers, we give a mathematically rigorous classification of (sufficiently nice) $c=1$ vertex operator algebras (VOAs) and vertex operator superalgebras (VOSAs). We confirm the lore that any such VO(S)A is either a lattice VO(S)A $V_L$ associated to a rank-1 integral lattice $L$, or can be obtained as an orbifold thereof, i.e. a $G$-invariant subalgebra $V_L^G$ for some finite group $G$ of automorphisms. All such $G$ are known, allowing for an explicit enumeration of nice $c=1$ VO(S)As. A key ingredient in our approach is to establish a general criterion for nice VOAs, requiring knowledge only of the vacuum character, for testing when the simple modules with integer conformal dimension span a symmetric fusion subcategory which is braided tensor equivalent to $Rep(G)$. In our companion paper, we calculate the ribbon auto-equivalences of the representation categories of the nice $c=1$ VOAs, and leverage this to obtain the classification of nice bosonic and fermionic $c=1$ full conformal field theories.   For physicists: We rigorously classify the chiral algebras that can arise in the holomorphic sector of a bosonic or fermionic rational $c=1$ conformal field theory (CFT) whose non-identity primaries all have positive conformal dimension. Thinking of chiral algebras as gapless boundary conditions of 3D topological quantum field theories (TQFTs), our result says that any such chiral algebra is either a holomorphic boundary of $U(1)_k$ Chern-Simons theory, or can be obtained by passing to the $G$-invariant states thereof for some finite group $G$ of symmetries. We explicitly enumerate these chiral algebras and also discuss their non-invertible symmetries. In a companion paper, we build on these results using techniques from the study of 3D TQFTs to classify full bosonic and fermionic rational $c=1$ CFTs.

</details>

### [Codes for Quantum Secret Sharing with a Helper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00234v1)

**Authors:** Eric Chitambar, Sarah Hagen, David W. Kribs, Zhao Ma, Mike I. Nelson, Andrew Nemec  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00234v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<sub>FIG. 2: (a) Blind LOCC-adressable helper codes use classical information sent from the helper to Bi to recover quantum information. This is similar to how classical information can sometimes be used in the decoding D of EA-codes (b). However, the helper’s system more closely resembles that of the receiver in an entanglement-assisted code: both hold a correctable set of qubits.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a deep structural analysis of Quantum Secret Sharing codes featuring a 'helper' party. It characterizes these codes, particularly the 'blind helper' type, by deriving necessary and sufficient conditions based on stabilizer theory. The work proves that for single-qubit secrets, recovery is always achievable using LOCC from the helper, advancing the theory of quantum information security.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical characterization of information access structures (like helper codes) is fundamental to quantum cryptography and secure quantum computation protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the general structure of Quantum Secret Sharing (QSS) codes, specifically focusing on the unique constraints imposed by the 'helper' access structure.

**Main result.** It fully characterizes blind helper stabilizer codes for single qubits, showing that recovery is always possible via one-way LOCC from the helper to the target party.

**Method.** The analysis employs stabilizer formalism, deriving necessary and sufficient conditions for helper codes, and comparing structural constraints derived from information theory and quantum error correction principles.

**Model / system.** The model is pure QSS schemes where a secret is encoded into a subspace of a multipartite system. The focus is on stabilizer codes, which are defined by commuting Pauli operators on the shared qubits.

**Key observables.** The possibility of recovery via Local Operations and Classical Communication (LOCC), and the structure of the logical operators supporting the secret.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parties ($n$), the size of the secret ($k=1$ qubit), and the structure of the access set $\Gamma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on pure QSS schemes using isometric encoding, and initial results are derived for stabilizer codes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the recovery of maximal entanglement using information from an authorized subset of parties.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining helper codes, reviewing error correction conditions, characterizing stabilizer codes using structural lemmas (e.g., Lemma 2 for blind helper codes), and finally deriving the general form of qubit helper codes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Helper quantum secret sharing is a form of secret sharing defined by its unique access structure. One special fixed party, called the helper, can work together with any other party to fully decode the secret. A blind helper is one who can provide this assistance while not holding any local information about the encoded secret. In this work, we analyze the general structure of QSS helper codes and present new code constructions. We fully characterize the structure of blind helper stabilizer codes and show that for the encoding of a single qubit, recovery is always possible using one-way local operations and classical communication (LOCC) from the helper to the targeted party. Furthermore, we demonstrate how such codes also allow for the helper to target larger subsets of parties by one-way LOCC, enabling them to be authorized to recover the secret. Finally, when each party only holds a qubit, we identify the general form of all helper codes (including non-stabilizer codes) and find that LOCC recovery is only possible in special cases.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous, non-asymptotic theory for the concentration of additive functionals of Stratonovich-type, which are crucial for analyzing thermodynamic quantities along fluctuating paths. By employing a novel symmetrization technique, the authors overcome mathematical hurdles associated with non-self-adjoint generators. The results provide powerful, explicit concentration bounds, particularly highlighting a distinct sub-Gaussian behavior under detailed balance.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical treatment of path functionals like work or entropy production is central to modern non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, providing tools for quantifying fluctuations beyond simple averages.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper tackles the challenging problem of proving non-asymptotic concentration inequalities for additive functionals of Stratonovich-type ($\mathcal{J}_t$) in the context of stochastic thermodynamics.

**Main result.** They successfully prove concentration inequalities for $\mathcal{J}_t$ for general geometrically ergodic diffusion processes, notably showing that under detailed balance, the concentration is sub-Gaussian with a variance independent of the spectral gap.

**Method.** The authors overcome the difficulty posed by the non-self-adjoint nature of the tilted generator by working with a symmetrized Dirichlet form and bounding the semigroup norm via the quadratic form.

**Model / system.** The analysis applies to general geometrically ergodic diffusion processes ($X_s$) describing fluctuating paths in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics. The comparison is drawn between Stratonovich-type functionals (currents) and classical Lebesgue-type functionals.

**Key observables.** Additive functionals $\mathcal{J}_t = rac{1}{t}\int_0^t U(X_s)\circ dX_s$, and their concentration bounds.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time $t$, the function $U$, and the properties of the underlying diffusion process (e.g., geometric ergodicity, detailed balance).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis requires working with a symmetrized Dirichlet form and assumes the underlying process is a geometrically ergodic diffusion process.

**Paper structure.** The paper contrasts the mathematical difficulty of Stratonovich functionals versus Lebesgue functionals, develops a novel symmetrization technique for the generator, proves the general concentration inequality, and finally establishes the distinct sub-Gaussian behavior under detailed balance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Additive functionals $\overline{J}_t=\frac{1}{t}\int_0^tU(X_s)\circ dX_s$ of Stratonovich-type recently attracted much attention in the context of inference of thermodynamic properties of complex systems from observations $U$ of individual fluctuating paths $(X_s)_{0\le s\le t}$, whereby $X_0$ is initiated from some general measure. Concentration results on $\overline{J}_t$, albeit desirable, are virtually nonexistent. They turn out to be significantly more challenging to prove than for classical Lebesgue-type functionals $\overlineρ_t=\frac{1}{t}\int_0^t V(X_s)ds$ because the tilt deforms the full second-order structure of the Feynman-Kac generator instead of contributing an additive potential. This renders the generator generally non-self-adjoint even under detailed balance. We overcome this by working with a symmetrized Dirichlet form with a new effective potential that now couples the observable to the non-equilibrium character of the dynamics. We prove concentration inequalities for $\overline{J}_t$ for any bounded, sufficiently smooth vector-valued function $U$ of a general geometrically ergodic diffusion process $X_s$, including explicit sub-gamma and Bernstein-type inequalities, and we obtain explicit upper bounds on ${\rm Var}(\overline{J}_t)$. Strikingly, under detailed balance the concentration of $\overline{J}_t$ is distinctively sub-Gaussian at all times and all deviations, with a variance proxy fixed by the noise alone and independent of the spectral gap, which has no analog for $\overlineρ_t$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces DF-SQD, a novel hybrid quantum-classical algorithm designed to improve the sampling-based diagonalization of molecular Hamiltonians. By deriving deterministic auxiliary-field circuits, DF-SQD efficiently proposes quantum states, leading to superior energy accuracy and reduced resource requirements compared to prior methods on benchmark molecules like $	ext{N}_2$ and $	ext{Fe}_2	ext{S}_2$.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the resource scaling challenge in quantum chemistry simulations, providing a concrete, hybrid algorithmic improvement for accessing high-accuracy electronic structure data on near-term quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is the quantum diagonalization of the Hamiltonian to accurately estimate ground-state eigenvalues and eigenvectors in quantum chemistry, which is computationally intractable for large systems.

**Main result.** The proposed DF-SQD method significantly improves energy accuracy and resource efficiency compared to existing sampling-based methods (SQD) on both small ($	ext{N}_2$) and larger ($	ext{Fe}_2	ext{S}_2$) molecular systems.

**Method.** DF-SQD is a hybrid algorithm that uses quantum circuits to deterministically propose occupation-number configurations, followed by classical configuration interaction to evaluate the Hamiltonian and guide subsequent proposals.

**Model / system.** The work applies to quantum chemistry calculations, specifically simulating the $	ext{N}_2$ molecule and the $	ext{Fe}_2	ext{S}_2$ cluster, utilizing active-space Hamiltonians and mapping them onto qubit systems.

**Key observables.** Energy error (relative to reference calculations), selected product-space dimension (number of determinants), sampling time, and QPU usage.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Active space size (e.g., 32 qubits for $	ext{N}_2$, 40 qubits for $	ext{Fe}_2	ext{S}_2$), number of shots, and error tolerances.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the ability to derive deterministic auxiliary-field circuits and assumes that the energy estimate converges monotonically with increasing sampling resources.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow (DF-SQD schematic), and comparisons of energy error and product-space dimension between DF-SQD and SQD on $	ext{N}_2$ and $	ext{Fe}_2	ext{S}_2$ using both simulation and hardware results.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the DF-SQD hybrid algorithm, details its mechanism using auxiliary-field circuits, and validates its performance by comparing it against established sampling methods on two distinct molecular systems ($	ext{N}_2$ and $	ext{Fe}_2	ext{S}_2$) across simulation and hardware platforms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sampling-based quantum diagonalization method exploits Quantum-centric supercomputing platforms to sample bitstrings for Hamiltonian projection on a quantum computer, and then classically diagonalize the Hamiltonian to estimate the eigenvalues and eigenvectors. In current quantum devices an algorithm is useful when shallow quantum circuits with error mitigation support can discover better results while having either a proof of convergence or some method to explain trust in experiment. In this paper, we introduce DF-SQD, a hybrid algorithm that derives deterministic auxiliary-field circuits from selected double-factorization leaves of the two-electron tensor. The circuits propose occupation-number configurations, while selected configuration interaction evaluates the original active-space Hamiltonian and can recentre subsequent proposal rounds. On N2 (32 qubits; 6-31G basis) and a 40-qubit [Fe2S2(SCH3)4]2- active-space Hamiltonian, we show that DF-SQD improves the energy obtained from sampled determinant spaces while using shallow number-preserving circuits in both simulator and hardware runs. For N2, DF-SQD is 45x more accurate with a 11.23\% smaller subspace, and due to its ability to sample better bitstrings at lesser shots it is 2.93x faster than SQD in quantum devices. For the iron-sulfur cluster, DF-SQD generated a subspace dimension of 221M with 400K shots, while SQD needed 1.5M shots to generate a 238M subspace, thus we have better subspace recovery evident from the hardware at 3.75x reduced shots. At a matched 50M subspace dimension, DF-SQD is 1.32x more accurate (achieves a 24.5\% relative error reduction over standard SQD). So overall, our method is able to discover better results with shallower circuits, is sample efficient, uses configuration recovery (so has targeted error mitigation) and we have empirical convergence observation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a profound mathematical duality between the eigenvalue statistics of Hermitian and non-Hermitian random matrices. By relating replica partition functions via nonlinear sigma models, the authors derive universal, closed-form results for non-Hermitian spectral properties. This advances the understanding of complex quantum systems described by non-Hermitian Hamiltonians.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between Hermitian and non-Hermitian spectral statistics is crucial for understanding open quantum systems, where non-Hermiticity arises naturally from coupling to an environment (dissipation).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper uncovers an exact duality connecting the level statistics of Hermitian and non-Hermitian random matrices.

**Main result.** This duality establishes a non-Hermitian counterpart to Dyson's threefold way, providing closed-form expressions for bulk pair-correlation functions and hard-edge spectral densities for previously intractable non-Hermitian classes.

**Method.** The duality is derived by relating two replica partition functions, which are formulated using fermionic nonlinear sigma models and evaluated in the large-N limit.

**Model / system.** The study uses random matrix theory, comparing Hermitian ensembles (governed by standard symmetries) with non-Hermitian ensembles (governed by symmetries like $	ext{TRS}^\dagger$, $	ext{PHS}$, and $	ext{Chiral Symmetry}$).

**Key observables.** Level statistics, including bulk eigenvalue pair-correlation functions and spectral densities near the origin (hard-edge statistics).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large-$N$ limit, symmetry classes (e.g., A, $	ext{AI}^\dagger$, $	ext{AII}^\dagger$), and the replica number $n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large-$N$ limit and the mathematical framework of nonlinear sigma models derived from replica methods.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares calculated pair-correlation functions ($\pi^2 R_2(\omega)$) for non-Hermitian classes A, $	ext{AI}^\dagger$, and $	ext{AII}^\dagger$ against exact diagonalization results.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically establishes the duality using sigma models, applies it to the three Wigner-Dyson classes in the bulk, and then extends the mapping to the seven nonstandard Altland-Zirnbauer classes for the hard-edge statistics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Random matrix theory describes complex quantum systems statistically, with symmetry as its organizing principle. We uncover an exact duality between the level statistics of Hermitian and non-Hermitian random matrices in the large-$N$ (matrix size) limit. It acts class by class: the two replica partition functions, given by fermionic nonlinear $σ$ models, are related by analytic continuation. Applied to the three Wigner--Dyson classes, the duality yields the universal bulk eigenvalue pair-correlation functions of non-Hermitian random matrices. This establishes a non-Hermitian counterpart of Dyson's threefold way, organized by transposition symmetry: generic complex, complex symmetric, and complex self-dual matrices. The dissipative spectral form factors of these classes follow in closed form as well. Applied to the seven nonstandard Altland--Zirnbauer classes, the duality yields the exact spectral densities near the origin, the non-Hermitian hard-edge statistics. Most of these statistics were previously known only numerically. Exact diagonalization confirms the analytical predictions, and physical models demonstrate their universality. We expect these results to be the tip of a deeper correspondence between Hermitian and non-Hermitian random matrix theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a dynamical framework for analyzing the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) by reparameterizing the control space using a strength-imbalance measure. The authors show that the structure of useful quantum dynamics is robust across changes in circuit depth and scheduling, even when the corresponding native parameter region becomes highly compressed at large system sizes. This separates the inherent physical dynamics from the limitations of the standard parameterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a dynamical framework that separates the intrinsic dynamics of the quantum process from the specific parametrization (native angles) is highly relevant for understanding the scalability and robustness of quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize the trainability of the QAOA gradient landscape by understanding how its gradient response changes across circuit parameters and problem size.

**Main result.** The gradient landscape exhibits a coarse organization in a dynamical representation (strength–imbalance) that persists across circuit changes, and near-optimal solutions occupy a distinct intermediate dynamical regime, separate from the largest local gradients.

**Method.** The authors introduce a dynamical representation of the QAOA parameter space using a norm-weighted layer strength and a cost–mixer imbalance, analyzing the gradient response using exact-state simulations and geometric bounds.

**Model / system.** The system is the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) applied to MaxCut problems, evolving under a time-dependent Hamiltonian composed of cost ($H_c$) and mixer ($H_m$) Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Gradient landscape, near-optimal solution regions, layer strength ($S_\ell$), cost–mixer imbalance ($\alpha_\ell$), and solution quality ($P_{	ext{tail}}(0.9)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($p$), system size ($n$), and the dynamical variables (strength and imbalance) which separate overall evolution scale from relative generator contributions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a piecewise constant control scheme, interpreting parameters as evolution times, and utilizes the Magnus expansion to describe effective dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the gradient response measure (GRMS) under random schedules, the persistence of high-quality solution regions across varying schedules, and the contraction of the high-response region in the native parameter space as system size increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for a dynamical representation by analyzing the QAOA gradient landscape. It then develops this framework using strength and imbalance, demonstrating that useful dynamics persist even if the native parameter region compresses with system size. Finally, it applies this to MaxCut to show the robustness of the optimal dynamical regime.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Characterizing the trainability of the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) requires understanding how its gradient landscape changes across circuit parameters and problem size. Yet these gradients are usually described in terms of the native QAOA angles, making it difficult to distinguish parameter specific features from broader changes in the underlying circuit dynamics. Here we introduce a dynamical representation of the QAOA parameter space based on a norm-weighted layer strength and a cost--mixer imbalance, separating the overall scale of the evolution from the relative contribution of the two generators.   Using exact-state simulations of MaxCut, we find that the gradient landscape exhibits a coarse organization in these dynamical variables that persists across changes in circuit depth and schedule structure, while the finer interference pattern remains schedule dependent. Near-optimal solutions do not simply coincide with the largest local gradients, but instead occupy a distinct intermediate dynamical regime. Uniform schedules recover the broad location of this regime, whereas nonuniform schedules mainly reorganize its fine structure. Across the system sizes studied, near-optimal solution regions remain extended in the dynamical representation while their preimages in the native QAOA angles become substantially compressed at larger sizes. These results separate the persistence of useful QAOA dynamics from their accessibility in the native parameterization, and provide a dynamical framework for interpreting QAOA trainability across circuit and problem scales.

</details>

### [Emerging Personas and Tools for Hybrid Quantum-HPC Systems](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01299v1)

**Authors:** Eun-Kyung Lee, Jessie Yu, Claudio Carvalho, Yoonho Park, Marcelo Amaral, Tim Osborne, Woong Shin  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01299v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.01299_figures/2609.01299_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Fig. 1: Overview of six quantum user personas: A. Quantum Kernel and Library Developers, B. Domain Scientists, C. QCSC Operators, D. QCSC System Engineers, E. QCSC HPC Software Developers, and F. Quantum Physicists. The three emerging QCSC roles are highlighted in yellow.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the operational challenges of integrating quantum processors with supercomputers, creating the concept of Quantum-Centric Supercomputing (QCSC). It achieves this by identifying and detailing six distinct user personas, each with unique tools and data requirements. The core contribution is establishing a framework based on these personas to guide the development of standardized data formats for the entire quantum-HPC ecosystem.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a high-level, systems-engineering perspective on the practical challenges of quantum hardware integration, which informs the necessary software and data infrastructure for future quantum simulation work.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is operationalizing Quantum-Classical Supercomputing (QCSC) by managing the complex integration of Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) with High-Performance Computing (HPC) systems.

**Main result.** The paper argues that defining distinct user personas and understanding their specific data needs is crucial for developing the necessary consistent data representations and analytics frameworks for the entire quantum-HPC ecosystem.

**Method.** The authors characterize six distinct user personas involved in QCSC environments, analyzing their responsibilities, required tools, and operational data needs.

**Model / system.** The focus is on the conceptual framework of Quantum-Centric Supercomputing (QCSC) systems, which integrate QPUs (tens to hundreds of qubits) with classical HPC infrastructure.

**Key observables.** Operational data, system health, resource utilization, circuit fidelity, and time-to-solution are key diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The scale of integration (tens to hundreds of qubits) and the need for unified resource management are critical.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The paper assumes that a single term like 'quantum user' is insufficient, necessitating a persona-based approach to system design.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an overview of six quantum user personas and their relationship to the layered QCSC architecture, while Table I lists the tools associated with each persona.

**Paper structure.** The paper identifies the QCSC problem, characterizes the six user personas, analyzes their roles and tools, and concludes by stressing the need for standardized data representations to support the diverse user base.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computing is entering an era where quantum processing units (QPUs) with tens to hundreds of qubits are increasingly integrated with high-performance computing (HPC) systems to support hybrid quantum-classical workloads. This paradigm, often referred to as Quantum-Centric SuperComputing (QCSC), introduces new operational, software, and infrastructure challenges. In this paper, we identify and characterize six distinct personas involved in QCSC environments. Particular attention is given to emerging roles that arise at the interface between quantum and HPC systems. We analyze the responsibilities and tools associated with each persona and examine their differing requirements for operational data. Consistent data representations and analytics frameworks will be required to support diverse users across the quantum-HPC ecosystem. By defining personas and their data needs, we provide a foundation for future discussions in this critical area.

</details>

**Summary.** This work solves the long-standing problem of finding exact joint eigenvalue densities for non-Hermitian random matrices with specific symmetries. The key finding is that these densities are mathematically equivalent to the scattering states of the Calogero model. This reveals that the spectral statistics are governed by integrable dynamics, leading to power-law tails in correlation functions absent in simpler models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between random matrix theory, integrability (Calogero model), and spectral statistics provides deep insights into the underlying symmetries governing complex quantum systems, which is relevant to understanding spectral rigidity in quantum chaotic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the exact joint eigenvalue densities for non-Hermitian random matrices possessing transposition symmetry.

**Main result.** These joint densities are shown to be scattering-state wave functions of the Calogero model, revealing complex spectral statistics beyond simple Coulomb gas behavior.

**Method.** The analysis involves solving differential equations derived from Gaussian integration by parts and relating the resulting structure to known integrable systems like the Calogero model.

**Model / system.** The model consists of non-Hermitian random matrices belonging to three symmetry classes ($	ext{AI}^\dagger$, $	ext{A}$, $	ext{AII}^\dagger$). The underlying physics is governed by an eigenvalue problem whose structure is mapped onto the Calogero Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Joint eigenvalue density ($ho_C(z)$), nearest-neighbor spacing distribution ($p_{	ext{NN}}(s)$), and two-point spectral correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry class dictates the Calogero coupling strength ($k$), which is crucial for determining the spectral behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on identifying an underlying integrable structure (Calogero model) and making approximations in the collision limit for density calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare origin-conditioned nearest-neighbor spacing distributions across different symmetry classes and show the asymptotic behavior of the spacing density.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by defining the symmetry classes, derives the governing differential equations for the density, connects the solution to the Calogero scattering states, and analyzes specific observables like correlation functions and spacing distributions for each class.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Determining exact joint eigenvalue densities is central to random matrix theory. We solve this long-standing problem for non-Hermitian matrices with transposition symmetry (complex symmetric and complex self-dual) at arbitrary matrix size. Up to a Vandermonde factor, they are scattering-state wave functions of the Calogero model, a line of particles interacting through an inverse-square potential, with the coupling strength set by the symmetry. In contrast to many previously known joint densities, the densities cannot be written as a gas of eigenvalues with pairwise interactions. We further compute the complex level spacing distributions and two-point spectral correlation functions, which carry power-law tails, absent in a Coulomb gas. Our results shed light on the interplay among random matrices, integrability, and symmetry.

</details>

### [Exact Virtual Channel Programming with Vanishing Excess Overhead](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01419v1)

**Authors:** Mingrui Jing, Mengbo Guo, Hongshun Yao, Xin Wang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01419v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.01419_figures/2609.01419_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Exact virtual channel programming. (a) A fixed physical processor with a finite-dimensional program register cannot exactly realize a continuous family of distinct unitary channels. (b) A physical normalized Choi state πE selects the target channel for a fixed linear retrieval rule P. Its averaged action satisfies P(ρS ⊗πE) = E(ρS) at the level of recon- structed output observables. (c) Each trial samples one of the physical channels in a norm-optimal quasi-decomposition P = p+Q+ −p−Q−and classically reweights the measured outcome. The total weight p+ + p−= ∥P∥⋄quantifies the cost of P. Minimizing this total weight over exact retrievers defines the programming overhead.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously quantifies the resource cost associated with simulating an arbitrary quantum channel family using limited physical resources. It derives sharp scaling laws showing a trade-off between quantum memory and classical sampling overhead. The results recast a fundamental theoretical obstruction into a precise, measurable quantitative bound.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental quantitative bounds on the limits of quantum simulation and programming, which is crucial for understanding the resource overheads in quantum computation and quantum networking protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the quantitative trade-off between the memory required to store a quantum program and the classical sampling overhead needed to exactly reconstruct the output statistics of an arbitrary quantum channel family.

**Main result.** It establishes sharp scaling laws for the required overhead, showing that the one-copy overhead $
u_1(	ext{CPTP}_d) = 2d^2 - 3 + 2/d^2$, and that for many copies, $
u_k(	ext{CPTP}_d)$ approaches a limit determined by the locally learnable unitary degrees of freedom.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques involving quasi-decomposition, semidefinite programming (SDP) formulations derived from mixed-tensor representations, and establishing lower bounds via covariant retrievers.

**Model / system.** The system models the process of 'virtual' programming of a fixed physical processor to emulate an arbitrary family of $d$-dimensional quantum channels ($	ext{CPTP}_d$). The reconstruction relies on randomized sampling and classical post-processing.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the programming overheads, $
u_k(	ext{CPTP}_d)$, quantified by the diamond norm $\lVert P Vert_\diamond$, and the required number of samples $N$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the system dimension $d$ and the number of copies $k$ used in the reconstruction protocol.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the target channel family is $	ext{CPTP}_d$ and that the reconstruction protocol involves sampling physical channels followed by classical post-processing.

**Figures summary.** Figures contrast physical channel implementation versus exact reconstruction protocols, while tables present numerical results for finite-copy calculations comparing SDP bounds with PBT-achievable upper bounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the quantitative obstruction to exact programming, deriving the optimal one-copy overhead ($
u_1$), and then establishing the asymptotic scaling law for the many-copy regime ($
u_k$ as $k 	o \infty$) using advanced algebraic and SDP techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A finite-dimensional physical processor cannot exactly program a continuous family of distinct unitary channels. We show that this obstruction becomes quantitative when the target channel is stored in a normalized Choi state and its output observables are reconstructed by sampling physical channels and classically post-processing their measurement outcomes. For arbitrary $d$-dimensional channels, we construct a target-independent exact reconstruction protocol and prove the optimal one-copy sampling overhead, which grows quadratically with system dimension. We further prove the sharp fixed-$d$ law that the excess overhead vanishes inversely with the number of identical Choi programs. The upper bound combines deterministic port-based teleportation with a quasi-decomposition that corrects its depolarizing distortion. The converse maps any low-overhead reconstruction protocol to a physical learner of unknown unitaries and uses local quantum estimation to recover the same leading coefficient. These results recast the universal no-programming obstruction as a quantitative trade-off between quantum program memory and classical sampling, with a leading cost that reflects the locally learnable unitary degrees of freedom.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses kernel collapse in quantum machine learning by proposing that the correlation fractal dimension ($D_2$) dictates the maximum usable number of qubits. The authors demonstrate that using $D_2$ as a budget keeps the quantum kernel functional on both simulators and hardware, whereas over-encoding using methods like PCA causes the kernel to collapse.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous, information-theoretic measure ($D_2$) to guide the design of quantum circuits for data encoding, directly addressing the resource scaling problem in QML.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum kernel methods applied to tabular data suffer from 'kernel collapse' when the feature map width exceeds the data's true intrinsic dimension.

**Main result.** The correlation fractal dimension ($D_2$) provides a superior, data-driven estimate for the optimal qubit budget, keeping the quantum kernel 'geometrically alive' where standard methods like PCA fail.

**Method.** The authors use the correlation fractal dimension ($D_2$) derived from the data to set an a priori qubit budget, comparing the resulting quantum kernel fidelity against those derived from PCA or all attributes.

**Model / system.** The model involves constructing quantum feature maps ($\vert\psi(x)angle$) using rotations on qubits (e.g., ZZ map) to define a quantum kernel $K_{ij} = \langle\psi(x_i) | \psi(x_j)angle$ for classification on tabular data.

**Key observables.** Kernel fidelity, the 'knee' width (last usable qubit count), and the ratio of nearest-neighbor to farthest-pair kernel similarities ($	ext{near}/	ext{far}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The correlation fractal dimension ($D_2$), the bandwidth ($c$), and the specific feature map used (e.g., ZZ layer depth).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optimal qubit budget is determined by the fractal dimension, and the kernel's usability is defined by maintaining specific structural inequalities (the 'alive' criterion).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the measured kernel fidelity (MAE) and the mean off-diagonal elements of the kernel matrix across various feature map widths, showing a clear collapse past the fractal dimension limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of kernel collapse, proposes $D_2$ as the solution, validates this budget using multiple datasets and map types (ZZ, IQP), and confirms the findings using both quantum simulators and actual quantum hardware measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Angle-encoded quantum kernels on tabular data collapse when the feature map is wider than the intrinsic dimension of the data. We propose the correlation fractal dimension D2 as an a priori qubit budget: encode D2 coordinates chosen by FD-ASE instead of the PCA-95% width or all E attributes. On nine data sets and a statevector simulator (n= 32), a one-layer ZZ fidelity kernel at q=D2 stays geometrically alive while the same kernel at the PCA-95% width has already collapsed. The budget is map-dependent: product-state and IQP maps overshoot it; a second ZZ layer undershoots it. Packed dense-angle and re-uploading encodings still live at the fractal q, but not when PCA-95% features are stacked onto those qubits. Shrinking the angle bandwidth moves the ZZ knee later; stretching it kills the kernel earlier. On IBM Quantum (ibm_fez, 256 shots, n=8) the one-layer ZZ kernel at the fractal width matches the exact kernel (MAE 0.021); past that width both hardware and simulator have collapsed. The ceiling is a property of the map-data pair at a stated bandwidth, not of the classical table alone.

</details>
