### [High-Rank Encoding Can Improve Approximate Quantum Error Correction](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00778v1)

**Authors:** Bikun Li, Liang Jiang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00778v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.00778_figures/2609.00778_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. (a) The upper bound ∆(ε, s) given by Eq. (5) for different values of s. The black dashed curve is the earlier ε(1−ε) bound derived from Ref. [27]. (b) Numerical estimates of the optimized gap versus optimized infidelity for the noise maps constructed below and different values of dL, with dC = d2 L. The inset compares the finite-error ratios of the plotted gaps to (εopt)2 with the asymptotic ceiling (dL −1)/4. The exact limiting coefficient is given by Eq. (25).</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that for optimal approximate quantum error correction, restricting the encoder to rank-one mappings sacrifices performance compared to unrestricted encoding. The authors derive a precise, asymptotically quadratic lower bound for this performance gap, demonstrating that higher-rank encoders are necessary for achieving the true optimum.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to quantify the performance limits of quantum error correction, which is fundamental to building fault-tolerant quantum computers. The explicit determination of the necessary encoder rank is a key theoretical result.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether optimal approximate quantum error correction (AQEC) requires an encoder of rank higher than one, challenging the assumption that rank-one encoders suffice for optimal performance.

**Main result.** The authors prove that for entanglement fidelity, a higher-rank encoder is necessary to achieve the global optimum, establishing a strictly positive gap between the unrestricted and rank-one achievable fidelities.

**Method.** The analysis uses entanglement fidelity as the objective function, deriving refined upper bounds on the infidelity gap by employing techniques like polar decomposition and analyzing the geometry of the error correction manifold.

**Model / system.** The model is the general quantum error correction framework involving an encoder ($C$), a noise map ($N$), and a decoder ($D$). The analysis focuses on comparing the optimal fidelity achievable with unrestricted encoders versus those restricted to rank-one mappings.

**Key observables.** Entanglement fidelity ($F_e$), the optimized gap ($\Delta_{opt}$), and the asymptotic quadratic coefficient ($\kappa^{(2)}$) quantifying the difference between optimal and rank-one performance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The small noise parameter ($\epsilon 	o 0+$) and the logical dimension ($d_L$) are crucial for determining the asymptotic behavior of the gap.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the encoder $C$ is a Completely Positive Trace Non-Increasing (CPTNI) map, and later relies on the existence of a global minimizer for the fidelity difference.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(a) compares the derived upper bound on the infidelity gap against prior work. Figure 1(b) shows numerical estimates of the optimized gap versus infidelity for varying logical dimensions, comparing them to the derived asymptotic ceiling.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing general upper bounds on the gap using fidelity measures, to constructing specific noise families that demonstrate a positive gap, and finally employing advanced geometric analysis (tangent spaces, residual equivariance) to derive the exact asymptotic quadratic coefficient.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventional quantum-code constructions encode pure logical states as pure code states, but this restriction can sacrifice performance. We show that intrinsic encoding randomness can improve optimal entanglement fidelity. We bound the loss from imposing a rank-one encoder and prove it is at most quadratic near perfect recovery after joint optimization. The optimized advantage survives small noise perturbations. An explicit noise family requires higher-rank encoders arbitrarily close to perfect recovery, with every optimal encoder mapping pure inputs to mixed code states.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper uses advanced algebraic topology to study how symmetries and their associated anomalies change when a quantum system undergoes dimensional reduction. By calculating the cohomology suspension map, the authors provide a systematic method to determine the 'higher anomaly' that governs the stability and classification of topological phases in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the concept of symmetry protection and anomaly classification is fundamental to understanding topological phases, which underpin many modern condensed matter models, including those involving strong correlations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper computes and tabulates the suspension map for cohomology operations, applying this to understand how anomalies and higher group structures behave under dimensional reduction in quantum field theories.

**Main result.** The analysis determines the 'higher anomaly' of a reduced symmetry and the reduced higher-group structure, both captured by the iterated cohomology suspension map $\Omega$.

**Method.** The authors use advanced algebraic topology, specifically studying the cohomology suspension $\Omega$ on Eilenberg-MacLane spaces, extending methods from Cartan and Moore, and automating calculations with a specialized software package.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum systems possessing discrete $p$-form symmetries. The physical process modeled is the dimensional reduction of the system by restricting symmetry defects to a codimension-$q$ subspace.

**Key observables.** The anomaly of the reduced symmetry (the higher anomaly) and the reduced higher-group structure, which are elements in various cohomology groups.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The codimension $q$ of the subspace, the form degree $p$, and the coefficient ring (e.g., $\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$ for anomalies).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the mathematical framework of generalized cohomology theories and the properties of the cohomology suspension map $\Omega$.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention extensive tables (e.g., Table 1, Table 9 & 10) providing computed results for cohomology operations and their dependence on the coefficient group.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining the physical problem (dimensional reduction of symmetries), then introducing the mathematical tool ($\Omega$), detailing the computational extension (p-adic series, `emcm`), and finally applying it to compute and tabulate the resulting anomalies and structures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We explicitly compute and tabulate the suspension map for cohomology operations, applying it to the dimensional reduction of anomalies and higher group structures of generalised symmetries in quantum field theories and lattice systems.   In a quantum system with a discrete $p$-form symmetry, we can restrict the symmetry defects to a codimension-$q$ subspace of spacetime, or equivalently confine the gauge field to a slab whose thickness approaches zero. This results in a $(p-q)$-form symmetry on the subspace. We determine the fate of two important properties of symmetries under this process. First, we compute the anomaly of the reduced symmetry, or the higher anomaly of the original symmetry, which acts as an obstruction to higher gauging and onsiteability. Second, for two symmetries forming a higher-group structure, encoded as a Postnikov class, we compute the reduced higher-group structure, which may or may not be trivial. Both cases are captured by the $q$-fold iteration of the cohomology suspension $Ω$, also known as the loop functor or transgression, in the cohomology of Eilenberg--MacLane spaces. Classical results state that $Ω$ is an isomorphism in a stable range and annihilates mixed anomalies.   To calculate $Ω$ over a base ring which is not a field, we make use of small models for the chain DGAs of Eilenberg--MacLane spaces, pioneered by Cartan and Moore, whose results we review and extend. The critical added step is the improvement of a mod $p$ chain contraction to the $p$-local integers, obtained via a $p$-adic series. We automate the computations in a software package \texttt{emcm}, and provide extensive tables of the results. Finally, to make contact with explicit cochain formulations of cohomology operations, we demonstrate how to calculate cup products and certain cup-$i$ products in this framework.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies quantum machine learning techniques, specifically the Quantum Weighted Moving Average (QWMA), to predict financial trends from Limit Order Book data. The model combines classical data preprocessing with quantum unitaries to process time-series information. Although quantum advantage was not proven, the resulting hybrid model performs competitively with state-of-the-art classical methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of quantum circuits to process time-series data, even in a financial context, provides an interesting, albeit tangential, application of quantum circuit design principles that could inform quantum algorithms for sequential data processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the feasibility of using quantum machine learning models to forecast price trends from complex, multivariate Limit Order Book (LOB) financial data.

**Main result.** While no definitive quantum advantage was shown, the combined classical-quantum model (QWMA) achieved performance comparable to the best classical benchmarks, suggesting the quantum component is highly expressive.

**Method.** The approach involves classically preprocessing LOB data (normalization) before encoding it into quantum states via a Unitary Embedding, which is then processed by a Quantum Weighted Moving Average (QWMA) operator.

**Model / system.** The core model is the QWMA, which is a quantum circuit structure based on a linear combination of unitaries (LCU). It is compared against simpler variants like QSMA and QEMA, using LOB data as the input time series.

**Key observables.** F1-score is the primary metric used to evaluate prediction performance across different datasets and model variants.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the number of qubits ($n_q$), the choice of unitary embedding circuit (e.g., Shallow, Circuit 1), and the weighting scheme (uniform vs. exponential).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors note that the model requires post-selection for hardware implementation and that the comparison is limited by the choice of fixed context windows and embedding circuits.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the F1-score of QEMA vs. QSMA across varying qubit counts and embedding circuits on different datasets, and illustrate the structure of the parameterized quantum circuits used for embedding.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QWMA model, details the classical preprocessing and quantum encoding steps, compares QWMA, QSMA, and QEMA performance on benchmark datasets (FI-2010, China A-shares), and analyzes the impact of normalization and weighting schemes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can quantum computers be useful for forecasting multivariate financial time series? In this work, we consider the problem of predicting price trends from limit order book (LOB) data. After identifying key components of classical models, we introduce the quantum weighted moving average (QWMA) model. The two main building blocks are, first, classically preprocessing via normalization of both feature and temporal dimensions and, second, a linear combination of unitaries-based layer for unitarily-embedded classical data. The models are evaluated on the FI-2010 benchmark dataset and a second dataset of China A-share stocks. While we do not present evidence of quantum advantage, the combined classical-quantum model demonstrates performances close to the best classical models. The quantum part of the model is expressive enough to focus on the most predictive parts of the time series. Several specializations of the QWMA model are considered, in particular a variant related to the widely-used exponential moving average (EMA). We give consideration to the limitations of the methods and the datasets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a framework for quantum mechanics on a real Kähler space, establishing a real analogue of the Schrödinger evolution equation. It proves that despite the mathematical shift from complex to real structures, fundamental physical observables like scattering cross sections and tunneling probabilities remain identical to their standard complex counterparts. This work is significant for understanding the mathematical robustness and geometric underpinnings of quantum theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical foundation for quantum mechanics using real structures, which is highly relevant for theoretical physicists exploring alternative mathematical formulations of quantum dynamics, especially those involving geometric structures like symplectic geometry.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes the mathematical framework for quantum mechanics when formulated over a real Kähler space, specifically studying the behavior of scattering and tunneling processes.

**Main result.** The authors prove that the real formulation yields an evolution operator that is both orthogonal and symplectic, and crucially, the scattering cross sections and tunneling probabilities match those derived from standard complex quantum mechanics.

**Method.** The methodology involves adapting concepts like Stone's theorem and scattering theory from complex Hilbert spaces to the real setting using the structure of a real Kähler space.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum particle governed by a Hamiltonian on a real Kähler space, which is mathematically realized by mapping the complex Schrödinger equation to a real evolution equation involving a symplectic matrix J.

**Key observables.** Differential scattering cross section, tunneling probability, probability current, and the evolution operator's orthogonality/symplectic nature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Hamiltonian H, the symplectic structure J, and the asymptotic limits for scattering theory.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on the existence of a real Kähler structure (K, g, $\omega$, J) and assumes asymptotic completeness for wave operators.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes detail calculations involving probability currents, scattering amplitudes $f(	heta)$, and the relationship between real and complex formulations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the real analogue of the evolution equation and Stone's theorem. It then applies this framework to scattering theory, demonstrating the equivalence of cross-sections, and finally analyzes tunneling probability, confirming its identity with the complex case.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish the general form of the evolution equation and study scattering and tunneling in real quantum mechanics. We prove an analogue of Stone's theorem for a real Kahler space and derive the corresponding general evolution equation, whose evolution operator is both orthogonal and symplectic. We formulate scattering theory in terms of real wave operators and the associated S-matrix. For the class of potentials considered, we show that the differential scattering cross section is equivalent to that obtained in standard complex quantum mechanics. We also show that the tunneling probability in real quantum mechanics is identical to that in complex quantum mechanics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the 'render ceiling,' a novel, model-free benchmark designed to rigorously separate perception failures from reasoning failures in vision-language models. By using rendered crystal structures, the authors demonstrate that a purely vision-based model can outperform complex VLMs, exposing that much of the apparent reasoning ability in current models is merely sophisticated pattern matching based on image extraction.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the subject is AI/ML, the rigorous, model-free benchmarking methodology and the use of deterministic geometric reconstruction to test model boundaries are highly relevant to developing robust simulation tools in physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing a model-free method to separate whether a vision-language model fails due to poor image perception or flawed reasoning about the image content.

**Main result.** The authors introduce the 'render ceiling,' a model-free benchmark that certifies model deficits, showing that a purely vision-based model outperforms most vision-language models on crystal structure tasks.

**Method.** They construct the render ceiling by inverting frozen camera views and re-solving cross-view geometric correspondences, bypassing the need for a secondary, potentially flawed, model.

**Model / system.** The study uses crystal structures as the physical system, testing vision-language models (VLMs) and supervised vision models on tasks like crystal system prediction from multiple rendered views.

**Key observables.** Accuracy scores against the model-free render ceiling ($R_1$), and the decomposition of error into 'perception share' and 'residual' components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is performed on 2,160 rendered crystal structures, and the performance is compared across fourteen different VLMs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The render ceiling is only guaranteed to fail through an enumerable set of projection coincidences, which was confirmed to be empty for the tested structures.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the model-free attribution instrument across different data 'rungs' (e.g., true positions vs. rendered geometry vs. text geometry); Figure 2 shows how the ceiling drops when camera views are withheld.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of separating perception from reasoning, introduces the model-free render ceiling using crystal structures, presents quantitative comparisons across various models, and analyzes the resulting error decomposition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multimodal evaluations cannot say whether a vision-language model misread an image or misreasoned about it, because every existing method for separating the two places a second model in the loop. We introduce the render ceiling, a model-free reference for benchmarks built by rendering known objects: inverting the frozen cameras and re-solving cross-view correspondence recovers exactly the answer the images support. We prove the ceiling fails only through an enumerable set of projection coincidences and certify that set empty on 2,160 rendered crystal structures, so every point of a model's deficit belongs to the model. Across fourteen vision-language models, supplying exact geometry as text lifts every model yet closes under half the gap for thirteen, while a supervised vision model with no language component reads the same images at 0.8952, above every vision-language model. The instrument exposes extraction-stage fabrication that downstream accuracy would misattribute to reasoning, yields camera-placement rules for benchmark builders, and transfers to any benchmark with an invertible forward rendering.

</details>

### [Structure-Aware Placement and Routing of Multi-Controlled Toffoli on Bivariate Bicycle Code Architectures](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00852v1)

**Authors:** Anik Basu Bhaumik, Suman Dutta, Siyi Wang, Anupam Chattopadhyay  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00852v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

</details>

<img src="2609.00852_figures/2609.00852_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

<sub>Figure 3: Inter-module routing characteristics under different factory placements and grouping configurations. (a)–(c) show the linear BB-module architecture for factory periods 2, 3, and 4, respectively, while (d)–(f) show the improvement in grid architecture vs linear for grouping factors 𝑘= 3, 4, and 5. (g)–(h) shows the aggregate and circuit failure probabilities for hardware error rates of 𝑝= 10−3 and and 𝑝= 10−4</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work tackles the resource overhead of implementing complex MCT gates on BB-code quantum computers. The authors introduce a novel, structure-aware placement strategy that groups interacting circuit components to minimize inter-module communication. This approach significantly reduces routing overhead and provides quantitative estimates of circuit failure probabilities for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation, the optimization techniques—specifically the graph-based placement heuristics and resource estimation—are relevant to designing complex physical implementations of quantum circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the major optimization challenge of efficiently mapping high-level Multi-Controlled Toffoli (MCT) gates onto fault-tolerant quantum architectures based on Bivariate Bicycle (BB) codes.

**Main result.** A targeted placement strategy exploiting the binary-tree structure of MCT decompositions reduces inter-module instruction counts by up to 16.02%, and grid-based layouts offer significant routing improvements over linear ones.

**Method.** The authors propose a structure-aware placement strategy combined with direct |CCZ> state injection to map optimal MCT decompositions onto the BB-code architecture, minimizing inter-module routing overhead.

**Model / system.** The system is a fault-tolerant quantum computer utilizing Bivariate Bicycle (BB) codes, which are a type of qLDPC code. The workload involves implementing MCT gates, requiring the management of magic-state factories for non-Clifford operations.

**Key observables.** Inter-module instruction count (routing overhead), aggregate execution errors (E), and circuit failure probabilities (P_fail).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The grouping factor (k), the physical error rate (p), and the topology (grid vs. linear).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the availability of optimal-Toffoli-depth MCT decompositions and relies on the Qiskit community's bicycle_numerics error estimator.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of routing performance between linear and grid factory placements, and show the calculated aggregate error and circuit failure probabilities as a function of circuit size (n) and physical error rate (p).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the difficulty of mapping MCT gates onto BB-code architectures. It then introduces the targeted placement strategy, detailing how grouping subtrees reduces routing overhead. Finally, it validates the approach by analyzing aggregate error and failure probabilities under different topological constraints.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The multi-controlled Toffoli (MCT) gate is a fundamental primitive in quantum circuit design, with applications in quantum arithmetic, cryptanalysis, and algorithmic implementations. Being a high-level logical operation, the efficient decomposition of MCT gates into lower-level netlists has remained a major optimization challenge for decades. While emerging quantum error-correcting codes such as bivariate bicycle (BB) codes drastically reduce fault-tolerance overhead, realizing non-Clifford circuits on modular BB-code architectures introduces complex compilation bottlenecks governed by inter-module routing, factory density, and layout. Consequently, the mapping of MCT gates onto BB-code architectures remains relatively unexplored.   In this paper, we overcome these challenges by mapping optimal-Toffoli-depth MCT decompositions (Dutta et al., PRA, 2025) onto BB-code-based fault-tolerant architectures via direct $\lvert \mathrm{CCZ} \rangle$ state injection from an external magic state factory. We introduce a targeted placement strategy that exploits the binary-tree structure of MCT decompositions to co-locate interacting subtrees. This approach reduces inter-module instruction counts by up to $\mathbf{16.02}\%$ compared to a naive sequential first-fit placement. We also evaluate the impact of factory placement across different topologies, demonstrating that grid-based layouts yield up to a $\mathbf{23.7}\%$ reduction in inter-module instructions relative to linear architectures (Yoder et al., arXiv, 2025). Finally, we validate the practical viability of our compiled circuits by analyzing aggregate execution errors and logical failure probabilities using the bicycle-ISA error estimator bicycle_numerics provided by the Qiskit community, https://github.com/qiskit-community/bicycle-architecture-compiler.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that determining the fermionic cohomology on the entire Fock space is $\mathrm{QMA}_1$-complete. It achieves this by mapping the physical problem onto a quantum computational hardness problem using advanced techniques involving differential operators and number-penalized lifts. This result establishes a fundamental computational barrier for solving zero-energy state problems in fermionic quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects abstract algebraic topology (cohomology) in quantum many-body systems directly to the computational complexity class $\mathrm{QMA}_1$, providing a powerful tool for classifying the hardness of ground-state problems in quantum physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational complexity of determining if the fermionic cohomology is non-zero on the entire, full Fock space.

**Main result.** The global fermionic cohomology problem on the full Fock space is proven to be $\mathrm{QMA}_1$-complete.

**Method.** The proof involves constructing a reduction from known $\mathrm{QMA}_1$-hard problems by lifting the problem structure to the full Fock space using number-penalized techniques and analyzing the resulting Laplacian operator.

**Model / system.** The system involves fermionic differential operators ($D$) acting on the full Fock space ($\mathcal{F}n$), which are related to the zero-energy states of an associated supersymmetric Hamiltonian. The structure is built upon marker differentials and data block encoding.

**Key observables.** The Betti number ($b_p$), which equals the zero-energy degeneracy of the Laplacian ($\Delta_p$), and the existence of non-zero cohomology classes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity class $\mathrm{QMA}_1$, the maximum number of modes a monomial acts on (e.g., $\le 41$), and the gap ($\delta$) in the Hamiltonian spectrum.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the exact verification convention of $\mathrm{QMA}_1$ and the ability to construct specific, sparse, and well-conditioned residual relations ($eR_ullet$).

**Figures summary.** The notes do not describe figures, but reference theorems and lemmas that establish the mathematical machinery for the complexity proof.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem (cohomology) to establishing the complexity class (QMA1) by first analyzing the specified-degree case, and finally generalizing the result to the full-Fock space using number-penalized lifts and explicit encoding schemes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fermionic cohomology characterizes the zero-energy states of the supersymmetric Hamiltonian associated with a fermionic differential. Previous work showed that the problem restricted to a particle-number sector specified with the input is $\mathrm{QMA}_1$-hard and belongs to $\mathrm{QMA}$. We study the global problem, in which no sector is specified and cohomology may occur anywhere in the full Fock space. This formulation directly matches the whole-space ground-state question: a specified-degree NO instance may still have zero-energy states in another sector, whereas the global NO promise excludes them across all sectors and their superpositions. We prove that this full-Fock problem is $\mathrm{QMA}_1$-complete for differentials given as exact lists of local monomials, even when each monomial acts on at most $41$ modes. As a companion result, we prove $\mathrm{QMA}_1$-completeness for the specified-degree problem with $30$-mode terms whose hard instances admit a one-dimensional block-chain realization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work details how Loop-Tree Duality simplifies the understanding of infrared and threshold singularities in complex quantum field theory calculations. It introduces a novel approach to construct finite scattering amplitudes by enforcing causal constraints. Furthermore, it provides a unique perspective by encoding this underlying causal structure using qubits and quantum circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is QFT, the explicit mapping of the causal/singular structure onto qubits and quantum circuits provides a direct, novel application of quantum information concepts to high-energy theoretical physics calculations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the complex theoretical challenge of understanding and constructing finite, infrared (IR) and threshold-free multiloop Feynman integrals in Quantum Field Theory.

**Main result.** The Loop-Tree Duality (LTD) provides a transparent framework for identifying these finite integrals, and the methodology can be mapped onto a quantum circuit structure using qubits.

**Method.** The authors review LTD, which reformulates loop integrations to explicitly handle singularities, and propose encoding the causal structure of the amplitudes using qubits and quantum circuits.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Quantum Field Theory (QFT) calculations involving multiloop scattering amplitudes. The mathematical framework utilizes Loop-Tree Duality (LTD) and is conceptually mapped onto quantum circuits.

**Key observables.** Infrared (IR) and threshold singularities, and the construction of finite, threshold-free scattering amplitudes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Loop integration parameters, external and internal momenta, and the structure of causal propagators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The LTD representation is assumed to inherently enforce causality by restricting configurations to acyclic paths, which is key to avoiding spurious singularities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate causal propagators, examples of collinear configurations leading to physical thresholds, and the directed states of a one-loop three-point function distinguishing finite from singular configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the LTD advantage in visualizing singularities, details novel strategies for constructing finite integrals (including pole vanishing conditions), and concludes by presenting a complementary perspective using qubits and quantum circuits to encode the causal structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

One of the main advantages of the Loop-Tree Duality representation of scattering amplitudes is that it makes the origin of infrared and threshold singularities particularly transparent. This talk reviews recent progress in describing how singularities emerge and cancel at the level of scattering and vacuum amplitudes, discusses a novel strategy to efficiently construct finite integrals, and presents a complementary perspective based on encoding the underlying causal and singular structure in terms of qubits and quantum circuits.

</details>

### [Uniform Hiding of Haar Block Transpose Gram Matrices](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01008v1)

**Authors:** Hongru Zhao  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01008v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<sub>FIG. 1. Proof architecture. The dense branch combines exact one column recursion, radial transport, centered COE scores, and telescoping. The rectangular branch uses a Haar block relative entropy bound and data processing. Their combination proves Theorem I.1.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.01008_figures/2609.01008_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Random matrix realization of the Haar lift proof of Lemma III.2. Biunitary convolution and the Gelfand pair property give the probability law identity in Eq. (III.9). The bottom line records the separate Markov contraction and variation derivative transport in Eqs. (III.10) and (III.11).</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2609.01008_figures/2609.01008_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 3. Proof flow for Lemma III.3. Appendix C supplies variation regularity for the scalar and orbital event paths. Appendix D evaluates their density scores by projective averaging and inverse Wishart moments. The final line propagates the third derivative bound from the origin to the full interval required in Eq. (III.18).</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2609.01008_figures/2609.01008_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 4. Eventwise Taylor decomposition used in Proposition III.4. The same beta sample defines the scalar shift, the centered orbital shift, and its truncation. Two intermediate laws expose the scalar Taylor contribution, the orbital Taylor contribution, and the exceptional event contribution before the total variation triangle inequality is applied.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes a strong, quantifiable bound on the total variation distance between the probability distribution generated by a complex interferometer (Haar-derived) and a simple Gaussian reference distribution. By employing advanced random matrix theory and telescoping arguments, the authors show that this difference decays rapidly with the size of the system, providing a critical component for hardness arguments in quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, quantifiable statement about the hardness of distinguishing the true quantum process (Haar-derived) from a classical Gaussian model, which is crucial for quantum security proofs.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove a uniform total variation comparison (hiding) between the probability law of a complex symmetric transpose Gram matrix derived from a Haar interferometer and its corresponding complex Gaussian reference law.

**Main result.** The total variation distance between the two probability laws is bounded by an explicit error term, $\delta_{M,N}$, which is quadratic in the number of selected output modes ($N$) and inversely proportional to the interferometer size ($M$).

**Method.** The proof combines advanced random matrix theory techniques, including centered circular orthogonal ensemble (COE) score analysis, rectangular relative entropy bounds, and a telescoping argument across ambient dimensions.

**Model / system.** The model is Gaussian Boson Sampling (GBS) implemented with squeezed vacuum states entering a passive interferometer, where the probability is governed by the squared hafnian of a complex symmetric product.

**Key observables.** The probability distribution governed by the transpose Gram product ($	ext{Y Y}^T$), and the total variation distance ($	ext{dTV}$) between the Haar-derived law and the Gaussian law.

**Important parameters / regimes.** N (selected output modes), K (squeezed inputs), M (physical mode count). The result is uniform for $N \le K \le M$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the pure, zero-displacement setting and specifically compares the ordinary transpose ($	ext{Y Y}^T$) rather than the Hermitian product ($	ext{Y Y}^*$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 summarizes the proof architecture combining ambient recursion, COE scores, and rectangular comparison. Other figures illustrate the Haar lift, Markov contraction, and density score evaluation steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the result by first establishing regularity and finite bounds for event paths (Lemma III.3), then deriving a one-step bound on the total variation distance ($	ext{dTV}(\mu_{m+1,K}, \mu_{m,K})$) via a Taylor decomposition (Proposition III.4), and finally telescoping this one-step bound to achieve the overall final bound.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gaussian boson sampling with equally squeezed active inputs assigns collision free probabilities through a complex symmetric transpose Gram matrix formed from a rectangular block of a Haar interferometer. We prove a finite total variation comparison with the corresponding complex Gaussian transpose Gram law. The error bound is explicit, quadratic in the number of selected output modes, inversely proportional to the interferometer size, and uniform in the number of squeezed active inputs. The proof combines a centered circular orthogonal ensemble score analysis in the dense regime with a rectangular relative entropy bound. This result supplies a random matrix replacement component of Gaussian boson sampling hardness arguments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a method to retrieve an input optical signal that has been encoded and propagated through a complex, coupled waveguide array. The retrieval mechanism relies on introducing a precise, engineered phase shift at the output section (decoder). Simulations confirm that this approach allows for high-fidelity signal reconstruction, suggesting applications in ultrafast coherent information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses coherent control and signal processing in structured photonic media, which has strong overlap with quantum optics and open quantum system dynamics, particularly regarding time-reversal concepts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is retrieving a phase-encrypted input optical signal with high fidelity after it has undergone complex, coherent propagation through an array of coupled waveguides.

**Main result.** The proposed scheme successfully demonstrates the retrieval of the input broadband signal using a localized phase-shifting mechanism in the decoder section of the waveguide array.

**Method.** The retrieval is achieved by applying a specific, engineered phase shift ($\pi$ shift in odd waveguides) at the decoder entrance, which theoretically causes a coherent inversion of the propagation evolution.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes an array of coupled dielectric waveguides, modeled by a discrete Schrödinger-type equation describing the evolution of the electric field components along the propagation direction (z).

**Key observables.** Global fidelity ($F_{gl}$) and phase fidelity ($F_{ph}$) are used to quantify the success of signal retrieval, alongside cross-sectional electric field distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include coupling strengths ($J_\ell$), dielectric permittivities ($\epsilon_{r1}, \epsilon_{sr}$), and the pulse characteristics ($\lambda = 1.55 \mu	ext{m}$, 18 fs duration).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ability to implement precise, engineered phase shifts at the decoder and assumes the weak-coupling regime for the waveguide array.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup of the coder and decoder, the evolution of the electric field magnitude through the system, and plots showing the fidelity versus propagation distance.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem in coupled waveguides, develops the theoretical framework using coupled-mode equations, proposes the phase-shifting retrieval concept, and validates the scheme using full-wave electromagnetic simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optical waveguides are key elements for high fidelity, long distance optical communications. Coupled waveguide arrays allow for higher information density, steerting the propagation direction, and for encoding information. However, due to the mixing of relative phases for short pulses containing multiple waveguide-mode frequencies, a process for retrieving an encoded input state once these signals undergo coherent propagation remains elusive. A concept is presented to extract with high fidelity the phase-encrypted input signal from spatio-temporal propagated states. As a realization, an array of coupled waveguides is suggested with the retrieval mechanism being realized by local phase shifts that comply with the identified retrieval concept. Three dimensional full-wave electromagnetic simulations for broadband optical signals in coupled dielectric waveguides confirm the validity of the scheme and the high fidelity of information retrieval pointing to potential applications, for instance in ultrafast coherent coding and decoding of information imprinted on pulse sequences.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes Majorana bound states in a superconductor-semiconductor nanowire coupled to an optical cavity. By solving the coupled electron-photon Hamiltonian, the authors show that the cavity interaction modifies the topological phase diagram, enabling the topological state to be accessed at lower external magnetic fields than previously thought possible.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly combines concepts from cavity QED (light-matter coupling) with topological superconductivity, which is a major area of research in AMO physics and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper theoretically investigates how coupling Majorana bound states (MBSs) in a superconductor-semiconductor heterostructure to a single-mode photonic cavity modifies the system's topological phase diagram.

**Main result.** Cavity coupling shifts the phase boundary to smaller Zeeman energies, and even weak coupling allows entering the topological phase at lower critical Zeeman energies than the uncoupled case.

**Method.** The authors derived an effective light-matter Hamiltonian and solved the coupled electron-photon system using exact diagonalization, high-frequency expansion, and mean-field decoupling.

**Model / system.** The model is a hybrid platform: a 1D quantum wire tunnel-coupled to an s-wave superconductor, all embedded within a single-mode photonic cavity. The coupling enters the effective superconducting pairing term.

**Key observables.** Topological phase boundary, gap closing, effective superconducting pairing strength.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Light-matter coupling strength (g), Zeeman energy ($V_Z$), number of photons (n).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The photonic vector potential is aligned along the tunneling direction, and the analysis uses approximations for the photon number (e.g., dark cavity limit or semiclassical limit).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the physical setup (wire-superconductor-cavity). Figure 2(a) shows the topological phase diagram dependence on $V_Z/\Delta_{eff}$ and $g$ for the dark cavity limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the coupled Hamiltonian, derives the effective wire-cavity Hamiltonian, and then applies various mathematical techniques (exact diagonalization, etc.) to calculate the phase diagram and analyze the impact of coupling strength.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically study a hybrid superconductor-semiconductor platform hosting Majorana bound states coupled to a single mode photonic cavity. Starting with a one-dimensional wire coupled to a bulk $s$-wave superconductor embedded in a photonic cavity, we derive an effective light-matter Hamiltonian for such a platform. Assuming that the photonic vector potential is aligned along the tunneling between a wire and a superconductor, we find that the cavity coupling enters only in the effective superconducting pairing term. By solving the coupled electron-photon Hamiltonian using different approaches, such as exact diagonalization in case of zero or large number of photons, high-frequency expansion, and mean-field decoupling, we find that the phase boundary between the topological trivial phases is shifted to smaller values of the Zeeman energy compared to the uncoupled case. Cavity embedding has the strongest effect on the phase diagram in the semiclassical regime, corresponding to a large number of photons. In all cases, we find that at large values of the light-matter coupling strength the effective superconducting pairing is suppressed, driving the system into the gapless phase. We demonstrate that even small light-matter coupling strength allows for entering the topological phase at values of the Zeeman energy compared to the uncoupled platform.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on using Aharonov-Bohm interferometry in graphene to directly probe the spin structure of fractional quantum Hall edge states. By coupling the target FQH edge to a spin-polarized probe, the researchers distinguish between spin-unpolarized and spin-polarized regimes of the $\nu=2/3$ state. This establishes edge transport as a key diagnostic tool for understanding spin-singlet topological orders.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to probe spin degrees of freedom in topological edge states using interferometry provides a powerful experimental tool for studying exotic quantum phases, which is highly relevant to understanding strongly correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to develop a spin-sensitive probe to characterize the spin-singlet topological order present in fractional quantum Hall (FQH) edge states, particularly the $\nu=2/3$ state.

**Main result.** The authors successfully identified the spin-unpolarized and spin-polarized regimes of the $\nu=2/3$ edge state using Aharonov-Bohm interferometry, confirming the existence of spin-charge separation effects.

**Method.** The primary method involves using a p-n junction to couple a target FQH edge state to a probe integer QH state, forming an Aharonov-Bohm interferometer whose interference pattern is spin-dependent.

**Model / system.** The system is graphene exhibiting FQH edge states. The theoretical analysis models the edge using boson fields and investigates the transition between $SU(2)$-symmetric (unpolarized) and broken symmetry (polarized) regimes.

**Key observables.** Aharonov-Bohm oscillation visibility, frequency components ($f_{nm}$), and the transition point in transmission ($\delta T$) between spin-unpolarized and spin-polarized states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factors ($\nu=2/3$), magnetic field ($B$), and the ratio of spin-up/spin-down coupling strengths.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interference pattern is governed by spin matching between the probe and target channels; the theoretical analysis assumes specific velocity orderings and negligible intra-channel interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate device schematics, AB oscillation measurements showing single/dual MZI frequencies, and theoretical models detailing the spin-charge separation and fractionalization into downstream/upstream components.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from experimental demonstration of spin-sensitive probing via AB interferometry in graphene to theoretical analysis of the resulting interference patterns, confirming spin-charge separation and fractionalization across different symmetry regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The edge modes of spin-singlet fractional quantum Hall (FQH) phases are manifestations of multicomponent topological order and SU(2) spin symmetry. An archetype is the spin-unpolarized state at $ν$=2/3, whose edge is expected to host spatially coexisting yet counter-propagating charge and neutral spin modes. Despite efforts, its edge properties, including spin coherence and spin-charge separation, have remained elusive owing to the difficulty of resolving spins in FQH edge transport. Here, we develop a spin-sensitive probe of graphene FQH edges by using a p-n junction to interface a target FQH state with a probe integer quantum Hall (QH) state of opposite polarity. An Aharonov-Bohm (AB) interferometer forms along the interface, when the target and probe edge channels carry the same spin. We identify the spin-unpolarized and polarized edges of the $ν$=2/3 states at lower and higher magnetic fields, respectively, through spin-dependent interference. A novel multiparticle AB interference between two electrons of opposite spin emerges from spin-charge separation and recombination on the unpolarized edge, featuring a spin swap. Our results establish edge transport as a probe of spin-singlet topological orders, with implications for parafermion platforms in graphene-superconductor hybrids.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes quantum transport in SSH chains under an electric field, revealing that Wannier-Stark ladders appear as distinct antiresonance signatures in electron transmission. Using the Global Matrix Method, the authors show these antiresonances are linked to exponentially localized states. This provides a general tool for studying such phenomena across various condensed matter and wave physics systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between topological features (SSH model) and transport phenomena (antiresonances) under a bias field is highly relevant to open quantum systems and engineered quantum transport devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper demonstrates that Wannier-Stark ladders (WSLs) in Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chains manifest as antiresonance signatures in electron transmission.

**Main result.** These WSL antiresonances are found to correspond to states that are exponentially localized at the potential's incident boundary, providing a unified framework for identifying such features.

**Method.** The Global Matrix Method (GMM) is employed to solve the Schrödinger equation for quantum transport across stratified electrostatic potentials.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum transport in a one-dimensional SSH chain subjected to a periodic potential profile combined with a uniform electric field.

**Key observables.** Electron transmission coefficient (T), reflection coefficient (R), and the relative pseudospin angle (gamma).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The electric field strength (F), hopping parameters (t, t'), and the energy (E) relative to the potential profile.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes wavefunctions are free of singularities when taking the limit epsilon -> 0.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SSH lattice structure, electronic band structure in junctions, and transmission spectra showing the characteristic antiresonances.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the SSH model and the problem setup, details the Global Matrix Method for calculating transmission, presents the main results showing the antiresonances, and concludes by generalizing the findings to other physical systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate that Wannier-Stark ladders (WSLs) in one-dimensional Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chains manifest as antiresonance signatures in the electron transmission. We study the quantum transport in the SSH chain in the presence of a periodic potential profile plus a uniform electric field. By employing the global matrix method with non-trivial matching conditions, we find that these WSL antiresonances correspond to states that are exponentially localized at the potential's incident boundary. These findings provide a robust, unified framework for identifying WSLs across diverse scales, ranging from electronic transport in trans-polyacetylene to classical-wave analogs in phononic crystals and surface water waves.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how to engineer multiple chiral Majorana modes in magnetic topological insulator heterostructures for topological quantum computing. By modeling the system using an extended 3D BHZ Hamiltonian, the authors show that magnetic effects and superconducting proximity can generate these robust channels. Furthermore, they establish that the superconducting phase difference acts as a key, tunable parameter controlling the number of accessible Majorana states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit inclusion of 3D confinement and the use of a full BdG Hamiltonian to control multiple channels makes this highly relevant for understanding engineered superconducting topological materials, bridging condensed matter theory with quantum information hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central goal is to achieve robust topological superconductivity featuring multiple chiral Majorana channels, which is crucial for scalable topological quantum computing.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that magnetic 3D topological insulator heterostructures can host multiple chiral Majorana modes, and the superconducting phase difference in a vertical junction can tune their effective multiplicity.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical modeling using an extended 3D Bernevig-Hughes-Zhang (BHZ) Hamiltonian within the Bogoliubov-de Gennes framework.

**Model / system.** The system is a magnetically doped 3D topological insulator heterostructure proximitized by an s-wave superconductor. The model explicitly incorporates vertical confinement and uses a complex 3D Hamiltonian containing terms for proximity pairing, Zeeman splitting, and orbital mixing.

**Key observables.** Local Majorana polarization (MP), Majorana polarization indicator ($\chi_{
u}(\Omega)$), minimum excitation gap ($E_{min}$), and the effective multiplicity of low-energy Majorana channels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic exchange coupling, orbital mixing strength, superconducting phase difference ($\phi$), and the degree of vertical confinement (3D vs 2D limits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of the 3D BHZ model and notes that the pair counting rule is ill-defined at $k_x=0$, necessitating the use of gauge-invariant diagnostics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the quantum-well origin of multiple chiral Majorana channels in the TI slab setup. Figure 10 summarizes the dependence of the minimum excitation gap and the pair count on the Josephson phase ($\phi$) and system size.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the full 3D Hamiltonian, analyzing the system in various limits (2D, quantum-well), deriving diagnostic tools like Majorana polarization, and finally demonstrating the tunability of the Majorana modes using a vertical Josephson junction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Achieving robust topological superconductivity with multiple Majorana channels is a key step for scalable topological quantum computing. To this end, we investigate magnetically doped three-dimensional topological insulator heterostructures proximitized by an s-wave superconductor within a fully three-dimensional extended Bernevig-Hughes-Zhang framework that explicitly accounts for vertical confinement. We show that magnetic exchange coupling, orbital mixing, and bulk band inversion cooperate to generate effective equal-spin p-wave pairing channels supporting multiple chiral Majorana modes. The number of Majorana channels is determined by the confined modes in the vertical direction and the high-Chern-number phases of the normal state, which allows us to derive an analytical criterion for the emergence of the multiple-Majorana topological superconducting phases. The chiral Majorana modes remain robust against strong disorder and moderate symmetry-breaking perturbations. We also demonstrate that a superconducting phase difference defining a vertical Josephson junction acts as a tunable parameter that controls the hybridization, minigap, and effective multiplicity of the low energy Majorana channels. Our results establish magnetic three-dimensional topological- insulator heterostructures as a promising platform for engineering multiple chiral Majorana modes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces 'geotronics,' a method that uses substrate topography to locally reorient anisotropic van der Waals materials like CrSBr. By conforming the material to patterned substrates, the authors demonstrate that a uniform external field can create spatially varying magnetic and electronic landscapes. This establishes substrate geometry as a powerful, non-invasive design principle for creating tunable quantum functionalities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to program quantum properties (like excitonic potentials or magnetic order) using purely geometric control, rather than chemical doping or junction fabrication, offers a novel platform for realizing tunable quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To achieve dynamic, on-demand, and position-dependent control over the electronic and magnetic structure of materials, moving beyond fixed properties set during fabrication.

**Main result.** The substrate topography can deterministically program local magnetic and electronic landscapes, creating switchable excitonic potential wells within a single, continuous crystal.

**Method.** The study uses the concept of 'geotronics,' where the mechanical conformability of anisotropic van der Waals materials is exploited by transferring them onto substrates with controlled topography (e.g., nanoindentation pits).

**Model / system.** The system studied is a bilayer of the antiferromagnetic semiconductor CrSBr. The key principle relies on the material's intrinsic anisotropy and mechanical compliance to the substrate geometry.

**Key observables.** Spatially resolved photoluminescence (PL) spectra, energy contrast ($\Delta E$) between flat and inclined regions, and local magnetic phase coexistence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local crystal tilt angle ($	heta \approx 10^\circ–20^\circ$), applied magnetic field ($B$), and the resulting excitonic energy contrast ($\sim 10–12 	ext{ meV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes the material is mechanically compliant enough to conform to the substrate topography and that the electronic/magnetic response is highly sensitive to local crystal orientation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept of geometronics using nanoindentation pits, showing PL spectra maps and line scans that demonstrate the field-dependent energy contrast and localized modulation of the excitonic potential.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of geometronics, demonstrates its feasibility using CrSBr on nanoindented substrates via PL spectroscopy, and concludes by establishing substrate topography as a universal design principle for programming electronic and magnetic functionalities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electronic structure in van der Waals materials is commonly engineered through composition, strain, electrostatic gating and heterostructure assembly. Here we introduce geometronics, a concept in which substrate geometry locally reorients an anisotropic crystal, transforming homogeneous external perturbation into programmable magnetic and electronic landscapes. We demonstrate this concept using a bilayer of the antiferromagnetic semiconductor CrSBr transferred onto an inverted pyramidal nanoindentation, where the local crystal orientation with respect to the external magnetic field drives the coexistence of antiferromagnetic and ferromagnetic phases within a single continuous crystal. The resulting magnetic landscape creates a switchable excitonic potential well of up to 10--12 meV, directly visualised by spatially resolved spectroscopy. More generally, geometronics provides a universal route for deterministically programmed electronic and magnetic landscapes without modifying the material itself. It therefore establishes substrate topography as a new design principle that exploits the intrinsic anisotropy of layered van der Waals materials.

</details>

**Authors:** Ivo A. Gabrovski, Louk Rademaker  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00146v1>

**Summary.** This work bridges the gap between the simple Landau level spectrum and the complex Hofstadter butterfly by tuning the strength of a periodic potential. The authors show that the butterfly emerges from broadened Landau 'caterpillars' through specific topological transitions. This has implications for understanding electronic properties in engineered systems like moiré materials under strong magnetic fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between magnetic quantization (Landau levels) and lattice periodicity (Hofstadter) is fundamental to understanding artificial quantum materials like moiré systems, making it highly relevant for condensed matter theorists.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to connect the two distinct limits of electron spectra in 2D: the discrete Landau levels (in a strong magnetic field) and the fractal Hofstadter butterfly spectrum (in a periodic potential).

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the butterfly spectrum emerges from broadened Landau 'caterpillars' by continuously increasing the periodic potential strength, revealing topological transitions along the way.

**Method.** The study employs numerical exact diagonalization, Wannierization procedures, and analysis of topological invariants like Chern numbers to track the spectral evolution.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by 2D electrons subjected to both a perpendicular magnetic field and a periodic potential, described by a Hamiltonian that transitions between the Landau gauge and a tight-binding form.

**Key observables.** Spectrum evolution (caterpillars to butterflies), Chern numbers, and the flux-dependent hopping parameters of the effective tight-binding model.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnetic flux $\phi = B a^2 / \Phi_0$, the periodic potential strength $U_0$, and the ratio of energy scales ($\hbar\omega_c$ vs. $E_U$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on the assumption of rational fluxes ($\phi = p/q$) to define a finite magnetic unit cell for diagonalization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral evolution from Landau levels to butterflies (Fig. 1), the periodic potential structure (Fig. 2), and the resulting effective 1D hopping chains.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the continuum Hamiltonian, then developing methods using magnetic translation operators for rational fluxes, and finally deriving an effective tight-binding model whose parameters depend critically on the magnetic flux.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is well-known that the spectrum of two-dimensional electrons in a perpendicular magnetic field is given by discrete flat Landau levels. By contrast, electrons in a two-dimensional tight-binding model give rise to a fractal Hofstadter butterfly spectrum. In this paper, we connect these two opposite limits by showing how a butterfly spectrum emerges from broadened Landau `caterpillars', by continuously increasing the periodic potential strength. We identify a series of topological transitions that isolate a lowest trivial band, a necessary condition for the butterfly to emerge. The resulting butterfly is topologically distinct from the Hofstadter butterfly at fluxes $φ>1$, due to anomalous behavior of diagonal hopping. Moreover, the hopping parameters of an effective tight-binding model, obtained by a Wannierization procedure at large magnetic field, are highly dependent on the flux, revealing that Wannier orbitals themselves change under the applied magnetic field. Our methods and results are relevant for artificial materials, such as moiré systems, where a full quantum of flux per lattice unit cell is experimentally accessible. On a theoretical level, having Wannierization methods for each specific flux allows more accurate many-body calculations in large magnetic fields.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines complex soliton solutions—specifically helical and straight multi-core structures—formed by vortex beams propagating in a cubic-quintic nonlinear medium. By analyzing the impact of off-axis phase singularities and applying external torques, the authors demonstrate precise control over the soliton's structure and propagation characteristics. This is significant for understanding advanced light manipulation in nonlinear optical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear optics and soliton physics, providing detailed theoretical predictions about complex, structured light beams (vortex solitons) whose dynamics are controlled by geometric parameters (off-axis pivots) and external forces (torque).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the existence and dynamics of solitons propagating along helical and straight trajectories induced by vortex beams with off-axis pivots in cubic-quintic nonlinear media.

**Main result.** The research predicts stable multi-ring, multi-core, and/or multi-split soliton structures, demonstrating that the helicity and propagation channels can be precisely tuned by applying an external torque.

**Method.** The analysis involves solving the nonlinear Schrödinger equation numerically, utilizing linear stability analysis (eigenvalue problems) to confirm stability, and analyzing energy flow via the Poynting vector.

**Model / system.** The system models optical beams propagating in a cubic-quintic (CQ) nonlinear medium, characterized by a single- or multi-ring potential. The dynamics are governed by the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation (NLSE) incorporating the effects of off-axis vortex beam pivots.

**Key observables.** Soliton structure (multi-ring, multi-core, split), helicity sign/period, linear eigenvalue spectra, and energy flow density.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Potential depth ($V_0$), propagation constant ($eta$), topological charge ($m$), and applied angular velocity ($\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of the CQ nonlinearity model and relies on solving the linearized eigenvalue problem for stability assessment.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concentric ring potentials, the field amplitude and Poynting vector for off-axis solitons, comparisons of power and stability for on-axis vs. off-axis states, and the field patterns under applied rotation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the governing NLSE, analyzing the effect of off-axis pivots on single-ring solitons, extending the analysis to multi-ring potentials, and finally demonstrating the co-existence and tunability of helical and straight propagation channels via external torque.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We address the existence and dynamics of solitons propagating along helical (spiral-rotating) and straight trajectories in the bulk waveguide with the cubic-quintic nonlinearity and a single- or multi-ring potential. The input is taken as a vortex beam with a single or multiple phase singularities (pivots), displaced from the waveguide's axis. The single off-axis pivot creates a one-ring helical soliton, with a closed or open (split) ring structure. The vortex beams with multiple off-center pivots give rise to complex states with multi-ring, multi-core, and/or multi-split structures. The multi-core/split solitons propagate along helical channels, or straight ones, which are parallel to the propagation axis. The sign and period of the helicity can be precisely adjusted by applying a torque with an appropriate angular velocity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the successful development of a cryogenic current comparator (CCC) that operates using helium gas instead of liquid helium. The system maintains high measurement precision, comparable to liquid-helium standards, and is successfully integrated with a Quantum Hall Resistance standard. This advancement significantly improves the practicality and sustainability of high-precision resistance metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is metrology, the engineering challenge of maintaining ultra-low noise, stable cryogenic environments using non-traditional cooling media (helium gas) is highly relevant to building complex quantum measurement apparatuses.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to develop and validate a high-precision cryogenic current comparator (CCC) system that operates without relying on liquid helium, thereby reducing operational costs and logistical constraints.

**Main result.** The helium-gas-cooled CCC achieved metrological performance comparable to liquid-helium cooling, successfully integrating with a Quantum Hall Resistance (QHR) standard with uncertainties reaching the nΩ/Ω level.

**Method.** The authors utilized a custom helium-gas chamber cooled by a cryogen-free dilution refrigerator to cool the CCC probe, allowing for precision resistance-ratio measurements.

**Model / system.** The system involves a CCC probe and a QHR standard integrated into a dilution refrigerator. Cooling is achieved via a helium gas medium in a custom chamber, which is thermally anchored to the 4 K stage.

**Key observables.** Type-A uncertainty ($1 	ext{ n}\Omega/\Omega$), SQUID $\phi-V$ characteristics, resistance-ratio measurements, and stage temperatures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature (4 K stage), helium gas pressure (low-pressure regime), and measurement time (e.g., 100 s averaging time).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The performance is assumed to be comparable to liquid-helium cooling, and thermal anchoring is crucial for minimizing heat load.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the physical setup, including the gas chamber and thermal anchors. Other figures show the dependence of SQUID amplitude and stage temperatures on helium gas pressure, and plots of resistance ratios and Allan deviations over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the engineering challenges of cryogen-free operation, presents the methodology for cooling the CCC with helium gas, validates the performance against liquid helium standards, and finally demonstrates the integration with a QHR standard.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report a cryogen-free cryogenic current comparator (CCC) system for precision resistance measurements. A helium-gas chamber was developed and installed on the 4 K stage of a cryogen-free dilution refrigerator equipped with a pulse-tube cryocooler. The CCC probe was housed in this chamber and was cooled through helium gas serving as a heat exchange medium. The metrological performance of this helium-gas-cooled CCC was evaluated through precision resistance-ratio measurements and found to be comparable to that obtained under liquid-helium cooling. A quantum Hall resistance (QHR) device was also integrated into the same refrigerator, enabling QHR/100~$Ω$ resistance-ratio measurements. The type-A uncertainty reached the 1~n$Ω$/$Ω$ level within an averaging time of 100~s, and the resulting resistance-ratio measurements agreed well with conventional liquid-helium-based measurements at the level of a few n$Ω$/$Ω$. The system provides this level of measurement performance while consuming less than 1~L of helium gas per thermal cycle.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally and theoretically characterizes electron tunneling in twisted double bilayer graphene. It reveals that the observed current features, such as resonant peaks and steps, are governed by the interplay between like-band and unlike-band tunneling pathways. The results highlight that the symmetry and magnitude of these features are highly sensitive to the twist angle and the relative doping of the graphene layers.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong dependence of tunneling features on band structure intersections and polarization effects provides a rich platform for studying quantum transport in engineered 2D materials, relevant to open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the electron tunneling characteristics in twisted double bilayer graphene (BLG) heterostructures.

**Main result.** The tunneling current exhibits distinct features—resonant peaks and steps—whose nature (symmetric vs. asymmetric) depends critically on the relative doping levels and the twist angle.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements of current-voltage (I-V) characteristics with detailed theoretical modeling based on electron dispersion intersections.

**Model / system.** The system is a twisted double bilayer graphene heterostructure studied in a dual-gate architecture. The theory models tunneling current by considering the competition between like-band (conductivity-conductivity) and unlike-band (conductivity-valence) electron tunneling contributions.

**Key observables.** Current-voltage (I-V) characteristics, zero-bias tunneling conductance maps, resonant peaks, steps, and regions of negative differential resistance (NDR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), bias voltage ($V_b$), and relative doping levels (equal vs. opposite density).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Tunneling transitions conserve lateral electron momentum and energy; the model incorporates the competition between different band types and the effect of sublayer polarization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate I-V curves and 2D maps showing how tunneling features change with twist angle and doping asymmetry, distinguishing between symmetric and asymmetric regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the tunneling current by decomposing it into like-band and unlike-band components, comparing theoretical predictions with experimental data across varying twist angles and doping conditions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron tunneling between sheets of bilayer Bernal graphene twisted at different small angles was studied experimentally and theoretically. The current-voltage characteristics exhibit resonant peaks, steps, and regions of negative differential resistance, the origin of which is explained by the intersections of energy- and momentum-shifted electron dispersions of adjacent layers. A theoretical analysis of tunneling transport demonstrated that the key to understanding this phenomenon lies in the competition between two contributions: between like (conductivity-conductivity or valence-valence) and unlike (conductivity-valence) bands of parallel bilayer graphene sheets. A systematic evolution of the tunneling current patterns with increase of the twist angle is investigated. Polarization of electron wave function across graphene sublayers caused by displacement field within bilayer graphene is shown to strongly affect the tunneling probability, thus enhancing negative differential resistance due to Van Hove singularities at the band edges.

</details>

**Authors:** Chakshu Gupta

The field A (here the Orbium glider; brightness is the cell value, dark 0 to bright 1; the arrow marks the travel direction) is convolved with the ring kernel K (zero at the centre, peaking at half the kernel radius) to give the potential U = K ∗A, a local ring-average. The growth G(U) (red = +1, grow; blue = −1, decay) is then scaled by the time step dt and added to the field. The sum is clipped to [0, 1]. The growth is positive at the leading edge and negative at the trailing edge, together advancing the glider.</sub>

The output bit is read at the window on the signal’s outgoing track.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2609.01348_figures/2609.01348_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>

Head-on Orbium collisions give survival at b = 0, annihilation at b = 7, and a miss at b = 22; the fourth outcome, a merger, appears at b = 0 in the perpendicular geometry used in the gate (Section 3.2), and also head-on (Figure 4). Each panel is one simulator run at ϕ = 0.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2609.01348_figures/2609.01348_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 4: Head-on Orbium collision outcome, plotted horizontally over the impact parameter b and vertically over eight relative breathing phases ϕ spanning the cycle. The miss region beyond b = 20 is uniform across phases, and survival is the most common outcome. Within b ≤20, only two of the twenty-one columns hold one outcome across all eight phases; in the rest, survival, annihilation and merger interleave. Here b alone therefore does not fix the head-on outcome.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2609.01348_figures/2609.01348_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 5: The INHIBIT gate, shown as the max-over-time projection of the field so that each bright streak is a glider’s whole path. The signal enters from the top toward the dashed output window. With the control absent, the signal reaches the window, giving out = 1 (left); with the control present, the collision blocks the signal, giving out = 0 (right).</sub>
