**Summary.** This work demonstrates the construction of basic logic gates, such as INHIBIT, within the continuous cellular automaton Lenia using pattern collisions. While simple chains of gates were successfully built, the authors identify that general computation is blocked by the inability to reliably route a signal after a turn or to absorb residual energy from collisions. This highlights the gap between local logic implementation and global circuit integration.

**Why it may be interesting.** It explores computation in a continuous, non-linear physical system, drawing parallels to quantum field theories or non-equilibrium dynamics where pattern formation and interaction govern information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether collision-based computation, established in discrete automata and physical media, can be realized in continuous cellular automata like Lenia.

**Main result.** An INHIBIT logic gate was successfully constructed and demonstrated in series, forming an AND-NOT chain; however, the ability to route a deflected signal or absorb surviving gliders for general circuit composition remains unproven.

**Method.** The authors constructed logic gates by simulating the collision dynamics of self-propelling patterns (gliders) within the continuous Lenia update rule.

**Model / system.** The system is Lenia, a continuous cellular automaton where cell values evolve smoothly via a growth rule involving convolution. Computation relies on the interaction and collision outcomes of localized, self-sustaining patterns called 'gliders' (e.g., the Orbium glider).

**Key observables.** Collision outcomes (annihilate, explode, merge, survive/miss), gate output states (e.g., INHIBIT function), and signal persistence/deflection.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Breathing phases ($\phi$), impact parameters ($b$), and the specific Lenia update rule parameters ($dt$, kernel $K$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation is the inability to demonstrate signal delivery to a downstream gate or find a reusable absorber for surviving gliders.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Lenia update rule, the collision setup involving signal and control gliders, and maps showing collision outcomes across varying impact parameters and breathing phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the feasibility of basic logic gates (INHIBIT) using single collisions, to demonstrating simple sequential composition (AND-NOT chain), and finally identifying the critical physical gaps (signal delivery and absorption) preventing universal computation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous cellular automata such as Lenia spontaneously produce lifelike, self-propelling patterns, including the Orbium glider, which travels in a straight line while pulsing through a fixed breathing cycle. Collision-based logic, where moving patterns compute by colliding, is established in discrete cellular automata and continuous physical media. Within continuous cellular automata, computation has so far been trained into the rule rather than emerging from collisions, and whether a fixed-rule automaton like Lenia can support general collision-based computation remains open. This paper constructs an INHIBIT gate from collisions of the Orbium glider. Of the patterns searched across four continuous-CA rule types, the Orbium glider is the only one shown to survive a collision with both copies intact. A control glider deflects a signal glider off its output line, so the output carries a signal only when no control is present. The gate blocks across all twenty-four phases of the breathing cycle and nine integer offsets of the control. Two such gates in series, with one signal line and two controls, compose into an AND-NOT chain, correct on all eight input combinations. By contrast, routing a signal beyond that single chain is undemonstrated. A deflected signal is not restored to a fixed landing position, and no reusable absorber for the surviving gliders was found. The immediate open question for collision-based computation in Lenia therefore narrows from whether a gate exists to whether a deflected signal can be delivered to a downstream gate, the next requirement for composing the gate beyond a single straight chain.

</details>

For thin-film Nb, the GL coherence length (orange shaded region) is expected to be reduced due to dimensional confinement [63–66], and likewise the local modulation of the BCS gap increases (black dashed) (the latter due to a reduction in kF ). **Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the effect of dynamical impurities, such as localized vibrational modes, on s-wave superconductors. It derives a closed-form solution for the induced subgap bound state energy and predicts characteristic modulations in the local density of states. The findings show that these dynamic defects create distinct signatures that differ from those caused by static disorder.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a concrete theoretical framework for understanding how coupling to low-energy bosonic modes (like phonons or TLSs) can create observable spectroscopic signatures in superconductors, which is relevant to understanding superconducting mechanisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how local, non-magnetic dynamical impurities (like vibron modes) induce subgap bound states and modify the local density of states (LDOS) in s-wave superconductors.

**Main result.** Dynamical impurities induce distinct subgap bound states whose energy depends on the impurity's frequency, and they cause a measurable suppression of the LDOS compared to static disorder.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Nambu-Gor’kov Green's function formalism and the T-matrix approximation to solve the self-consistent Lippmann–Schwinger equation for the impurity scattering potential.

**Model / system.** The system is an s-wave superconductor modeled with an impurity potential derived from a dynamical excitation (vibron/TLS). The analysis distinguishes between static ($	au_3$) and dynamical ($	au_0$) impurity coupling.

**Key observables.** Subgap bound state energy ($\omega^*$), Local Density of States (LDOS) modulation $N(\omega; R_i)$, and the superconducting gap $\Delta(R_i)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Vibron frequency ($\omega_V$), spectral width ($\epsilon$), superconducting gap ($\Delta$), and the electron-boson coupling constant ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the T-matrix approach, often using the elastic scattering limit approximation, and assumes the static contribution is negligible compared to the dynamical $	au_0$ potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the induction of subgap states by dynamical impurities, show the dependence of $\omega^*$ on $\omega_V$, and map the LDOS modulation for both static and dynamic impurity types.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the T-matrix solution for off-diagonal (inelastic) scattering, derives the LDOS modulation formulas for both $	au_3$ and $	au_0$ impurities, and compares the results across different scattering regimes (elastic vs. inelastic).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We find that a single non-magnetic dynamical impurity (e.g., a low-frequency localized "vibron" mode) induces a subgap bound state in an s-wave superconductor. A closed-form solution for the bound state energy is found as a function of the vibron frequency in the elastic scattering limit, with the salient features of the bound state energies unaffected by inelastic scattering processes in the sub-THz regime. In addition to subgap features in the low-frequency local density of states, such impurities result in real space regions of reduced spectroscopic weight outside the gap peak.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, machine learning-enabled framework for Process-Technology Co-optimization (PTCO) applied to 2D TMD FETs. It introduces a new diagnostic metric, Transition Voltage ($	ext{V}_{	ext{Trans}}$), and uses GPR alongside multi-objective ranking to predict device performance from limited experimental data. The framework proves its utility by accurately guiding the optimization of gate stack recipes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on semiconductor device physics, the core methodology—using ML to map complex, high-dimensional process parameter spaces to performance metrics—is highly relevant to modeling complex quantum or many-body systems where experimental characterization is difficult.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of optimizing the fabrication process and technology parameters (PTCO) for 2D TMD FETs, as performance is highly sensitive to process variations and existing ML methods often rely on incomplete simulation data.

**Main result.** The developed ML-enabled PTCO framework successfully guides gate-stack optimization by predicting performance for untested recipes, confirming that device performance is governed by complex interactions among process parameters.

**Method.** The authors developed an experimental data-driven framework combining automated metric extraction, a Fuzzy TOPSIS multi-objective ranking scheme, and Gaussian Process Regression (GPR) for predictive modeling.

**Model / system.** The physical system is 2D transition metal dichalcogenide (TMD) Field-Effect Transistors (FETs). The optimization targets the gate stack recipe, which is defined by 7 key fabrication parameters.

**Key observables.** Key observables include the on-current ($	ext{I}_{	ext{ON}}$), Subthreshold Swing (SS), and a novel Transition Voltage ($	ext{V}_{	ext{Trans}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization is governed by the interaction of 7 key fabrication parameters defining the gate stack recipes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that performance can be accurately modeled and predicted from statistically meaningful experimental data, allowing for iterative refinement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the need for $	ext{V}_{	ext{Trans}}$ in 2D devices, the extraction process for $	ext{V}_{	ext{Trans}}$, and comparisons showing how the ML-predicted ranking aligns with experimental validation, emphasizing coupled parameter effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the PTCO framework, details the extraction of $	ext{V}_{	ext{Trans}}$ alongside SS and $	ext{I}_{	ext{ON}}$, utilizes Fuzzy TOPSIS for multi-objective ranking, and validates the predictive power of the ML model using experimental feedback.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the first experimental machine learning (ML)-enabled Process-Technology Co-Optimization (PTCO) framework for optimizing 2D transition metal dichalcogenide (TMD) FET fabrication directly from statistically meaningful experimental data rather than pure simulation data. We first introduce a transition voltage metric, VTrans, to quantify the gate voltage required for off-to-on switching and reveal its direct correlation with subthreshold swing (SS), highlighting an overlooked switching characteristic that governs both off-state and on-state performance. By integrating automated metric extraction, multi-objective recipe ranking, and predictive modeling, our framework uncovers hidden process-performance correlations and predicts the performance of unexplored fabrication recipes from limited experimental data. Experimental validation shows close agreement with ML predictions, thus demonstrating the framework's ability to efficiently guide gate stack optimization through iterative experimental feedback.

</details>

**Authors:** Jian-Feng Zhang, Zhong-Yi Lu, Tao Xiang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00586v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

(c) Effects of self-doping and interlayer hopping, isolated by fixing δsd or the interlayer hopping parameters at their ambient-pressure values.</sub>

</details>

<sub>FIG. 3. Ni-dx2−y2-resolved electronic and magnetic responses at ambient and optimal pressures. (a,b) Zero-energy spectral function Ax(k, 0) on the kz = 0 and π planes at (a) 0 and (b) 150 GPa. (c,d) Corresponding static intra-orbital spin susceptibility χx s(q, 0) on the qz = 0 and π planes. Pressure sharpens the Fermi-surface spectra and enhances their three-dimensionality, while the susceptibility remains peaked near (π, π) with weak qz dependence.</sub>

**Summary.** This theoretical study explains the superconducting dome in high-pressure nickelates by modeling the competition between kinetic energy increases and correlation effects. The pairing mechanism is found to be robustly $d$-wave, but the dome's shape is dictated by pressure-induced doping changes. It provides a unified microscopic picture for the material's unusual superconducting behavior under extreme compression.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cuprate/nickelate physics, the methodology—using correlated electron models (Hubbard-like terms) and pairing theory (Eliashberg)—is highly relevant to understanding unconventional superconductivity in strongly correlated materials, which often shares theoretical parallels with AMO systems exhibiting strong interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the microscopic origin of the broad, nonmonotonic, and robust superconducting dome observed in infinite-layer $	ext{La}_{0.8}	ext{Sr}_{0.2}	ext{NiO}_2$ under extreme high pressure.

**Main result.** The superconductivity is governed by a competition: initial enhancement from increasing kinetic energy and optimal coupling, followed by suppression due to pressure-induced self-doping into the overdoped regime.

**Method.** The study employs first-principles calculations combined with a two-orbital model and the FLEX approximation solved via the linearized Eliashberg equation to calculate the pairing tendency ($\lambda$).

**Model / system.** The system is the infinite-layer nickelate $	ext{La}_{0.8}	ext{Sr}_{0.2}	ext{NiO}_2$. The low-energy description focuses on the $	ext{Ni-}d_{x^2-y^2}$ orbital, treating the $d_{z^2}$ orbital's fluctuations as suppressed.

**Key observables.** The leading Eliashberg eigenvalue ($\lambda$), the spin susceptibility ($\chi_s^x(\mathbf{q}, 0)$), and the doping level ($\delta_{sd}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pressure (up to 210 GPa), the ratio of correlation strength to hopping ($U_x/t_1$), and the kinetic energy scale ($t_1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a low-energy effective two-band Hamiltonian, and the pairing mechanism is assumed to be dominated by the $d_{x^2-y^2}$ orbital.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate DFT/Wannier dispersions and hopping processes under pressure, showing how $U_x/t_1$ and self-doping change. A key figure shows the Eliashberg eigenvalue ($\lambda$) dependence on pressure, confirming the dome structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the experimental observation (the superconducting dome). It then develops a theoretical framework using DFT/Wannier functions and calculates the pairing strength using FLEX/Eliashberg theory. The core argument links the pressure dependence of electronic parameters ($t_1, U_x/t_1, \delta_{sd}$) to the observed superconducting dome shape.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent transport measurements on freestanding La$_{0.8}$Sr$_{0.2}$NiO$_2$ membranes revealed a broad superconducting dome extending from ambient pressure to 210 GPa, with an onset transition temperature reaching 74.5 K near 146 GPa. Using first-principles calculations, a pressure-dependent two-orbital model, and self-consistent FLEX calculations combined with the linearized Eliashberg equation, we determine how compression modifies the pairing tendency. Pressure increases the kinetic-energy scale, reduces $U_x/t_1$, strengthens interlayer hybridization, and transfers holes from the La/Sr-derived charge reservoir to the correlated Ni sector. Within the present low-energy description, the increasing kinetic scale and the approach to optimal intermediate coupling account for the initial enhancement of pairing, whereas pressure-induced self-doping into the overdoped regime is primarily responsible for its high-pressure suppression. Despite a pronounced three-dimensionalization of the Fermi surface, the pairing-relevant spin susceptibility remains weakly dependent on $q_z$ and peaked near $(π,π)$. Consequently, the Ni-$d_{x^2-y^2}$-dominated $d$-wave pairing state remains stable over the calculated pressure range. These results provide a unified microscopic interpretation of both the superconducting dome and its unusual robustness under megabar compression.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work explains the unexpected, reentrant formation of polar nanoregions in SrTiO3 by modeling the coupling between polar and antiferrodistortive lattice fluctuations. The key mechanism involves the hybridization of these two modes, which drives a finite-momentum instability at intermediate temperatures. This provides a fluctuation-based explanation for the PNRs, avoiding the need for a conventional phase transition.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism relies on coupling between different types of lattice fluctuations (polar and AFD), which is a concept relevant to understanding emergent order in complex quantum materials, bridging concepts from soft matter and condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the anomalous, reentrant-like weakening of local polar textures (PNRs) in quantum paraelectric SrTiO3 upon cooling, which contradicts the expectation that ordering tendencies strengthen as thermal fluctuations are suppressed.

**Main result.** The reentrant PNR behavior is explained by the interplay between intrinsic polar and antiferrodistortive (AFD) fluctuations, where mode hybridization causes a finite-q instability at intermediate temperatures, leading to PNR formation.

**Method.** The authors employ a self-consistent phase-field theory incorporating coupled ferroelectric and AFD fluctuations to model the system's free energy and mode dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum paraelectric SrTiO3, modeled using an effective Hamiltonian that includes coupled polar (P) and AFD (Q) fluctuations. The theory analyzes the interplay between these two modes via an interaction term, $V_{	ext{int}}$.

**Key observables.** Polar order parameter ($P_0(T)$), AFD order parameter ($Q_0(T)$), hybridized collective-mode dispersions ($\omega_P, \omega_Q$), and real-space polar correlation functions $C(r,T)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The crossing temperature of the polar and AFD modes ($\approx 52 	ext{ K}$), which maximizes hybridization and triggers the instability; the strain level ($	ext{0.02\%}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reentrant behavior is interpreted as a thermal crossover in fluctuation organization, not a thermodynamic phase transition, as the lower hybridized mode remains gapped.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature dependence of order parameters, mode dispersions, and real-space visualizations of PNRs, showing the nonmonotonic evolution of correlations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the anomalous PNR behavior observed experimentally. It then develops a phase-field theory coupling polar and AFD modes. The core argument shows that mode crossing leads to a finite-q instability (Brazovskii-type), causing PNRs, which subsequently weaken as the modes decouple at lower temperatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent real-space imaging in quantum paraelectric SrTiO$_3$ [Nature 656, 54 (2026)] reveals that local polar textures do not continuously grow upon cooling, but reach a maximum intensity at intermediate temperatures around 60-65~K and weaken again toward the quantum paraelectric ground state. Such a reentrant-like weakening of local polar textures challenges the conventional paradigm in which ordering tendencies generally strengthen as thermal fluctuations are suppressed. Here we employ a self-consistent phase-field theory showing that this anomalous behavior naturally arises from the interplay between intrinsic polar and antiferrodistortiv (AFD) fluctuations. We demonstrate that flexoelectric-like coupling strongly hybridizes the polar and AFD modes. As the uncoupled polar and AFD modes cross near 52~K, their hybridization is maximized, driving the lower hybridized branch to develop a minimum on a finite-wave-vector shell. This finite-$q$ softening triggers a Brazovskii-type instability, strongly enhancing polarization correlations and producing nanoscale polar textures. Away from the crossing temperature, the two modes become increasingly detuned, weakening their hybridization and the associated finite-$q$ softening. These results reaveal the origin of the formation of polar nanoregions in SrTiO$_3$, naturally explaining the unexpected confinement to an intermediate-temperature window and providing a mechanism beyond the quantum-fluctuation-based interpretation suggested by experiment. Furthermore, we predict an unconventional reentrant-like sequence in weakly strained SrTiO$_3$, evolving from ferroelectric to paraelectric, polar-nanoregion, and eventually paraelectric regimes upon heating from zero temperature.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates superconducting proximity effects in semiconductor/superconductor hybrids by measuring quantum oscillations in a buried InAs quantum well. By analyzing the SdH oscillations under various superconducting films, the authors determine the intrinsic normal-state parameters of the semiconductor. This technique overcomes the ambiguity caused by the superconducting metal shunt, providing crucial data for understanding interface physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology of using quantum oscillations to probe parameters inaccessible via standard tunneling spectroscopy in superconducting hybrids provides a powerful, complementary diagnostic tool for studying superconducting interfaces and proximity effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is accurately characterizing the electronic parameters of a semiconductor quantum well when it is buried beneath a superconducting metal film, as the metal shorts the electrical transport.

**Main result.** The study successfully determines the normal-state parameters (density, mass, g-factor) of the buried semiconductor using quantum oscillations, showing that the superconducting metal induces a distinct interface subband without altering the core parameters of the well.

**Method.** The analysis relies on measuring Shubnikov-de Haas (SdH) oscillations in the presence of superconducting films, complemented by a Dingle analysis that explicitly accounts for the shunt resistance.

**Model / system.** The system is a superconductor-semiconductor hybrid, specifically an InAs quantum well placed beneath various superconducting metals (Al, Sn, Nb, etc.). The physics involves analyzing the superconducting proximity effect and the resulting interface subband formation.

**Key observables.** Shubnikov-de Haas oscillation frequencies, oscillation amplitudes (analyzed via Dingle fitting), cyclotron masses, and g-factors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quantum lifetime ($	au_q$), superconducting gap ($\Delta_0$), quasiparticle weight ($Z$), and the coupling strength ($\Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the SdH oscillations of the buried well remain measurable despite the shunt, and that the measured parameters can be separated from the superconducting renormalization effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the buried well and the metal-induced interface subband, and show the SdH oscillation envelopes for both the fundamental and interface branches fitted using Dingle analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of shunt-induced ambiguity, presents the measurement technique using multiple superconducting films, analyzes the resulting quantum oscillations to extract parameters, and concludes by comparing these findings to predictions from tunneling spectroscopy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconductor-semiconductor hybrids host emergent states of matter and offer a platform for new qubits, but the superconducting metal shunts electrical transport, which rules out conventional semiconductor characterization and leaves the hybrid parameters to speculation. Here we determine density, mass, $g$-factor, mobility and subband occupation beneath the superconductor, from Shubnikov-de Haas oscillations of a buried InAs quantum well under Al, Sn, V, Nb, Ta and Re films, with a Dingle analysis that accounts for the shunt. Every metal adds an interface subband whose occupation falls into one of two classes, whereas the mass and $g$-factor of the buried well are unchanged to within 10\%. Within the uncertainty set by the transport mobility, no film shortens the quantum lifetime of the buried well, and Al and Sn lengthen it. Quantum lifetimes bound the hybridization of the interface subband to 2-4~meV. These measurements supply the normal-state parameters that tunnelling spectroscopy renormalizes but cannot measure.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces anti-higher-order topological insulators (anti-HOTIs) as the dual counterpart to conventional topological insulators. By analyzing a bilayer Chern insulator model, the authors demonstrate that anti-HOTIs exhibit a unique state distribution where edge states exist but corner states are suppressed. This work establishes anti-topology as a new theoretical framework for topological materials research.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of anti-topology provides a new framework for classifying topological phases, which is highly relevant to understanding boundary physics in condensed matter systems, potentially leading to novel material designs.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need to explore the dual counterpart of topological states, introducing the concept of anti-topological states and anti-higher-order topological insulators (anti-HOTIs).

**Main result.** The authors establish anti-topology as a new paradigm, demonstrating that anti-HOTIs host states along most boundaries but are characteristically absent at the corners, unlike conventional HOTIs.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical modeling based on bilayer Chern insulator Hamiltonians and analyzes the topological invariant defined by the charges enclosed by the Band Inversion Surfaces (BIS).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a bilayer Hamiltonian applied to a Chern insulator, incorporating components derived from a modified Qi–Wu–Zhang (QWZ) model. The analysis is performed on nanoribbons and nanoflakes.

**Key observables.** The topological invariant is the set of topological charges enclosed by the BIS; key observables include the gap magnitude and the spatial distribution of edge/anti-corner states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the Chern numbers enclosed by the BIS at high-symmetry points ($\Gamma, X, Y, M$), and the system parameters $B$ and $J$ in the phase diagram analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system preserves inversion symmetry, which helps pin the topological charges at specific high-symmetry points in the Brillouin Zone.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic difference between HOT (corner states) and anti-HOT (anti-corner states), show band structures of nanoribbons at varying angles, and present phase diagrams mapping the anti-HOT phase based on enclosed topological charges.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of anti-topology via duality, develops the model using a bilayer Hamiltonian, calculates the topological invariant using BIS analysis, and finally demonstrates the anti-HOT phase through numerical simulations and phase diagram construction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Duality is a fundamental concept in physics that connects complementary opposites like particles and holes. Similarly, while topological states in topological insulators localize at boundaries, the existence and nature of their dual counterparts remain unexplored. Here, we introduce anti-topological states as the dual of topological states, exemplified by anti-higher-order topological insulators. Unlike higher-order topological insulators, where states localize at corners, anti-higher-order topological insulators host states along edges but absent at corners, realizing an inverted distribution of states. We demonstrate this phenomenon in a bilayer Chern insulator with opposite Chern numbers, where the band inversion surfaces enclose distinct high-symmetry points. The topological invariant distinguishing these phases is given by the topological charges enclosed by band inversion surfaces. This work establishes anti-topology as a new paradigm, opening a chapter in topological research.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the observation of large, highly reversible magnetostriction in the kagome antiferromagnet YMn6Sn6 at room temperature. The mechanism is determined to originate from the competition between different magnetic exchange interactions, which drive the system from a helical to a collinear magnetic state. This establishes kagome helimagnets as a promising, low-dissipation platform for magnetomechanical applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the concept of field-induced, reversible lattice strain driven by competing quantum interactions (exchange coupling) provides a model for understanding controllable, low-dissipation coupling mechanisms relevant to quantum sensing or actuation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses the need for high-performance magnetostrictive materials, specifically by systematically exploring large magnetostriction in antiferromagnets, which are often overlooked compared to ferromagnets.

**Main result.** The kagome antiferromagnet YMn6Sn6 exhibits large, nearly hysteresis-free magnetostriction (exceeding 400 ppm) at room temperature, which is attributed to the competition between intralayer and interlayer exchange interactions.

**Method.** The study combines extensive experimental measurements (magnetostriction, XRD, STEM) with first-principles theoretical calculations (DFT) to model the magnetoelastic coupling.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the kagome helimagnet YMn6Sn6, characterized by competing magnetic exchange interactions (intralayer FM vs. interlayer coupling) that stabilize a noncollinear helical magnetic ground state.

**Key observables.** Large anisotropic and volume magnetostriction ($\sim 300-400$ ppm), field-induced magnetic phase transitions (helical to collinear), and the degree of magnetostrictive reversibility.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature (300 K), applied magnetic field up to 9 T, and the ratio/competition strength between intralayer and interlayer exchange interactions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The large magnetostriction is primarily driven by exchange-interaction-mediated spin structure reconstruction rather than conventional Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC) effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure, XRD patterns, STEM images confirming homogeneity, and detailed plots showing anisotropic strain responses ($\lambda_{\parallel}$ vs. $\lambda_{\perp}$) correlated with magnetic field-induced phase transitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the technological need, presents structural characterization, details the magnetic phase transitions under field, quantifies the anisotropic magnetostriction, and finally uses DFT calculations to prove the exchange-driven nature of the magnetoelastic coupling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The pursuit of high-performance magnetostrictive materials is crucial for advancing technologies in sensing, actuation, and microelectromechanical systems. Although pronounced magnetostrictive effects have been observed in a few ferromagnets, a systematic exploration of magnetostriction across a broader range of antiferromagnets remains limited. Here, we report the observation of large magnetostriction in the kagome antiferromagnet YMn6Sn6. Under a magnetic field, the system undergoes a field-induced evolution from a helical magnetic ground state toward a collinear field-polarized state, accompanied by a large anisotropic lattice strain and a substantial volume magnetostriction exceeding 400 ppm at room temperature. Remarkably, the magnetostrictive response remains nearly fully reversible up to 9 T with nearly hysteresis-free behavior, effectively minimizing the energy dissipation commonly associated with domain-wall pinning. Combined experimental measurements and theoretical calculations reveal that the large, nearly hysteresis-free magnetostriction originates from the competition between intralayer ferromagnetic and interlayer exchange interactions. This exchange-driven magnetoelastic coupling further gives rise to a strongly direction-dependent lattice distortion pathway. This work establishes kagome helimagnets as a tunable platform for low-dissipation magnetoelastic functionalities and responsive magnetomechanical applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the superconducting diode effect in Ising superconductors due to field-induced s+if pairing. By developing a two-component Ginzburg-Landau theory, the authors demonstrate that the SDE is governed by the coupling between singlet and triplet channels. This effect provides a novel transport measurement to detect the presence of hidden triplet pairing states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling between magnetic fields, spin-orbit coupling, and superconducting symmetry breaking provides a rich platform for studying emergent quantum phenomena in condensed matter systems, relevant to understanding topological superconductivity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the superconducting diode effect (SDE) arising from field-induced s+if pairing in Ising superconductors.

**Main result.** The SDE is shown to be a transport probe for the hidden triplet pairing channel, with the diode response strength controlled by the ratio of Zeeman to spin-orbit energies ($\Delta Z/\Delta 	ext{so}$).

**Method.** The authors construct and analyze a symmetry-constrained two-component Ginzburg-Landau theory for coupled singlet and triplet order parameters, deriving coefficients from a microscopic model.

**Model / system.** The system is an Ising superconductor (e.g., monolayer TMDs) subjected to an in-plane magnetic field, which induces an s+if condensate character via triplet Cooper pairs.

**Key observables.** Superconducting Diode Effect ($\eta_{	ext{SDE}}$), Lifshitz invariants, and the superconducting order parameters ($\psi$ and $\mathbf{d}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zeeman energy ($\Delta Z$), spin-orbit energy ($\Delta 	ext{so}$), and the ratio $\Delta Z/\Delta 	ext{so}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the GL theory framework and the assumption that the triplet sector can be integrated out to yield an effective single-component theory.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 illustrates the temperature and field dependence of the diode efficiency, showing the triplet channel extends the diode regime beyond the singlet-only critical field.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical mechanism (SOC leading to triplet pairing and Lifshitz invariants) to constructing the full GL theory, deriving effective coefficients, and finally analyzing the resulting SDE behavior under varying field strengths.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The in-plane critical field of Ising superconductors exceeds the Pauli limit by an order of magnitude because the Ising spin-orbit coupling locks the electron spins out of the basal plane. The same locking converts an in-plane Zeeman field into a source of equal-spin triplet Cooper pairs, so that the field-driven condensate acquires an $s+if$ character. We show that this conversion channel also generates Lifshitz invariants, the odd-in-momentum terms of the Ginzburg-Landau expansion responsible for the superconducting diode effect. When the basal mirror symmetry of the monolayer is lifted by a substrate or a gate, the field-induced triplets couple linearly to the Cooper-pair momentum, and an intrinsic diode response develops whose strength is set by the ratio of the Zeeman and spin-orbit energies rather than by the small ratio of the spin-orbit and Fermi energies familiar from parity-mixing mechanisms. We construct the symmetry-constrained two-component Ginzburg-Landau theory of the coupled singlet and triplet order parameters and derive all of its coefficients from the microscopic model of an Ising superconductor; the complete functional, including all gradient and quartic terms, is generated by a single pair-breaking function of temperature, field, and Cooper-pair momentum. An attractive triplet channel reshapes the diode response: at weak fields it suppresses the efficiency through destructive interference between the direct and the collective-mode conversion paths, while at strong fields it extends the diode regime well beyond the singlet-only critical field, with the maximal efficiency reached along the triplet-enhanced phase boundary. The diode effect thereby serves as a transport probe of a hidden triplet pairing channel and of the field-induced $s+if$ state.

</details>

**Summary.** This work extends Floquet scattering theory to analyze quantum transport in topological semimetals like nodal-ring and multi-Weyl systems under periodic driving. By solving the full boundary problem, the authors prove that while evanescent modes are required for mathematical consistency, only propagating modes dictate observable transport properties. This establishes a unified framework for understanding Floquet-induced phenomena like Fano resonances in these complex materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical treatment of boundary conditions in the presence of time-periodic driving and complex band structures (Weyl/Nodal-Ring) provides a powerful, general framework for analyzing open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a comprehensive Floquet scattering theory to analyze quantum transport in periodically driven nodal-ring and multi-Weyl semimetals.

**Main result.** It establishes that while evanescent modes are mathematically necessary for boundary matching, they do not contribute to measurable transport quantities like transmission or shot noise, which are determined entirely by propagating channels.

**Method.** The authors solve the full boundary-value problem using Floquet scattering theory, constructing the scattering matrix by matching wavefunctions and their derivatives at potential interfaces.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Nodal-Ring Semimetals (NRSs) and multi-Weyl Semimetals (mWSMs) subjected to a time-periodic driving potential. The analysis involves solving the time-dependent Schrödinger equation in the Floquet basis.

**Key observables.** Transmission, reflection, pumped shot noise, and Floquet-induced Fano resonance features.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The driving frequency ($\omega$), the potential strength ($V_0, V_1$), and the topological nature of the semimetals (NRSs, mWSMs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the zero-temperature limit for shot noise calculations and relies on the mathematical necessity of including evanescent modes for consistency in nonlinear dispersion regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the scattering geometry for a quasiparticle incident on a time-periodic potential well in a double-Weyl mWSM, dividing the space into incident, well, and transmitted regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from developing the full Floquet scattering formalism for anisotropic topological semimetals, deriving the scattering matrix by solving boundary conditions across interfaces, and finally analyzing the resulting transport coefficients and Fano resonance signatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a comprehensive Floquet scattering theory for quantum transport in periodically driven nodal-ring semimetals (NRSs) and multi-Weyl semimetals (mWSMs), extending our earlier study reported in Annalen der Physik 535, 2200460 (2023), in which evanescent modes were neglected, to a complete formalism that includes both propagating and evanescent channels. By solving the full boundary-value problem, we obtain the complete set of scattering states and show that, although evanescent modes are indispensable for satisfying the matching conditions at the potential interfaces, they carry zero net probability current and do not contribute to any observable transport quantity. We identify a previously unexplored transport regime in NRSs in which two propagating channels coexist and participate in coherent scattering, producing multi-channel quantum interference and Floquet-induced Fano resonances. The transmission, reflection, pumped shot noise, and the associated Fano resonance features are determined entirely by the propagating channels, and the resonance energies coincide with those of the corresponding quasi-bound states of the static potential well. Our results establish a unified framework for Floquet transport in anisotropic topological semimetals, and confirm that the approximation of neglecting evanescent modes in our earlier work is justified for all measurable transport properties.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the high-density behavior of interlayer excitons in MoSe2/WSe2 heterobilayers using PLE spectroscopy. By tuning the excitation energy, the researchers drive the system into a nonlinear regime, observing PL saturation and a systematic blueshift in the emission energy. This blueshift confirms the presence of a net repulsive exciton-exciton interaction, establishing PLE as a key diagnostic tool for these quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of PLE spectroscopy to map out the nonlinear response of excitons in a solid-state van der Waals heterostructure provides a powerful experimental tool for studying many-body physics in quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to probe the nonlinear, high-density interactions of dipolar interlayer excitons (IXs) within MoSe2/WSe2 heterobilayers.

**Main result.** The IX emission exhibits a systematic blueshift proportional to the increasing IX population, confirming a net repulsive exciton-exciton interaction dominated by dipole-dipole repulsion.

**Method.** Excitation-energy-dependent photoluminescence excitation (PLE) spectroscopy and power-dependent PL measurements are used to selectively vary and probe the high-density regime of the excitons.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a van der Waals heterobilayer of MoSe2/WSe2, which forms interlayer excitons due to type-II band alignment, giving rise to an intrinsic out-of-plane dipole moment.

**Key observables.** Interlayer exciton photoluminescence (PL) energy shift (blueshift), saturation of PL response, and apparent broadening of intralayer resonances in PLE spectra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (4 K vs 75 K), varying excitation power, and the density regime achieved through resonant excitation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed blueshift is attributed to repulsive exciton-exciton interaction, and the PLE measurements are sensitive enough to probe nonlinear population dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize the structure, band alignment, temperature dependence (Varshni fit), magnetic field effects (Zeeman shifts), and the dependence of IX emission energy on excitation energy and power.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical system and methods (PLE, power dependence) to access the nonlinear regime, presents evidence of nonlinear dynamics (saturation, broadening), and quantifies the interaction strength via the systematic blueshift of the IX emission.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interlayer excitons in transition-metal dichalcogenide heterobilayers possess intrinsic out-of-plane dipole moments, providing a platform for investigating exciton-exciton interactions at high densities. Here, we use excitation-energy-dependent photoluminescence excitation (PLE) spectroscopy to probe the nonlinear response of dipolar interlayer excitons in chemical vapor deposition-grown MoSe$_2$/WSe$_2$ heterobilayers. By tuning the excitation energy across intralayer exciton resonances at fixed excitation power, we selectively vary the population injected into the interlayer exciton states. Resonant excitation drives the system into a nonlinear regime, leading to saturation of the interlayer exciton photoluminescence and an apparent broadening of the intralayer $1s$ resonances in the PLE spectra. At the same time, the interlayer exciton emission exhibits a pronounced blueshift, reaching approximately 2.5 meV at 4 K and 1 meV at 75 K. The blueshift increases systematically with the interlayer exciton population and is consistent with a net repulsive exciton-exciton interaction, with contributions from dipole-dipole repulsion in the density regime investigated. Our results establish PLE as a sensitive approach for accessing the nonlinear, high-density regime of interlayer excitons and probing their interactions in van der Waals heterostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This research establishes that the speed at which antiferromagnetic insulators demagnetize is controlled by the coupling between lattice vibrations (phonons) and spin excitations (magnons). By calculating the exchange striction tensor, the authors provide a predictive, computable parameter that successfully explains the vast difference in demagnetization rates observed between $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$ and $	ext{FeBO}_3$.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, computable material parameter ($\partial J / \partial u$) that links fundamental lattice physics (phonons/displacements) directly to ultrafast magnetic dynamics, which is highly relevant for understanding energy dissipation and non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in solids.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the fundamental physical mechanism that sets the speed limit for demagnetization in antiferromagnetic insulators.

**Main result.** The demagnetization rate is governed by energy flow from the lattice into the spins, quantified by the exchange striction tensor ($\partial J / \partial u$). This mechanism explains the large difference in demagnetization speeds between $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$ and $	ext{FeBO}_3$.

**Method.** The study combines first-principles calculations (DFT+U) to compute exchange striction parameters with atomistic spin-lattice dynamics (SLD) simulations to model the time evolution of the magnetic order parameter.

**Model / system.** The system involves antiferromagnetic insulators ($	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$, $	ext{FeBO}_3$) modeled using spin operators and phonon operators. The Hamiltonian is expanded to include an interaction term driven by the dependence of the exchange coupling ($J$) on atomic displacements ($\mathbf{u}$), leading to a one-phonon-two-magnon process.

**Key observables.** Demagnetization time ($	au$) measured via time-resolved Second-Harmonic Generation (SHG) contrast; the exchange-striction tensor ($\partial J / \partial u$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of decay rates ($	au_{	ext{DM},1p1m}^{-1} / 	au_{	ext{ex},1p2m}^{-1}$); the magnitude of the exchange coupling sensitivity to displacement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on perturbative expansions in atomic displacements and assumes that the one-phonon-two-magnon (1p2m) channel dominates over the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DM)-driven 1p1m channel.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare crystal structures and magnetic paths of $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$ and $	ext{FeBO}_3$, showing the overlap between phonon dispersions and the two-magnon continuum, which dictates the coupling strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the experimental challenge (fast demagnetization) to proposing the mechanism (lattice coupling via exchange striction). It details the theoretical derivation of the coupling vertex (1p2m process) and concludes by applying this framework to compare two specific materials, quantifying the rate difference.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Antiferromagnets combine terahertz spin dynamics with insensitivity to stray fields, and how quickly their order can be manipulated sets the speed limit on device operation. Femtosecond optical pulses demagnetize antiferromagnetic insulators on timescales that span picoseconds to nanoseconds across compounds, and no material parameter is known that accounts for the spread or predicts where a new compound will fall. In a compensated antiferromagnet, no angular momentum needs to leave the spin system, so the rate is set by energy flow from the lattice into the spins. Time-resolved second-harmonic generation experiments show that Cr2O3 demagnetizes within 2 ps once the lattice is driven above the Neel temperature, two orders of magnitude faster than the structurally similar FeBO3. First-principles calculations trace the disparity to exchange striction: short Cr-Cr contacts make the exchange coupling tenfold more sensitive to atomic displacements and widen the phase space for phonon decay into magnon pairs. Spin-lattice simulations with ab initio parameters reproduce the order of magnitude of the measured ratio. The derivative of the exchange coupling with respect to the ionic displacement thus emerges as a computable parameter that predicts how fast an insulating antiferromagnet can be demagnetized. The results advance our understanding of ultrafast control in insulating antiferromagnets, and suggest a practical pathway to screen candidate materials for thermally assisted antiferromagnetic memory before synthesis.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the thermodynamics of 2D superconductors in a magnetic field, finding that the critical field for vortex formation depends on the sample size. By comparing weak and strong screening limits, the authors derive specific scaling laws for this critical field and establish a firm microscopic foundation for the weak-field ground state.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of size-dependent critical fields and the non-commutativity of limits is highly relevant to understanding finite-size effects in quantum materials, which is a key area in modern condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the weak-field thermodynamics of two-dimensional (2D) superconductors, specifically addressing the non-commutativity of the weak-field and thermodynamic limits.

**Main result.** The lower critical field required to nucleate the first vortex is size-dependent, scaling differently in weak ($\propto \mathcal{A}^{-1}\ln(\mathcal{A})$) and strong ($\propto \mathcal{A}^{-1/2}$) screening regimes.

**Method.** The analysis uses microscopic models, solving for the ground state energy of both vortex-free and single-vortex states by incorporating screening effects via coupling to 3D Maxwell equations.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D superconductor (e.g., atomically thin TMDs) modeled using a Hamiltonian involving Landau levels and attractive contact interactions. The study compares the energy density in the presence of an external magnetic field ($B$) versus the thermodynamic limit ($A 	o \infty$).

**Key observables.** Lower critical field $B_v(\mathcal{A})$, energy density ($E/A$), and the vortex orbital moment ($m_v$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System area ($\mathcal{A}$), external magnetic field ($B$), screening regimes (weak vs. strong), and the ratio of characteristic length scales ($\xi, \Lambda, R$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on treating the problem non-perturbatively using Landau quantization and making approximations in momentum space for calculating vortex self-energies.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ground state energy dependence on field and area, showing non-analytic behavior at zero field and comparing energy densities for uniform vs. single-vortex states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical difference between 2D and 3D superconductors, deriving the energy functional for vortex states, analyzing the scaling laws for the first-vortex field in different screening regimes, and concluding with the thermodynamic limit behavior.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a bulk 3D type-II superconductor, the lower critical field at which an isolated vortex becomes thermodynamically favorable is a size-independent material property. We show that the situation is different in 2D superconductors: the larger the superconductor, the weaker the field needed to create its first vortex. The lower critical field in 2D is always size-dependent. For a disk of area $\mathcal A$, the lower critical field $B_v(\mathcal A)$ scales as $\mathcal A^{-1}\ln(\mathcal A/\mathcal A_0)$ in the weak-screening regime and as $\mathcal A^{-1/2}$ in the strong-screening regime. We derive these results from an analytically tractable microscopic model that admits many-body wavefunctions for both the uniform and singly quantized vortex states in a magnetic field, and incorporate screening by coupling their long-distance 2D supercurrents to 3D Maxwell equations. These results motivate organizing the weak-field ground-state of a 2D superconductor in the $(1/\mathcal A,B)$ plane. The origin represents the zero-field thermodynamic limit and it is singular. Approaching the origin along the $B$ axis leads to an increasingly dilute vortex lattice, whereas approaching along the $1/\mathcal A$ axis yields the uniform vortex-free state. Our theory shows that every trajectory carrying fixed finite flux ultimately approaches the vortex-free state in the thermodynamic limit and provides a firm microscopic foundation for the weak-field thermodynamics of 2D superconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes spatiotemporal chaos in coupled map lattices by extending the spatial coupling range. Using Covariant Lyapunov Vectors, the authors predict that the system's dynamics exhibit a distinct transition between two regimes, with the dividing point being directly related to the system's fractal dimension. This provides a powerful, general diagnostic tool for characterizing complex, extended nonlinear systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical chaos, the use of advanced spectral tools like CLVs to characterize the structure of chaotic attractors and their scaling properties is highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the chaotic dynamics of large one-dimensional lattices of nonlinear maps as the extent of spatial coupling is increased.

**Main result.** The analysis reveals a clear decomposition of the dynamics into two regimes based on the spatial power spectrum of the CLVs, with the separating index corresponding to the system's fractal dimension.

**Method.** The study employs Covariant Lyapunov Vectors (CLVs) to theoretically predict the spectral variation of CLVs and the spectrum of Lyapunov exponents, independent of the specific nonlinear mapping function.

**Model / system.** The system is a Coupled Map Lattice (CML) defined by a 1D lattice of nonlinear maps, coupled via a spatially extended interaction term. The dynamics are governed by $\vec{u}(n+1) = A_c(\epsilon, \xi_d)\vec{f}(\vec{u}(n))$.

**Key observables.** Covariant Lyapunov Vectors (CLVs), Lyapunov exponent spectrum, spatial power spectrum of CLVs, and the fractal dimension of the dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spatial coupling extent ($\xi_d$) is the primary varying parameter, alongside fixed values for the map parameter ($r=2.8$) and coupling strength ($\epsilon=0.7$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical approach assumes the spatial coupling is symmetric and that the coupling matrix $A_c$ is sparse and banded.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the 1D lattice setup, the evolution of the correlation length ($\xi_c$) and chaotic fraction ($\phi_c$) as $\xi_d$ increases, and the spatial power spectra of the CLVs.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework using CLVs to analyze the spectral properties of the coupled system. It analyzes the spatial power spectrum, deducing a two-regime structure for the CLVs, and relates the transition index to the fractal dimension.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The chaotic dynamics of a large one-dimensional lattice of nonlinear maps with a spatial coupling of increasing extent is explored using the covariant Lyapunov vectors (CLVs). A theoretical approach is developed that predicts the spectral variation of the CLVs and the spectrum of Lyapunov exponents. The theoretical approach is independent of the details of the mapping function and requires only a description of the lattice coupling. The general structure of the spatial power spectrum of the CLVs is determined using only the eigenvectors of the coupling matrix. This yields a decomposition of the CLVs into two regimes. The first regime, corresponding to the largest Lyapunov exponents, contains highly entangled CLVs with significant contributions from large scale spatial structures and aligns with the sorted eigenvectors, which occur in pairs with wavenumbers that increase linearly with their index. The second regime contains significant mixing of length scales with increasing index. The index separating these two regimes is approximately equal to the fractal dimension of the dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work classifies coherent conductance peaks in quantum dots and quantum point contacts by analyzing their temperature scaling behavior. The authors distinguish between peaks arising from spin exchange dynamics, characteristic of Kondo physics, and those arising solely from cotunneling. This classification is crucial for understanding the fundamental spin interactions governing transport in mesoscopic quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The clear distinction between spin-exchange (Kondo) and cotunneling mechanisms in transport measurements provides a powerful experimental diagnostic tool for understanding spin interactions in nanoscale quantum conductors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper classifies coherent peaks in nanoelectronic devices based on whether their underlying spin dynamics are governed by spin exchange (Kondo physics) or purely by cotunneling of spin pairs.

**Main result.** Two distinct categories of coherent peaks are identified: one involving spin exchange (seen in QPC/even-particle QDSET ZBPs) and another involving only cotunneling (seen in odd-particle QDSET ZBPs).

**Method.** The classification relies on detailed scaling analysis of temperature-dependent differential conductance ($dI/dV$) measurements and theoretical reproduction using Green's function techniques.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes Quantum-dot single-electron transistors (QDSETs) and Quantum Point Contacts (QPCs), which model localized spins coupled to metallic leads.

**Key observables.** Differential conductance ($dI/dV$), zero-bias peaks (ZBPs), and the scaling temperature ($T_{SC}$) relative to the Kondo temperature ($T_K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), gate voltage ($V_G$), and the ratio of $T_{SC}$ to $rac{1}{2}	ext{FWHM}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed physics can be separated into distinct spin dynamics mechanisms (spin exchange vs. cotunneling) and utilizes scaling functions derived from theoretical models.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic device setups (QDSET/QPC) and compare temperature-scaled conductance data, showing different scaling behaviors for different peak types.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the physical systems, developing scaling functions for different peak types, and then using theoretical calculations (Green's functions, matrix reduction) to confirm the spin dynamics responsible for the observed conductance features.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coherent peaks appearing in the differential conductance of quantum-dot single-electron transistors (QDSETs) and quantum point contact (QPC) devices are classified into two categories according to the scaling function onto which the temperature-scaled differential-conductance maxima collapse and the underlying spin dynamics. The zero-bias peaks (ZBPs) observed in QPCs and in the triplet state of the even-particle sector of QDSETs belong to the same category, whereas the ZBP in the odd-particle sector of a QDSET belongs to a different category together with all finite-bias coherent peaks observed in QPCs and in the even-particle sector of QDSETs. The spin dynamics of the former category involve spin exchange, a hallmark of Kondo dynamics, whereas those of the latter category involve only cotunneling of an up--down spin pair. Furthermore, for the former type of ZBP, the scaling temperature coincides with one-half of the full width at half maximum (FWHM), which corresponds to the Kondo temperature. In contrast, for the latter type, the scaling temperature does not coincide with the (1/2)FWHM-derived energy scale. To support these findings, the gate-voltage-dependent differential-conductance line shapes measured in the odd-particle sector of a QDSET are theoretically reproduced. The results demonstrate that the observed ZBP is a merging of two coherent side peaks generated solely by the cotunneling of up--down spin pairs.

</details>

**Authors:** Zhipeng Zeng, Yuge Chen, Jean-Noël Fuchs, Jianxin Zhong, Rémy Mosseri

**Summary.** This work analyzes topological insulators in 1D chains created via the cut-and-project method, which imposes inversion symmetry. The authors establish a precise mathematical criterion—related to the ratio $n/\gcd(M,n)$—that dictates whether a topological transition occurs between different insulating phases. This provides a deep theoretical understanding of how lattice geometry and band filling control topological behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection between the topological invariants (polarization jumps) and simple arithmetic properties ($n/\gcd(M,n)$ parity) provides a powerful, model-independent tool for classifying topological phases in engineered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the electronic properties and topological transitions in inversion-symmetric topological insulators constructed using the cut-and-project method for binary chains.

**Main result.** Topological transitions are strictly governed by an arithmetic criterion: they occur if and only if $n/\gcd(M,n)$ is odd, with specific exceptions noted for even values.

**Method.** The analysis employs tight-binding models derived from the cut-and-project method, utilizing symmetry constraints (inversion symmetry) and topological diagnostics like the Zak phase and polarization.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional periodic chain with a binary hopping structure ($t_A, t_B$), generated by projecting a 2D lattice structure ($\mathbb{Z}^2$) via the cut-and-project method for rational slopes $\alpha=p/q$.

**Key observables.** Electric polarization ($P$), topological transition points, and the energy spectrum visualized as a "Cut-and-Project butterfly".

**Important parameters / regimes.** The hopping ratio $t_A/t_B$, the number of sites $n=p+q$, and the number of filled bands $M$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to rational slopes $\alpha = p/q$, and the model assumes spinless electrons with a single orbital per site.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the CP construction, the "CP butterfly" spectrum vs. rescaled slope, and plots showing polarization jumps confirming the arithmetic criterion.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the CP model and its inherent symmetry, establishing the Hamiltonian and spectral features, and then deriving the necessary and sufficient conditions for topological transitions based on the periodicity parameters $n$ and $M$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the electronic properties of binary tight-binding chains generated by the cut-and-project method for rational slopes $α=p/q$, leading to periodic and inversion symmetric chains with $n=p+q$ sites. The binary structure is encoded in two hopping amplitudes $t_a$ and $t_b$. For fixed $t_a \neq t_b$, the support of the energy spectrum as a function of $p/n$ gives rise to a "Cut-and-Project butterfly". We concentrate on insulators with $M$ filled bands among a total of $n$ bands and vary $t_a/t_b$. Inversion symmetry constrains the electric polarization $P$ to $0$ or $P_q/2$ modulo a polarization quantum $P_q = \gcd(M,n)/n$. A topological transition, between two insulators that differ by their quantized polarization, occurs if and only if $n/\gcd(M,n)$ is odd. When $n/\gcd(M,n)$ is even, the two insulating regimes have a vanishing polarization and no topological transition occurs, despite the gap closing at $t_a=t_b$. When $n$ is even and $M$ odd, we find an adiabatic path between $t_a>t_b$ and $t_a<t_b$ that maintains inversion symmetry and a gap.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically examines how topological valley kink states behave in multilayer graphene under disorder and a magnetic field. The key finding is that applying a perpendicular magnetic field breaks symmetry, which effectively suppresses backscattering between opposite valleys. This demonstrates that magnetic fields and disorder engineering are powerful tools for controlling topological transport in graphene nanostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The control mechanisms identified—layer-selective disorder and magnetic fields—provide concrete, tunable knobs for manipulating topological edge state transport, which is relevant for designing robust quantum interconnects or novel electronic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum transport of Topological Valley Kink States (VKSs) in rhombohedral multilayer graphene (RMG) when subjected to Anderson disorder and a perpendicular magnetic field.

**Main result.** The perpendicular magnetic field breaks layer symmetry, suppressing intervalley backscattering and significantly enhancing the transmission of VKSs through disordered regions. Furthermore, the transport is highly sensitive to the layer-selective distribution of disorder.

**Method.** The analysis employs numerical Landauer-Büttiker conductance calculations based on a tight-binding model Hamiltonian, supplemented by low-energy continuum descriptions for topological invariants.

**Model / system.** The system is ABC-stacked rhombohedral multilayer graphene (RMG) modeled by a tight-binding Hamiltonian incorporating nearest-neighbor hopping, layer-dependent electrostatic potentials, and disorder terms. The perpendicular magnetic field is included via the Peierls substitution.

**Key observables.** Conductance ($\langle G angle$), wave function localization, intervalley scattering suppression, and the valley Chern number ($C_{\xi}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Perpendicular magnetic field strength ($\mathbf{B}_{\perp}$), disorder strength/distribution, and the number of graphene layers ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an armchair edge termination for the ribbon geometry and utilizes low-energy continuum descriptions near the Dirac points.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic transport device, layer-polarized wave function distributions, band structures showing VKSs, and conductance measurements comparing results with and without a magnetic field.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model for VKSs in RMG, then analyzes the transport behavior in the absence of a magnetic field (showing layer polarization), and finally details how the perpendicular magnetic field breaks symmetry to enhance transport by suppressing intervalley scattering.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological valley kink states (VKSs), which are quantum valley Hall states emerging at the interfaces between adjacent domains with opposite valley Chern numbers, have attracted considerable interest in graphene-based systems. In this work, we investigate the quantum transport of VKSs in ABC-stacked rhombohedral multilayer graphene in the presence of Anderson disorder and a perpendicular magnetic field. Two prominent transport characteristics are revealed. First, in the absence of a magnetic field, the kink states exhibit strong layer polarization, with their wave functions predominantly localized and equally distributed on the outermost top and bottom layers. As a result, their transport properties are highly sensitive to the layer-selective disorder distribution. Second, under a perpendicular magnetic field, the layer-symmetric spatial distribution of VKSs is broken, leading to a significant reduction in the wave-function overlap between counter-propagating VKSs from opposite valleys. Consequently, intervalley scattering is suppressed, and the transmission of VKSs through disordered regions is substantially enhanced. Our results provide new insights into multichannel topological valley transport in rhombohedral multilayer graphene and demonstrates disorder-engineering and magnetic fields as effective approaches for manipulating the propagation of VKSs.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes a honeycomb lattice model to show how anisotropic hopping can generate narrow-sense Type-III Dirac cones. Crucially, the model also predicts additional flat energy lines at the critical point, which leads to a pronounced enhancement of the electronic density of states and specific heat compared to ideal Dirac cone predictions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that geometric features (flat lines) can dramatically enhance thermodynamic responses (specific heat) near a critical point is a general concept relevant to understanding quantum criticality in various condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the realization of narrow-sense Type-III Dirac cones and the emergence of additional flat lines in the band structure of a honeycomb lattice.

**Main result.** The model demonstrates that anisotropic next-nearest-neighbor hoppings can induce narrow-sense Type-III Dirac cones, and the co-existence of these cones with additional flat lines significantly enhances the density of states and electronic specific heat.

**Method.** The analysis employs a tight-binding model approach, calculating the energy dispersion, density of states (DOS), and electronic specific heat ($C$) by tuning the hopping anisotropy ratio.

**Model / system.** The system is a single-orbital honeycomb lattice described by a tight-binding Hamiltonian incorporating nearest-neighbor and anisotropic next-nearest-neighbor hopping terms. The electronic properties are analyzed across Type-I, narrow-sense Type-III, and Type-II Dirac cone regimes.

**Key observables.** Dirac cone type (I, III, II), Density of States (DOS), Electronic Specific Heat ($C$), and the existence/energy of additional flat lines.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of anisotropic next-nearest-neighbor hopping to nearest-neighbor hopping ($t'/t$), particularly around the critical point $t'/t = 0.5$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a single-orbital tight-binding model approximation for the electronic structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy bands for different cone types, the dependence of the DOS and specific heat on the anisotropy ratio, and the local character of energy extrema (saddle points) at high-symmetry points.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian, first establishing the realization of Type-III cones, then detailing the appearance of additional flat lines at the critical point, and finally quantifying the resulting enhancement in thermodynamic properties like the specific heat.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Critically tilted Dirac cones have attracted considerable attention because of their unconventional electronic properties in two-dimensional massless Dirac-fermion systems. Among them, the narrow-sense type-III Dirac cone is characterized by a flat dispersion along the direction connecting the two Dirac points.   Using a tight-binding model on a single-orbital honeycomb lattice, we demonstrate that narrow-sense type-III Dirac cones can be realized by tuning anisotropic next-nearest-neighbor hoppings. Type-I and type-II Dirac cones emerge on either side of the critical point, enabling a systematic investigation of the electronic properties across the type-I, narrow-sense type-III, and type-II regimes.   We further find that the present model exhibits additional flat lines in momentum space whose energy coincides with the Dirac-point energy at the narrow-sense type-III critical point. This band structure produces a pronounced enhancement of the density of states at the Fermi energy and, consequently, a substantial enhancement of the electronic specific heat compared with that expected for an ideal narrow-sense type-III Dirac cone. Our results show that the honeycomb lattice with anisotropic next-nearest-neighbor hoppings provides a simple platform for exploring unconventional thermodynamic properties arising from the energy coincidence of narrow-sense type-III Dirac cones and additional flat lines at the Fermi energy.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the anti-higher-order Weyl semimetal (anti-HOWSM), a novel topological phase characterized by the systematic suppression of boundary states, leading to an 'anti-hinge' response. Using a minimal two-band model, the authors demonstrate that this phase is topologically distinct from conventional higher-order semimetals by analyzing boundary state localization via Berry curvature and the Band Inversion Surface.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends the understanding of topological boundary physics beyond standard surface or hinge states, proposing a mechanism for engineered directional transport blocking, which is relevant for designing novel quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper introduces and characterizes the anti-higher-order Weyl semimetal (anti-HOWSM) phase, a topological state where the conventional boundary hierarchy is reversed compared to standard higher-order topological insulators.

**Main result.** The anti-HOWSM realizes an anti-hinge response, meaning the bulk topology forces boundary states to selectively vanish at specific crystalline orientations, establishing a dual counterpart to conventional higher-order topology.

**Method.** The authors use a minimal two-band model Hamiltonian and employ techniques like calculating Berry curvature, slice Chern numbers, and analyzing the Band Inversion Surface (BIS) winding charge to diagnose the topological phase.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a minimal two-band Hamiltonian $H(\mathbf{k}) = d(\mathbf{k}) \cdot \sigma$, which is analyzed in the context of 3D Weyl semimetals, often simulated on nanoribbons or nanoflakes.

**Key observables.** Anti-hinge states, anti-Fermi arcs, quantized jump of the slice Chern number, and the BIS enclosing multiple high-symmetry points with opposite topological charges.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The anisotropy parameter $\lambda$, the momentum coordinates ($\mathbf{k}$), and the topological charges associated with the BIS.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the comparison between conventional HOWSM, the anti-HOWSM, and the use of BIS winding charge alongside slice Chern numbers for complete characterization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy spectra for nanoribbons comparing QAHI (chiral states) to anti-HOTI (anti-corner states), and phase diagrams showing the transition between trivial, QAHI, and anti-HOTI regions based on system parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the boundary physics of conventional higher-order Weyl semimetals (HOWSM) with the proposed anti-HOWSM, using Hamiltonian analysis, topological invariant calculations (Chern numbers, BIS), and numerical simulations on finite geometries to demonstrate the anti-hinge response.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Higher-order topology extends the bulk-boundary correspondence by enabling corner or hinge localized states. Here we identify an anti-higher-order Weyl semimetal, a three dimensional topological phase in which the conventional boundary hierarchy is reversed. Unlike conventional higher-order Weyl semimetals where bulk topology enforces hinge Fermi arcs, this phase hosts anti-hinge states, meaning the bulk topology forces states to vanish at specific hinge orientations. Using a minimal two-band model, we show how Weyl points separate the Brillouin zone into quantum anomalous Hall and anti-higher-order topological insulating regions, with the latter characterized by a band-inversion surface enclosing two distinct high-symmetry points carrying opposite topological charges. Analytical solutions for Weyl points, Berry curvature monopoles, and slice Chern numbers are derived, with numerical simulations confirming the resulting anti-hinge behavior. Our work establishes anti-higher-order topology as a dual counterpart to conventional higher-order phenomena, further extending the exploration of topological phases.

</details>
