### [Comment on "Distinct Behaviors of Inner and Outer CuO$_2$ Planes in Quadruple-Layer Cuprate (Cu,C)Ba$_2$Ca$_3$Cu$_4$O$_{11 + δ}$"](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00467v1)

**Authors:** Jeffery L. Tallon  
**Type:** both · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.00467v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.00467_figures/2609.00467_page1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Low-resolution page preview, page 1</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the two-gap superconductivity in a quadruple-layer cuprate, suggesting that the inner and outer $	ext{CuO}_2$ planes act as largely decoupled superconductors. The key finding is that the critical temperatures for both gaps correlate strongly with the local structural parameter, the Bond-Valence Sum ($V^+$), implying that pairing is governed by short-range, local electronic structure rather than bulk properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of two distinct, locally governed superconducting gaps in a single material provides a concrete example of emergent, multi-component superconductivity, which has parallels in understanding complex, multi-band correlated superconductors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the distinct superconducting pairing mechanisms and critical temperatures in the quadruple-layer cuprate $	ext{(Cu,C)}	ext{Ba}_2	ext{Ca}_3	ext{Cu}_4	ext{O}_{11+\delta}$ by analyzing the separate superconducting gaps in the inner and outer $	ext{CuO}_2$ planes.

**Main result.** The observed two-gap superconductivity suggests two largely decoupled superconducting planes, each governed by its local crystal structure, as evidenced by the consistency of $T_{c2}$ with the Bond-Valence Sum parameter ($V^+$) calculated locally.

**Method.** The analysis combines photoemission spectroscopy results (revealing two gaps) with structural calculations using the Bond-Valence Sum ($V^+$) parameter derived from crystallographic bond lengths.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the quadruple-layer cuprate $	ext{(Cu,C)}	ext{Ba}_2	ext{Ca}_3	ext{Cu}_4	ext{O}_{11+\delta}$, which exhibits superconductivity in two distinct $	ext{CuO}_2$ planes (Inner and Outer). The underlying physics is modeled by analyzing the superconducting gaps and their dependence on local structural parameters.

**Key observables.** Two superconducting gaps ($	ext{IP}$ and $	ext{OP}$), bulk critical temperature ($T_c$), second gap-opening temperature ($T_{c2}$), and the Bond-Valence Sum parameter ($V^+$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping levels ($p$): $	ext{IP}$ ($eta$-band) is heavily underdoped ($p \approx 0.07$); $	ext{OP}$ ($\alpha_2$ band) is strongly overdoped ($p \approx 0.25$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the superconducting pairing temperatures are determined by the local nearest-neighbor structure, quantified by the $V^+$ parameter, suggesting weak coupling between the planes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 plots the maximum critical temperature ($T_{c,	ext{max}}$) against the Bond-Valence Sum parameter ($V^+$) for various cuprates, including specific data points for $T_c$ and $T_{c2}$ versus $V^+(	ext{IP})$ and $V^+(	ext{OP})$ for $	ext{CuC-1234}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first presents the observation of two distinct superconducting gaps in $	ext{CuC-1234}$. It then correlates the measured $T_c$ and $T_{c2}$ values with the structural Bond-Valence Sum parameter ($V^+$). Finally, it discusses the implications of this correlation, suggesting local structural control over pairing temperatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a recent Letter, Sun et al. report photoemission spectroscopy measurements on the four-layer cuprate (Cu,C)Ba2Ca3Cu4O11 (CuC-1234) in which they resolve two superconducting gaps associated with the inner (IP) and outer (OP) CuO2 planes. CuC-1234 has a Tc as 117 K in a sample previously structurally characterized using neutron powder diffraction. From the Luttinger sum, the beta-band of the IP was found to be heavily underdoped (p~0.07) while the OP alpha2 bonding band was strongly overdoped (p~0.25). Each revealed a gap with very different momentum and temperature dependences. A large gap on the beta band was found to close at the bulk Tc, while a smaller gap on the alpha2 band closed at 70 K. Such two-gap behavior would indicate weak-coupling between the IP and OP, resulting in a second gap-opening temperature, Tc2, and implying a two-step development of the superfluid density on cooling, first on the IPs then on the OPs. Here, we point out that the observed values of Tc and Tc2 are fully consistent with a long-standing correlation of Tc with a bond-valence sum parameter, V+, calculated from the crystallographic bond lengths. This suggests that Tc2 in the OP is entirely consistent with the local structure of the OP where the value of V+(OP) is reduced by the presence of the apical oxygens and Tc2 reduced accordingly. It is as though two largely decoupled superconductors are present in the single 1234 compound and their individual pairing temperatures are determined by their local nearest-neighbour structure, reflecting the very short-range physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the synthesis and characterization of the trilayer nickelate $	ext{Sm}_4	ext{Ni}_3	ext{O}_{10-\delta}$, expanding the study of this superconducting material class. While the material shows a density wave transition, superconductivity is not observed even under extreme pressure. The authors argue that significant structural distortions, particularly in the $	ext{Ni-O-Ni}$ bond angle, are the primary physical mechanism suppressing superconductivity in this system.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the physics of strongly correlated electron systems, particularly high-Tc superconductivity, by linking subtle structural changes (bond angles, distortions) to emergent electronic states (DW, superconductivity), which is a major theme in condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to expand the understanding of high-temperature superconductivity in the trilayer Ruddlesden-Popper (RP) nickelate family, specifically by synthesizing and characterizing the Sm$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10-\delta}$ compound.

**Main result.** The synthesized $	ext{Sm}_4	ext{Ni}_3	ext{O}_{10-\delta}$ exhibits a density wave (DW) transition at ambient pressure, but superconductivity was not observed even under high pressure (up to 80 GPa), suggesting structural distortions are key barriers.

**Method.** The research combines experimental techniques, including High-Pressure/High-Temperature (HPHT) synthesis, transport/magnetization measurements, and structural characterization (XRD), with theoretical calculations using Density Functional Theory (DFT).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the trilayer Ruddlesden-Popper nickelate $	ext{Sm}_4	ext{Ni}_3	ext{O}_{10-\delta}$. The electronic behavior is governed by the correlated Ni $3d$ orbitals, and the structural analysis focuses on the $	ext{Ni-O-Ni}$ bond angle and octahedral distortions.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), Density Wave (DW) transition temperature ($\sim 180 	ext{ K}$), $	ext{Ni-O-Ni}$ bond angle, and electrical/magnetic transport anomalies under pressure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High pressure (up to 80 GPa), rare-earth element size (Sm vs La/Pr), and the degree of octahedral distortion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the deviation of the $	ext{Ni-O-Ni}$ bond angle from $180^\circ$ is the key structural factor inhibiting superconductivity in this series.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate structural analysis (crystal structure, bond angle variation), electrical transport (resistance anomalies at 180 K), and magnetization measurements showing magnetic transitions corresponding to the electronic changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of literature review (contextualizing RP nickelates), synthesis and structural characterization of $	ext{Sm}_4	ext{Ni}_3	ext{O}_{10-\delta}$, detailed measurements (transport, magnetism, high pressure), and theoretical interpretation linking structural distortions to electronic properties.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The discovery of high-temperature superconductivity in Ruddlesden-Popper (RP) nickelates has attracted significant attention. Bulk superconductivity emerges under pressure in trilayer nickelates La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10-δ}$ (T$_c$ $\approx$ 30 K) and Pr$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10-δ}$ (T$_c$ $\approx$ 40.5 K), where the reduced ionic radius of Pr$^{3+}$ may generate internal chemical pressure and enhance T$_c$. However, synthesizing trilayer RP phases with smaller rare-earth elements (Ln) is extremely challenging. So far, only the La, Pr, and Nd analogues have been synthesized with stable phases in the single rare-earth form. Here we report the first successful high-pressure and high-temperature (HPHT) synthesis of samarium-based compound Sm$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10-δ}$. Magnetization and transport measurements consistently confirm a density wave (DW) transition at ~180 K at ambient pressure. Through a careful fitting to the structural data of Sm$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10-δ}$, it is found that the bond angle of (Ni-O-Ni) associating with the interlayer apical oxygen is much smaller than 180$^{\circ}$, which was assumed to be the key factor for the occurrence of superconductivity. By applying pressures up to 80 GPa, despite partial suppression of insulating behavior and the DW order, but superconductivity is not observed in our present study. Density functional theory calculations suggest that the 3d$_{z^2}$ and 3d$_{x^2-y^2}$ are separated from other t$_{2g}$ orbitals and make a primary contribution to the Fermi surface. The newly synthesized trilayer nickelate Sm$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10-δ}$ offers a unique platform for probing the fundamental physics of RP nickelates.

</details>

**Summary.** This research successfully synthesizes a highly symmetric, tetragonal nickelate phase ($	ext{La}_2	ext{SrNi}_2	ext{O}_{7-\delta}$) at ambient pressure. While the material is metallic, the authors argue that the excess hole doping shifts the electronic structure away from the optimal half-filled state, thereby suppressing the interlayer magnetic coupling required for superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the fundamental mechanism of high-Tc superconductivity in nickelates, which is a canonical problem in strongly correlated electron physics, providing crucial insights into orbital physics and doping effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central question is determining the necessary conditions—structural, electronic, or chemical—to achieve high-temperature superconductivity in bulk Ruddlesden-Popper nickelates at ambient pressure.

**Main result.** The authors report synthesizing a tetragonal $	ext{La}_2	ext{SrNi}_2	ext{O}_{7-\delta}$ phase, but attribute the absence of superconductivity to a significant deviation from the half-filling state of the $	ext{Ni}-3d_{z^2}$ band, which weakens the necessary interlayer antiferromagnetic coupling.

**Method.** The study combines high-pressure/high-temperature synthesis, structural characterization (XRD, STEM), transport measurements, and theoretical calculations (DFT) to map the phase diagram and electronic structure.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the heavily Sr-doped $	ext{La}_2	ext{SrNi}_2	ext{O}_{7-\delta}$ Ruddlesden-Popper nickelate, which is studied across varying oxygen content, pressure, and doping levels.

**Key observables.** Tetragonal crystal structure ($	ext{I}4/	ext{mmm}$), metallic resistance behavior, large $\gamma$ pocket on the Fermi surface, and the suppression of density-wave features.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ambient pressure vs. high pressure (up to 51.9 GPa); Sr doping level; $	ext{Ni}-	ext{O}-	ext{Ni}$ bond angle ($180^\circ$ ideal); filling of the $	ext{Ni}-3d_{z^2}$ orbital.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The superconducting pairing mechanism is critically dependent on maintaining the half-filling state of the $	ext{Ni}-3d_{z^2}$ orbital, which mediates interlayer antiferromagnetic interaction.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the crystal structure and lattice parameters (XRD/STEM), show the temperature and pressure dependence of resistance and magnetization, and present DFT calculations illustrating the band structure and Fermi surface evolution under pressure.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the structural synthesis of the tetragonal phase, then characterizes its electronic and transport properties, and finally uses DFT calculations to explain the failure to achieve superconductivity by analyzing the orbital filling relative to the half-filled state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-temperature superconductivity has been found in bilayer Ruddlesden-Popper (RP) nickelates in bulk samples under high pressure, or in thin films via compressive strain. In the superconducting state, a tetragonal structure with a straight Ni-O-Ni bond along c-axis has been commonly observed, together with the suppression or diminishing of the density-wave orders. Therefore, it remains an open question whether these factors are sufficient for achieving superconductivity at ambient pressure. Here we report the first successful synthesis of heavily Sr-doped La$_{2}$SrNi$_{2}$O$_{7-δ}$ under high-pressure and high-temperature (HPHT) conditions with a flux method. X-ray diffraction and scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM) confirm that the material adopts a tetragonal (I4/mmm) structure with an 180$^{\circ}$ Ni-O-Ni bond angle along c-axis. Resistance measurements reveal metallic behavior with a low-temperature upturn and no density-wave features are observed. However, neither pressure nor oxygen variation induces superconductivity. Density functional theory calculations indicate that the holes introduced by Sr doping are predominantly doped into the Ni-3d$_{z^2}$ orbital, leading to the appearance of a very large $γ$ pocket on the Fermi surface at ambient pressure and significantly reducing the occupation of the Ni-3d$_{z^2 }$ orbital. Combining the experimental observations with theoretical calculations, we attribute the absence of superconductivity to the serious deviation from the half-filling state of the Ni-3d$_{z^2 }$ band, which is crucial for the interlayer antiferromagnetic interaction and thus for pairing. Our work unravels important issues for achieving superconductivity in bilayer nickelate system.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the superconducting state in overdoped cuprates by measuring the electronic specific heat. The authors find that the superconducting anomaly persists sharply deep into the overdoped regime. They conclude that the observed drop in $T_c$ is due to a gradual reduction in the intrinsic pairing strength, rather than being dominated by increasing structural disorder.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cuprates, the quantitative analysis of pairing strength suppression and the interplay between intrinsic pairing and disorder effects in a strongly correlated superconductor provides valuable lessons for understanding pairing mechanisms in other correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The physical origin of the suppression of superconductivity with hole doping in overdoped cuprates remains unclear.

**Main result.** The superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$) decrease is consistent with a smoothly decreasing pairing strength rather than being predominantly driven by increasing disorder.

**Method.** The study uses electronic specific heat measurements on single crystals and models the anomaly using a weak-coupling BCS-like framework incorporating disorder effects.

**Model / system.** The system is far-overdoped $	ext{Tl}_2	ext{Ba}_2	ext{CuO}_6$ single crystals. The analysis employs BCS theory adapted for d-wave pairing and incorporates known cation disorder to model the superconducting state.

**Key observables.** The superconducting specific heat jump ratio, $\Delta\gamma(T_c)/\gamma_n$, and the intrinsic pairing strength parameter, $\Delta\gamma_{int}/\gamma_n$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$) ranges from 14 to 25 K in the studied overdoped regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis isolates the electronic specific heat contribution by fitting the normal-state background with a polynomial, and the modeling relies on a weak-coupling BCS framework.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the electronic specific heat anomaly ($\Delta C/T$) from single crystals versus polycrystalline samples, showing the extracted intrinsic gap parameter ($\Delta\gamma_{int}$) is relatively independent of doping.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the mystery of doping-induced superconductivity suppression, presents single-crystal specific heat measurements showing sharp anomalies, models these anomalies using BCS theory incorporating disorder, and concludes that pairing strength variation is the dominant factor.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The physical origin of the suppression of superconductivity with hole doping in overdoped cuprates remains unclear. We measure the electronic specific heat of microgram-scale Tl$_2$Ba$_2$CuO$_6$ crystals and find sharp superconducting anomalies persisting far into the overdoped regime, with $Δγ(T_{\rm c})/γ_n\approx0.6$ for $T_{\rm c}=14$-$25$K. A weak-coupling BCS-like framework incorporating the known Fermi surface and cation disorder quantitatively reproduces the observed anomaly and its weak doping dependence. We conclude that the observed $T_{\rm c}(p)$ cannot be driven predominantly by increasing disorder and is instead consistent with a smoothly decreasing pairing strength.

</details>
